Speak, and you will be heard; ask, and you will be given; intercede, and your intercession will be accepted.” I will say, “My Lord, my community!” He will then set a limit for me, and I will admit them to Paradise.’”

Thus the Messiah—may Allah’s blessings and peace be upon him—directed them to Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and spoke of the perfection of his servitude to Allah and the completeness of Allah’s forgiveness of him. For there is no bond between created beings and the Creator other than pure servitude and dependence on the servant’s part

---

## Page 7614

and pure generosity and kindness on the part of the Lord, Mighty and Majestic.

It is established in the two Sahīh collections that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“None of you will enter Paradise by virtue of his deeds.” They asked, “Not even you, O Messenger of Allah?” He replied, “Not even I, unless Allah envelops me in His mercy and grace.”

It is also established in an authentic report that he used to say:

“O people, repent to your Lord! By Him in whose hand is my soul, I seek Allah’s forgiveness and repent to Him more than seventy times a day.”

It is also established in an authentic report that he said:

“A veil comes over my heart, and I seek Allah’s forgiveness a hundred times a day.”

Thus, through the perfection of his servitude to Allah, his love for Him and dependence upon Him, and his repentance and seeking forgiveness, he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, became the best of creation in Allah’s sight. For all goodness comes from Allah, and no created being possesses anything of its own. Rather, the creature is dependent in every respect, while Allah has no need of it in any respect and bestows His kindness upon it in every respect. The more a servant grows in humility and servitude, the nearer he draws to Allah and the higher he rises in rank. His repentance and seeking forgiveness are part of this.

In a hadith, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Every child of Adam sins, and the best of those who sin are those who continually repent.”

Reported by Ibn Mājah and al-Tirmidhī.

---

## Page 7615

Surah Jonah

Shaykh al-Islām, may Allah have mercy on him, said:

Section

His words:

“He is the One who made the sun a radiance and the moon a light, and determined phases for it, so that you may know the number of years and the reckoning”; 

and His words:

“And He made the night for rest, and the sun and the moon for reckoning”;

and His words:

“The sun and the moon follow precise calculations”;

and His words:

“And the moon—We have determined phases for it, until it returns like an old date-palm stalk”;

and His words:

“They ask you about the new moons. Say: They are markers of time for people and for Hajj”—

all provide evidence for using the means of timekeeping mentioned in them to reckon years and make calculations.

If His words, “so that you may know the number of years and the reckoning,” are connected to His words, “and determined phases for it,” then the statement applies exclusively to the moon. If, however, they refer back to the beginning of the passage, they apply to both the sun and the moon.

The first interpretation is supported by His words concerning the new moons, which accord with it. Moreover, the sun’s being a radiance and the moon’s being a light do not in themselves enable one to know the number of years and the reckoning, unlike the determination of the moon’s phases, for it is this that

---

## Page 7616

enables one to know the number of years and the reckoning. He does not mention the sun’s passage through the zodiacal signs.

This is supported by His words:

“Indeed, the number of months with Allah is twelve months in the Book of Allah”—to the end of the verse.

This explicitly establishes that the year is lunar. His words, “Hajj is during well-known months,” also support this. However, the other interpretation is supported by His words:

“We made the night and the day two signs. We effaced the sign of the night and made the sign of the day illuminating, so that you may seek bounty from your Lord and know the number of years and the reckoning.”

There is a reason for this—and Allah knows best—which reveals the wisdom behind what the Book and the revealed law prescribe concerning reckoning by lunar months and years rather than solar ones: months and years, as defined by various nations, are either determined by counting or by a natural cycle. A lunar month follows a natural cycle, while its year is determined by counting. A solar month, by contrast, is determined by counting, while its year follows a natural cycle.

The wisdom in making our month lunar is therefore clear, since it follows a natural cycle. Its beginning is tied to the crescent rather than the conjunction because the crescent is precisely ascertainable through the senses, without error or any need for calculation. The conjunction, by contrast, is imperceptible and requires calculation, as does a solar month if its limits are to be determined. As for the solar year, although it follows a natural cycle, it is of the same kind as

---

## Page 7617

the conjunction: it is not directly perceptible to the senses, but requires calculation of the sun’s movement through its stations. What the senses perceive is only an approximation. The end of winter and the arrival of the season the Arabs call summer and others call spring are plainly perceptible. By contrast, the sun’s alignment with a particular part of the celestial sphere called such-and-such a zodiacal sign, or with either of the points known as the Head and the Tail, requires calculation.

Since there are twelve zodiacal signs, when the lunar month has recurred twelve times, all of them have been traversed, thus constituting a full year. To this year are tied the rulings of our religion concerning periods fixed by revealed law or by stipulation. These include periods prescribed directly by revealed law, as with fasting and Hajj; those occasioned by a person’s own action, such as the waiting period, the period of sworn abstinence from marital relations, and fasting in expiation or fulfilment of a vow; and those fixed by stipulation, such as the term of a debt, the period for exercising a contractual option, oaths, and so forth.

---

## Page 7618

He also said:

This is an explanation of verses that have proved difficult to understand, so much so that a number of Quran commentaries offer only erroneous explanations of them. Among these is His statement:

“And what do those who invoke partners besides Allah follow?”

Some have supposed that mā (“what”) is a negative particle, but this is mistaken. Rather, it introduces a question, for they do invoke partners alongside Him, as He has stated in several places. Thus, these partners are described in the Quran as being invoked because they are followed, and only leaders are followed. Hence His statement:

“They follow nothing but conjecture.”

Had He intended negation, He would have said: “They follow none but those who are not partners.” Rather, He explained that the polytheist possesses no knowledge: he has nothing but conjecture and eager desire, as in His statement:

“May the liars be destroyed!”

---

## Page 7619

Surah Hūd

He also said:

Section

Concerning the words of Allah Most High:

“Is one who stands upon clear evidence from his Lord and is followed by a witness from Him”

This encompasses everyone who stands upon clear evidence from his Lord and is followed by a witness from Him. The clear evidence is beneficial knowledge, and the witness that follows it is righteous action. This includes the Messenger and all who follow him until the Day of Resurrection. The Messenger stands upon clear evidence from his Lord, and his followers likewise stand upon clear evidence from his Lord.

Concerning the Messenger, Allah said:

“Say: Indeed, I stand upon clear evidence from my Lord.”

Concerning the believers, He said:

“Is one who stands upon clear evidence from his Lord like those whose evil deeds have been made appealing to them and who follow their own desires?”

He mentioned this after describing the two groups at the beginning of the surah, saying:

“Those who disbelieve and turn others away from the path of Allah—He has rendered their deeds worthless.”

“But those who believe, do righteous deeds, and believe in what has been sent down to Muhammad—and it is the truth from their Lord—He has absolved them of their misdeeds and set their affairs right.”

“That is because those who disbelieve follow falsehood, while those who believe follow the truth from their Lord.”

These verses continue up to His words:

“Is one who stands upon clear evidence from his Lord”

---

## Page 7620

And Abū al-Dardā’ said:

“No community perishes until its people follow their desires and abandon the clear proofs and guidance brought to them by their prophets.”

Allah Most High said:

“Say, ‘This is my way: I call to Allah with clear insight—I and those who follow me.’”

Thus, whoever follows him calls to Allah with clear insight, and this insight is the clear evidence.

He also said:

“Is one who was dead, then We brought him to life and gave him a light by which to walk among people…”

The light by which he walks among people is the clear evidence and insight. He also said:

“Allah is the Light of the heavens and the earth…”

Ubayy ibn Kaʿb and others said that this is a likeness of the believer’s light. It is His light in the heart of His believing servant, arising from beneficial knowledge and righteous action. This is clear evidence from his Lord.

He also said:

“Is one whose heart Allah has opened to Islam, so that he stands upon light from his Lord”

This light upon which he stands, and the opening of his heart to Islam, constitute the clear evidence from his Lord. It is the guidance mentioned in His words:

“Those stand upon guidance from their Lord.”

Here He used the preposition denoting being above something, because the heart finds neither stability nor firmness unless it knows the truth with certainty. Knowledge and faith then become a dye that colours it, as He said:

“The colouring of Allah—and whose colouring is better than Allah’s?”

It also becomes a firm station for the heart, as He said:

“Say, ‘O my people, act according to your station; I too am acting, and you will come to know.’”

The words makān (place) and makānah (station) may refer to something upon which a thing rests, even if it does not surround it—a roof, for example—or they may refer to something that surrounds it. Thus, since the rightly guided stand upon guidance, light, clear evidence, and insight from their Lord, this has become

---

## Page 7621

a firm station for them upon which they have settled, and it may also surround them—unlike those of whom He said:

“And among people is one who worships Allah on the edge: if good befalls him, he is reassured by it, but if a trial befalls him, he turns back on his face.”

Such a person is neither steadfast nor firmly settled nor reassured. Rather, he is like someone standing on the edge of a valley—that is, its side. He may feel reassured when good befalls him, yet he may also fall face-first into the valley. Likewise, He distinguished between one who

“founded his building upon fear of Allah and His good pleasure”

and

“one who founded his building on the edge of a crumbling bank, which collapsed with him into the Fire of Hell.”

The same applies to those who were on the brink of a pit of fire, from which He rescued them. There are many passages that illustrate this.

It has thus become clear that the Messenger and those who follow him stand upon clear evidence from their Lord, insight, guidance, and light. This consists of the faith in their hearts, knowledge, and righteous action. Then He said:

“And a witness from Him follows him.”

The pronoun in “from Him” refers to Allah Most High. That is, a witness from Allah follows this person who stands upon clear evidence from his Lord. The witness is from Allah, just as the aforementioned clear evidence upon which he stands is also from Allah.

As for the view that “the witness” comes from the person mentioned himself, interpreting it as his tongue or as ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib, this is weak. The fact that a person’s witness comes from him does not entail that the witness is truthful, for it is like the testimony of

---

## Page 7622

a person on his own behalf—unlike when the witness is from Allah, for then Allah Himself is the witness. This resembles the claim that His words:

“Say, ‘Allah is sufficient as a witness between me and you, as is whoever has knowledge of the Scripture.’”

refer to ‘Alī. This is a weak interpretation, because the testimony of a relative who has followed his religion and received guidance only through him does not constitute proof of his truthfulness or an argument against disbelief. By contrast, the testimony of those who possess knowledge of the earlier Scripture is proof and mercy, as He says in this surah:

“And before it was the Scripture of Moses, a guide and a mercy.”

He also says:

“And a witness from the Children of Israel testified to its like.”

And He says:

“So if you are in doubt about what We have revealed to you, ask those who have been reading the Scripture before you.”

And so the verse continues. He also says:

“Those to whom We have given the Scripture know that it has been sent down from your Lord in truth.”

This witness from Allah is the Quran.

As for those who say that it is Gabriel, Gabriel said nothing of his own accord; rather, he conveyed the Quran from Allah. Gabriel bears witness that the Quran was sent down from Allah and that it is true, as He says:

“But Allah bears witness to what He has sent down to you: He has sent it down with His knowledge, and the angels bear witness. And sufficient is Allah as a witness.”

The one who says that it is Gabriel explains “follows him” as meaning “recites it,” as in His words:

“So when We have recited it, follow its recitation.”

That is, when Gabriel recites it, follow what he recites. He also says:

“He was taught by one mighty in power.”

Those who say that the witness is his tongue take the aforementioned pronoun to refer to the Quran, although it has not been mentioned, because they understand the clear evidence to be the Quran. Yet if the clear evidence were the Quran

---

## Page 7623

there would have been no need for that. Moreover, He has said, “upon clear evidence from his Lord,” thus already stating that the Quran is from Allah. It is also known that Gabriel brought it down to Muhammad, and that both conveyed and recited it. Thus, saying that Gabriel or Muhammad “and recites it” amounts to pointless repetition. This is why nothing of this kind is mentioned in the Quran.

Furthermore, we find no parallel in the Quran for someone being “upon the Quran,” for the Quran is Allah’s speech, and one is not said to be “upon” it. If what is meant is believing in the Quran and acting upon it, then this is precisely what we have explained: the clear evidence is faith in what the Messenger brought—namely, his declaration that he is Allah’s Messenger and that Allah sent the Quran down to him. When this surah, which is Meccan, was revealed, only part of the Quran had been revealed before it. What people were commanded to do at that time was to believe in what had been revealed of it. Whoever believed in that at the time and died upon that faith was among the people of Paradise.

Furthermore, calling Gabriel a witness has no parallel in the Quran. Likewise, calling the Messenger’s tongue a witness, or calling ‘Alī a witness, has no parallel in the Book and the Sunnah. This is unlike Allah’s testimony: Allah has spoken in several places of His testimony in support of His Messenger, and He has called what He revealed a testimony from Him, saying:

“And who is more unjust than one who conceals a testimony he has from Allah?”

This indicates that Allah’s speech, which He sent down and in which He conveyed what He conveyed, is a testimony from Him.

---

## Page 7624

And He, glorified be He, judges, bears witness, gives rulings, relates accounts, gives glad tidings, and guides through His speech. He also describes His speech as judging, giving rulings, relating accounts, guiding, giving glad tidings, and warning, as He says:

“Say, ‘Allah gives you a ruling concerning them.’”

“Say, ‘Allah gives you a ruling concerning collateral inheritance.’”

And He says:

“Indeed, this Quran relates to the Children of Israel most of that over which they disagree.”

And He says:

“We relate to you the best of stories.”

And He says:

“Say, ‘Indeed, I stand upon clear evidence from my Lord, yet you have denied it. What you seek to hasten is not in my power. Judgment belongs to Allah alone. He relates the truth, and He is the best of judges.’”

And He says:

“Indeed, this Quran guides to that which is most upright.”

Likewise, He calls the Messenger a guide, saying:

“And indeed, you guide to a straight path.”

He also calls him a bearer of glad tidings and a warner, and calls the Quran a bearer of glad tidings and a warner. Likewise, since He bears witness in support of the Messenger and the believers through His speech which He sent down, and since His speech is a testimony from Him, His speech is a witness from Him, just as it judges, gives rulings, relates accounts, gives glad tidings, and warns.

When ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib was told, “You have appointed a created being as arbiter,” he replied, “I have not appointed a created being as arbiter; I have appointed only the Quran as arbiter.” For the judgment delivered by the Quran is Allah’s judgment, and the testimony borne by the Quran is the testimony of Allah, the Almighty and Majestic.

‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Zayd ibn Aslam said—he was a leading scholar and learned Quranic exegesis from his father, Zayd, who was a leading scholar in that field. Mālik and others learned exegesis from him, as did ‘Abd Allāh

---

## Page 7625

ibn Wahb, Mālik’s companion, and Asbagh ibn al-Faraj, the jurist—concerning Allah Most High’s words:

“What of one who stands upon clear evidence from his Lord and is followed by a witness from Him?”

He explained: “The Messenger of Allah ‘stood upon clear evidence from his Lord,’ and the Quran follows him as a witness as well, because it is from Allah.”

Among the interpretations al-Zajjāj mentioned was: “The Messenger of Allah recites the Quran, and it is a witness from Allah.”

Abū al-‘Āliyah said: “‘What of one who stands upon clear evidence from his Lord?’ refers to Muhammad, and ‘is followed by a witness from Him’ refers to the Quran.” Ibn Abī Hātim said: “A similar interpretation was reported from Ibn ‘Abbās, Muhammad ibn al-Hanafiyyah, Mujāhid, Abū Sālih, Ibrāhīm, ‘Ikrimah, al-Dahhāk, Qatādah, al-Suddī, Khusayf, and Ibn ‘Uyaynah.”

What they said is correct. However, this does not imply that his followers do not stand upon clear evidence from their Lord. Rather, they too stand upon clear evidence from their Lord.

Concerning “What of one who stands upon clear evidence from his Lord?” al-Hasan al-Basrī said: “The believer stands upon clear evidence from his Lord.” Ibn Abī Hātim related this.

It was also reported from al-Husayn ibn ‘Alī that “is followed by a witness from Him” means Muhammad, a witness from Allah. This interpretation implies that the one who stands upon clear evidence is the person for whom he bears witness.

The view that the witness is Muhammad is like the view that he is Gabriel, for both conveyed the Quran, and Allah chooses messengers from among the angels and from among mankind.

---

## Page 7626

so He chose Gabriel from among the angels and Muhammad from among mankind. He said concerning Gabriel:

“Indeed, it is the word of a noble messenger.”

And He said concerning Muhammad:

“Indeed, it is the word of a noble messenger.”

Both are messengers from Allah, as He said:

“Until clear evidence came to them: a messenger from Allah, reciting purified scriptures containing upright writings.”

Each is a messenger from Allah who conveyed what he was sent with and testifies that what he brought is Allah’s speech. As for their bearing witness to what the Quran bears witness to, this is something shared by everyone who believes in the Quran. Every believer bears witness to everything to which the Quran bears witness, by virtue of believing in it, whether or not he has conveyed it.

Thus, the Messenger’s belief in what he brought is distinct from his conveying it. He is commanded to do both and is rewarded for both, as Allah said:

“The Messenger believes in what has been sent down to him from his Lord, and so do the believers.”

For this reason, he would say, “I bear witness that I am Allah’s servant and His Messenger.” Gabriel and Muhammad therefore bear witness to what the Quran bears witness to by virtue of their belief in it, not by virtue of their being sent with it. Being sent with it entails their testimony that Allah spoke it. Someone who is not a messenger may nonetheless be sent with a message; the messenger then testifies that these are the sender’s words, even if the sender is neither truthful nor wise. Yet it is known that Gabriel and Muhammad know that Allah is truthful and wise, so they bear witness to what Allah bears witness to. Likewise, the angels and the believers testify that whatever Allah says is true

---

## Page 7627

and that Allah is truthful and wise: He declares only what is true and commands only what is just.

“The word of your Lord has been perfected in truth and justice.”

It has thus become clear that the testimony of Gabriel and Muhammad is the testimony of the Quran, and the testimony of the Quran is the testimony of Allah Most High. The Quran is a witness from Allah, and this witness agrees with and follows the one who stands upon clear evidence from his Lord. For the Quran revealed by Allah bears witness that the clear evidence, insight, light and guidance upon which the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and the believers stand are true.

“And follows him” uses the verb yatlūhu in the sense of following, as in His words:

“Those to whom We have given the Scripture recite it as it ought to be recited.”

That is, they follow it as it ought to be followed. He also says:

“By the moon when it follows the sun.”

That is, when it follows it. Likewise, qafāhu means “he followed him.” He has said:

“Do not follow that of which you have no knowledge.”

Thus, this witness follows the one who stands upon clear evidence from his Lord, confirming his truthfulness, commending him, supporting him and making him steadfast, as He says:

“Say: The Holy Spirit has brought it down from your Lord in truth, to make those who believe steadfast.”

And He says:

“We relate to you accounts of the messengers with which We make your heart steadfast.”

And He says:

“Those are the ones in whose hearts He has inscribed faith and whom He has strengthened with a spirit from Him.”

Allah has called the Quran an authority in more than one place. Thus, when this authority sent down by Allah follows this believer, it serves to strengthen him and give him mastery in knowledge and action. He says:

“We send down of the Quran that which is healing and mercy for the believers.”

---

## Page 7628

“Whenever a surah is revealed, some of them say, ‘Which of you has this increased in faith?’”—to the end of the verse.

Jundub ibn ‘Abd Allāh and ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar said: “We learned faith, then we learned the Quran, and so increased in faith.” Thus, they would learn faith and then learn the Quran.

Commenting on His words, “Light upon light,” one of them said: “The light of the Quran upon the light of faith,” as He says:

“But We have made it a light by which We guide whom We will of Our servants.”

Al-Suddī said concerning His words, “Light upon light”: “The light of the Quran and the light of faith when they come together; neither exists without the other.”

It thus becomes clear that His words, “Is one who stands upon clear evidence from his Lord,” refer to the guidance of faith. “And a witness from Him follows him”—that is, from Allah—refers to the Quran, a witness from Allah that agrees with faith and follows it. He says “follows him” because faith is the intended aim: the purpose of revealing the Quran is to bring about faith and increase it. Hence, faith without recitation of the Quran benefits its possessor, who enters Paradise through it, whereas the Quran without faith brings no benefit in the Hereafter; rather, its possessor is a hypocrite.

Thus, in the two authentic hadith collections, Abū Mūsā reports that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“A believer who recites the Quran is like a citron: its taste is pleasant and its fragrance is pleasant. A believer who does not recite the Quran is like a date: its taste is pleasant, but it has no fragrance. A hypocrite who recites the Quran is like an aromatic plant: its fragrance is pleasant, but its taste is bitter,

---

## Page 7629

and a hypocrite who does not recite the Quran is like a bitter gourd: its taste is bitter, and it has no fragrance.”

This is why He described faith as “clear evidence” and the Quran as a witness. The word for “clear evidence” derives from the notion of clarity and clarification. It denotes a clear way—a plain, evident path—and also that by which the truth becomes clear. It is therefore clear in itself and makes other things clear. It may also mean clarification, that is, indication and guidance. In this sense, it resembles guidance, as when one says, “So-and-so follows guidance and possesses knowledge.” Thus, it may be understood as a verbal noun, an adjective, or an agent.

An example is His saying:

“Has there not come to them clear evidence of what is in the former scriptures?”

This means an explanation of their contents, that which explains their contents, or the clear matter contained in them. He also called the Messenger “clear evidence,” saying:

“Until clear evidence came to them”—“a Messenger from Allah.”

For he makes the truth clear, while the believer follows a clear path and possesses light from his Lord. The purpose of a witness is to testify in favour of the one for whom he bears witness. Thus, it testifies in favour of the believer concerning what he follows. Faith is described as coming from Allah, just as the witness is described as coming from Allah, because Allah sent faith down into the very depths of people’s hearts. Thus, in the two authentic hadith collections, Hudhayfah reports that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Allah sent faith down into the very depths of people’s hearts; then they learned from the Quran and learned from the Sunnah.”

Furthermore, faith is what Allah has commanded. Moreover, faith is nothing other than what the Messenger has conveyed, and this is something the Messenger has conveyed. However, the Messenger receives two kinds of revelation: revelation spoken by Allah and recited, and revelation that is not recited. Thus, He said:

---

## Page 7630

“Thus We have revealed to you a spirit by Our command”—to the end of the verse. This encompasses both the Quran and faith.

It has been said that the pronoun in His words,

“We made it a light by which We guide whom We will of Our servants,”

refers to faith. This view is attributed to Ibn Abbās. It has also been said that it refers to the Quran, which is the view of al-Suddī. It encompasses both, while grammatically the pronoun refers to the spirit that He revealed: the revelation that brought faith and the Quran.

It has thus become clear that both are light and guidance from Allah, originating with Him. One is apprehended by the heart through the observable evidences of faith, such as the evidences of divine lordship and prophethood; the other is heard with the ears. The faith granted to the believer is like what Allah promised in His words:

“We will show them Our signs on the horizons and within themselves until it becomes clear to them that it is the truth.”

That is, that the Quran is true. These signs come after the revelation of the Quran. They include His granting victory to His Messenger and the believers at Badr and on other occasions. These were observable signs confirming what the Quran had foretold. Yet the believers had already believed before this.

It has also been said: the revelation of most of the Quran, through which Allah strengthened His Prophet and the believers. Hence His words:

“Is it not sufficient that your Lord is a witness over all things?”

He testifies to the truthfulness of His Messenger through the signs demonstrating his prophethood. The believers believed in those signs; then He sent down Quranic revelation as a witness in his favour. Then He manifested signs they could see for themselves, making it clear to them that the Quran is true.

---

## Page 7631

Thus the Quran accorded with faith, and the subsequent signs accorded with both the Quran and faith. Hence His words:

“And before it was the Book of Moses, a guide and a mercy.”

The pronoun in “And before it” refers to the witness, namely the Quran, as Allah Most High says:

“Say: Have you considered: if it is from Allah and you disbelieve in it, while a witness from the Children of Israel testifies to its like”—to the end of the verse.

Then He says:

“And before it was the Book of Moses, a guide and a mercy”—to the end of the verse.

The pronoun in “And before it” refers to the Quran: that is, before the Quran, as Ibn Zayd stated. It has also been said that it refers to the Messenger, as Mujāhid stated. The two interpretations necessarily go together.

There are two grammatical interpretations of His words, “And before it was the Book of Moses.” According to one, a single constituent is coordinated with another; according to the other, an entire clause is coordinated with another clause. On the first interpretation, the meaning is: “and a witness from Him follows it,” and the Book of Moses, which preceded it, likewise follows it in support. For it bears witness to the same thing as the Quran, and it is a witness from Allah. On the other interpretation, “And before it was the Book of Moses” is a separate clause, but its content confirms the Quran, as He states in Sūrat al-Ahqāf.

His words, “Those believe in it,” indicate that “Is one who stands upon clear evidence from his Lord” encompasses the believers, just as it encompasses the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, for they believe in both the earlier and the later Scripture. The pronoun “those” refers back to them, for they believe in it—in the witness from Allah. Believing in it is therefore believing in the Messenger and the Scripture that preceded it.

---

## Page 7632

Then He said:

“Whoever among the factions disbelieves in it, the Fire is his promised abode.”

Imam Ahmad, Ibn Abī Hātim and others narrated from Ayyūb, from Saʿīd ibn Jubayr, who said: “No hadith from the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, ever reached me in any form without my finding its confirmation in the Book of Allah. Then I learned that he had said: ‘No one in this community, whether Jew or Christian, hears of me and then fails to believe in what I have been sent with without entering the Fire.’”

Saʿīd said: “I asked, ‘Where is this in the Book of Allah?’ Then I came upon this verse: ‘Whoever among the factions disbelieves in it, the Fire is his promised abode.’” He said: “The factions are all the religious communities.”

As for His words, “Those believe in it,” everyone who stands upon clear evidence from his Lord believes in the witness from Allah, and believing in it means believing in what Moses brought. He said, “Those believe in it”: they are the followers of Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, from among his Companions and others until the Hour is established. Then He said:

“Whoever among the factions disbelieves in it, the Fire is his promised abode.”

The factions are the various peoples who banded together and formed parties, as Allah Most High said:

“Before them, the people of Noah denied, as did the factions after them. Every nation plotted against its messenger to seize him.”

Allah has mentioned groups of these factions in this surah and elsewhere. Concerning those who denied Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, Allah Most High said:

“They are but a host of the factions, to be defeated there.”

They are those concerning whom He said:

“So set your face toward the religion, inclining to the truth,

---

## Page 7633

the natural disposition Allah has instilled in mankind. There is no changing Allah’s creation. That is the upright religion, but most people do not know. Turn to Him in repentance, fear Him, establish prayer, and do not be among the polytheists—those who divided their religion and became sects, each faction rejoicing in what it has.”

Concerning the Christian factions, He said:

“But the factions differed among themselves. So woe to those who disbelieve when they witness a tremendous Day!”

And the subsequent verses.

As for the view that the pronoun in His words, “Those believe in it,” refers to the people of truth, identified as Moses, Jesus and Muhammad: if what is meant is those who believed in the two Scriptures before the Quran was revealed, they have not previously been mentioned, and the pronoun in “in it” is singular. Moreover, someone who believed in the Book of Moses but not in the Gospel, after the latter had been revealed and the proof had been established against him through it, would not be a believer.

Abū al-Faraj reported these two views without naming their proponents. Al-Baghawī and others mentioned no disagreement that those intended are the believers in Muhammad. They did, however, mention the view that those intended are the People of the Book who believed in him, and this is plausible. Perhaps this is what those whose view Abū al-Faraj reported meant; otherwise, their statement has no basis.

It is surprising that Abū al-Faraj subsequently mentioned four views concerning the factions:

“The first” is that they comprise all religious communities. This was the view of Saʿīd ibn Jubayr.

---

## Page 7634

and “the second” identifies them as the Jews and Christians; this was Qatāda’s view. “The third” identifies them as Quraysh; this was al-Suddī’s view. “The fourth” identifies them as Banū Umayya and Banū al-Mughīra. He said: “That is, Abū Talha ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā.” This was Muqātil’s view.

This verse requires that the pronoun in His words, “Whoever disbelieves in it,” refer to the Quran. Likewise, in “Those believe in it,” the reference is to the Quran. The evidence is His statement, Exalted is He:

“So be in no doubt about it: it is the truth from your Lord.”

This undoubtedly refers to the Quran. It has also been said that it refers to the preceding statement concerning “whoever among the factions disbelieves in it.” But this too is part of the Quran. Thus, by their agreement, what is meant is belief or disbelief in the Quran. Anyone who said that “those” refers to people other than those who believe in Muhammad did not grasp what he was saying.

Two interpretations of His words, “And before it was the Book of Moses,” have already been discussed: is this the coordination of a clause or of a single constituent? Most hold that it is a single constituent.

Al-Zajjāj explained the meaning as follows: Before this there was the Book of Moses, evidence of Muhammad’s mission. Thus, “they recite the Book of Moses” is coordinated with His words, “and a witness from Him recites it”—that is, “and he recites the Book of Moses”—because Moses and Jesus foretold Muhammad in the Torah and the Gospel. The word imāman (“as a guide”) is in the accusative as a circumstantial qualifier.

---

## Page 7635

I say:

It has already been explained that the witness follows the one who stands upon clear evidence from his Lord—that is, he follows him, bearing witness to the clear evidence upon which he stands.

His words, “Is one who stands upon clear evidence from his Lord,” mean: is he like one who does not? Al-Zajjāj said: “The corresponding clause is omitted because what follows indicates it, namely His words:

‘The two groups are like the blind and the deaf, and the seeing and the hearing.’”

Ibn Qutayba said: “Having mentioned, before this verse, people who inclined towards this world and desired it, He then introduced this verse. The implied wording is: ‘Is one whose condition is such like one who desires this world?’ What preceded was sufficient in place of an answer, since it indicated it.” Ibn al-Anbārī said: “It was omitted only because the meaning is clear. This occurs frequently in the Quran.”

I say:

A parallel to the omission in this verse is:

“Is one whose evil deeds have been made appealing to him, so that he regards them as good”—

that is, like one who is not so? After this, He says:

“Whoever among the factions disbelieves in it.”

This is the other category, set in contrast to the one who stands upon clear evidence from his Lord. On this understanding, the verse means:

“Is one who stands upon clear evidence from his Lord like those whose evil deeds have been made appealing to them and who follow their own desires?”

It also means: “Is one who stands upon clear evidence from his Lord”—that is, who has insight into his religion—like one who desires the life of this world and its adornment? This is like His words:

“Is one who was dead, then We gave him life…”

And His words:

“Is one who stands upon clear evidence from his Lord like one whose evil deeds have been made appealing to him?”

And His words:

“Is one who guides to the truth more worthy of being followed, or one who does not find guidance…”

In constructions of this kind, however, what is omitted may be something else, as in His words:

“Or one who

---

## Page 7636

is brought up amid adornments”—that is, do you assign to Him one who is brought up amid adornments? There must be an indication of what has been omitted. The omitted clause might also be something such as: Is one whose condition is such to be condemned, disparaged, shunned rather than followed, put to trial, or punished? As He says:

“Is one whose evil deeds have been made appealing to him, so that he regards them as good? For Allah leads astray whom He wills and guides whom He wills.”

It has been said that the omitted clause in this verse yields the meaning: “Is one whose evil deeds have been made appealing to him”—so that he regards falsehood as truth and ugliness as beauty—like one whom Allah has guided, so that he sees truth as truth, falsehood as falsehood, ugliness as ugliness, and beauty as beauty?

It has also been said that its answer is implicit in His words:

“So do not let yourself perish in sorrow over them.”

But one may object: what does the interrogative mean unless an implied clause is supplied? That is: this person—can you guide him, or can your Lord? Or can you requite him? As He says:

“Have you seen the one who takes his own desire as his god? Will you then be a guardian over him?”

Hence His words:

“For Allah leads astray whom He wills and guides whom He wills.”

And likewise His words:

“Have you seen the one who takes his own desire as his god, and whom Allah has led astray with full knowledge…”

On this understanding, its meaning is like that of His words:

“Is one who stands upon clear evidence from his Lord like one whose evil deeds have been made appealing to him?”

Accordingly, the meaning here is: “Is one who stands upon clear evidence from his Lord, whom a witness from Him follows, and before it was the Book of Moses” to be condemned, opposed, disbelieved, or the like? This parallels His words:

“Say: I stand upon clear evidence from my Lord, yet you have denied it.”

And He omitted the consequent of

---

## Page 7637

the conditional clause, as also in His words:

“Have you considered whether he is upon guidance, or enjoins righteousness? Have you considered whether he denies the truth and turns away?”

It is now clear that the verse conveys one of the noblest meanings, one from which everyone can benefit. The verse speaks of someone who stands upon clear evidence from his Lord—faith to which the Quran bears witness—and thus stands upon light and proof from his Lord concerning what rational and revealed proofs establish. As He says:

“And We have sent down to you a clear light.”

The clear light sent down includes the Quran. Qatādah said: “Clear evidence from your Lord.” Al-Thawrī said: “It is the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.” Al-Baghawī said: “This is the view of the commentators.” Yet I have not found it transmitted from anyone other than the latter, nor did Ibn al-Jawzī attribute it to anyone else.

He mentioned three interpretations of “proof”: the first is that it means an authoritative argument; the second, that it means the Messenger. He also attributed to Qatādah the view that it means the Quran. What Ibn Abī Hātim transmitted from Qatādah through a reliable chain of transmission, however, is that it means clear evidence from Allah. Clear evidence and authoritative argument encompass the signs with which the prophets were sent. Thus, everything that establishes the prophethood of Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is a proof.

Allah Most High says:

“These, then, are two proofs from your Lord.”

To those who said, “None will enter Paradise unless they are Jews or Christians,” He said: “Say: Bring your proof.” Muhammad is the truthful one whose truthfulness is confirmed, and Allah has established many proofs of his truthfulness

---

## Page 7638

and Muhammad himself became a proof, and he established proofs of his truthfulness. Evidence supporting evidence is itself evidence, and proof supporting proof is itself proof. Every sign attesting to him is a proof. “Proof” is a generic term, not restricted to a single proof, as in His words:

“Say: Bring your proof, if you are truthful.”

Had they then brought several proofs, they would have complied. The point is that reason recognizes that this proof attests to his truthfulness. It is clear evidence from Allah, as Qatādah said, and an authoritative argument from Allah, as Mujāhid and al-Suddī said. The believer stands upon that clear evidence, followed by a witness from Allah: the light that He sent down along with the proof. And Allah knows best.

Section

As for those who say that His words,

“Is one who stands upon clear evidence from his Lord”

refer to Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace—as a group of the early Muslims said—they may mean to give an example rather than restrict the meaning to him alone. Quran commentators often intend this. Muhammad was the first to stand upon clear evidence from his Lord and to be followed by a witness from Him. The same applies to the other prophets, and he is the best of them and their leader. The believers follow him, and through him they came to stand upon clear evidence from their Lord.

An address may be directed to him in its wording while its meaning is general, as in His words:

“So if you are in doubt about what

---

## Page 7639

We have revealed to you.”

“If you associate others with Allah, your deeds will surely come to nothing.”

“So when you have finished, strive diligently.”

“Say: If I go astray, I go astray only to my own detriment.”

There are other examples of this kind. The basic principle is that whatever is addressed to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, concerning commands, prohibitions, and permissions also applies to his community, just as his community shares with him in legal rulings and other matters, unless evidence establishes that something is exclusive to him. Thus, whatever ruling applies to him also applies to his community unless it is specifically restricted to him. This is the position of the early Muslims and the jurists, and the evidence for it is abundant. For example, Allah says:

“So when Zayd had no further need of her, We gave her to you in marriage,”

to the end of the verse. And when He permitted him to marry a woman who offered herself to him in marriage, He said:

“Exclusively for you, rather than the other believers,”

to the end of the verse.

If this holds even when the wording specifically addresses him, how can wording that applies generally to both him and the believers be restricted to him alone? The word man (“whoever”) is among the strongest expressions of generality, particularly when used conditionally or interrogatively, as in His words:

“So whoever does an atom’s weight of good will see it,
and whoever does an atom’s weight of evil will see it.”

And His words:

“Is one whose evil deeds have been made appealing to him, so that he regards them as good…?”

And His words:

“Is one who was dead, whom We then brought to life…?”

And His words:

“Is one who stands upon clear evidence from his Lord like one whose evil deeds have been made appealing to him?”

Furthermore, He subsequently says:

“Those believe in it. But whoever among the factions disbelieves in it, the Fire will be his appointed destination.”

After this, He mentions:

“The likeness of the two groups.”

The two groups have already been mentioned before this. And His words, “Those

---

## Page 7640

believe in it,” refer to a group, yet nothing suitable to serve as their referent has previously been mentioned except man (“whoever”). A pronoun sometimes agrees with the singular wording of man and sometimes with its collective meaning, as in His words:

“Among them is one who listens to you.”

“Among them are those who listen to you.”

“Whoever does righteous deeds, whether male or female…”

“Whoever does righteousness, whether male or female, while being a believer, We will surely grant them a good life,” to the end of the verse.

A demonstrative referring to its collective meaning makes this even clearer than a pronoun does. Thus, His words, “Those believe in it,” show that those who stand upon clear evidence from their Lord are many, not one.

Ibn Abī Hātim said: ‘Āmir ibn Sālih narrated to us, from his father, from al-Hasan al-Basrī, concerning the words, “Is one who stands upon clear evidence from his Lord…?” He said: “The believer stands upon clear evidence from his Lord.” What al-Hasan al-Basrī said is correct, and the Messenger is the foremost of the believers, as He says:

“And I have been commanded to be among the believers.”

As for those who say that the witness from Allah is Muhammad—as Ibn Abī Hātim reported: al-Ashajj narrated to us; Abū Usāmah narrated to us, from ‘Awf, from Sulaymān al-Fulānī, from al-Husayn ibn ‘Alī, concerning the words, “And a witness from Him follows it”: “This means Muhammad, a witness from Allah”—here, his being a witness from Allah means that he is Allah’s Messenger. He testifies that the believers follow the truth. Although he also testifies on his own behalf that he is Allah’s Messenger, the truth of his testimony about himself is already established by the proofs demonstrating his prophethood. His testimony concerning the believers, however, is known only through him, through what has reached him of the Quran and what he reports from

---

## Page 7641

his Lord. Thus, when he testifies, he is a witness from Allah.

As for his testimony concerning their faith, their acceptance of the truth, and other matters, it is as in His words:

“How will it be, then, when We bring a witness from every nation and bring you as a witness over these?”

“And the Messenger will be a witness over you.”

Yet whoever holds this view may mean the Quran by “clear evidence,” for the believer follows the Quran, and Muhammad is a witness from Allah who recites it, just as Gabriel recited it.

Whoever says that the witness is Muhammad’s tongue intends by this statement recitation: that is, Muhammad’s tongue recites the Quran and is a witness “from him,” meaning from his own person, since his tongue is part of him. This view and others like it are weak. And Allah knows best. This is assuming that the statement is established as coming from the person to whom it is attributed, for both this view and its opposite are reported from ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib.

A group of ignorant Shī‘ah thought that ‘Alī was the witness “from him,” meaning from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, as he said to him:

“You are of me, and I am of you.”

But he also said this of others. It is established in the two Sahīh collections that he said:

“The Ash‘arīs are of me, and I am of them.”

And he said of Julaybīb:

“This man is of me, and I am of him.”

---

## Page 7642

And every believer is of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, as Abraham, Allah’s close friend, said:

“Whoever follows me is of me.”

And it is said:

“Whoever does not taste it is of me.”

They narrated this view from ‘Alī himself, but it is also reported from him, through a stronger chain of transmission, that he said: “Whoever said this has lied.” Ibn Abī Hātim cited the following report from Husayn ibn Zayd al-Tahhān: Ishāq ibn Mansūr narrated to us; Sufyān narrated to us, from al-A‘mash, from al-Minhāl, from ‘Abbād ibn ‘Abd Allāh, who said: “‘Alī said: ‘There is no one among Quraysh about whom a verse has not been revealed.’ He was asked: ‘What, then, was revealed about you?’ He replied:

“‘And a witness from Him follows it.’”

This is certainly a lie attributed to ‘Alī. Even if the transmission from this ‘Abbād is established, he narrates objectionable reports from ‘Alī, such as the statement: “I am the greatest of the truthful; I embraced Islam seven years before anyone else.”

They have also narrated from ‘Alī something that contradicts this. Ibn Abī Hātim said: My father narrated to us; ‘Amr ibn ‘Alī al-Bāhilī narrated to us; Muhammad ibn Shawwās narrated to us; Sa‘īd ibn Abī ‘Arūbah narrated to us, from Qatādah, from ‘Urwah, from Muhammad ibn ‘Alī—meaning Ibn al-Hanafiyyah—who said: “I said to my father: ‘Father, concerning “And a witness from Him follows it,” people say that you are that witness.’ He replied: ‘I wish I were. But it is his tongue?’”

Ibn Abī Hātim said: “Something similar was also narrated from al-Hasan and Qatādah.”

I say: It has already been reported from his son al-Husayn that the “witness from Him” is Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. The scholars of the Prophet’s household only stated that it was Muhammad in refutation of those ignorant people who said that it was ‘Alī. For this surah was revealed in Mecca, and ‘Alī was

---

## Page 7643

at that time a young child who had not yet reached puberty. He was among those who followed the Messenger. Even had he been the Messenger of Allah’s son rather than his cousin, his testimony would have carried no weight with either Muslims or disbelievers. Indeed, such testimony is open to suspicion of bias arising from kinship. Hence, most scholars hold that a father’s testimony in favour of his child, or a child’s testimony in favour of his father, is not accepted. How, then, could such testimony be made evidence confirming the prophethood of Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace?

For the same reason, they said that Allah’s words,

“and whoever has knowledge of the Scripture,”

refer to ‘Alī. Besides their lying, they are the most ignorant of people, for they have attributed to Allah and the Messenger the use of an argument that only an ignorant person would use. Seeking to exalt ‘Alī, they have attributed ignorance to Allah and the Messenger. Yet ‘Alī’s merit lies only in his following the Messenger: if the foundation is undermined, what rests upon it is invalidated.

As for the view of some commentators that the “witness” is Gabriel, peace be upon him, ‘Ikrimah narrated this from Ibn ‘Abbās. Ibn Abī Hātim cited it from him, and cited similar statements from Abū al-‘Āliyah, Abū Sālih, Mujāhid—in one of the reports from him—Ibrāhīm, ‘Ikrimah, al-Dahhāk and ‘Atā’ al-Khurāsānī. These commentators understood “and follows it” to mean “recites it”: that is, a witness from Allah—Gabriel—recites the Quran, which is the clear proof. Another explanation is that what they mean is that the Quran, which Gabriel recites, is the witness for Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace; that is, what he recites has come from Allah. The weakness of this view has already been explained, for everyone who interprets “follows it”

---

## Page 7644

as meaning “recites it” takes the pronoun to refer to the Quran and regards the witness as something other than the Quran. Yet the Quran has not previously been mentioned. He only said:

“Is one who stands upon clear proof from his Lord…”

“Clear proof” cannot be interpreted as memorizing the Quran, for all believers stand upon clear proof from their Lord, even if they have not memorized the Quran. Insight into the religion, however, is different: anyone who lacks insight from his Lord is not truly a believer, but is among those who will say to Munkar and Nakīr, “Ah, ah! I do not know. I heard people saying something, so I said it.” The Quran’s praise is for the person who stands upon clear proof from his Lord: he possesses guidance, light and insight, whether or not he has memorized the Quran. If following the Quran is intended, that is faith itself. Moreover, most of the Quran had not yet been revealed when this verse was revealed.

It has already been explained that what is distinctive about Gabriel and Muhammad is their conveying the message from Allah and their truthfulness in doing so. As for the truth of Allah’s message, this is what every messenger bears witness to. The two of them are not distinguished by this; rather, they believe in it just as every angel and every believer does. Their testimony that the Prophet and the believers are upon the truth belongs to this second, shared category.

Had He said, “A messenger from Allah conveys it and brings it down,” their interpretation would have been tenable, as He says:

---

## Page 7645

“Say, ‘The Holy Spirit has brought it down.’”

“The Trustworthy Spirit has brought it down.”

“For he has brought it down upon your heart by Allah’s permission.”

As for his being a witness who recites it, there is no parallel to this in the Quran. Moreover, the witness that is from Allah is His speech, for His speech came down from Him, as in the words, “They know that it has been sent down from your Lord in truth.” A messenger may also be described as being from Him, as He says, “A messenger from Allah.” Of a person who bears witness, one says, “He is one of Allah’s witnesses.” But calling him “a witness from Allah”—as the signs Allah creates to confirm His Messenger are called “a proof from Allah” and “signs from Allah”—requires evidence of such usage.

The Quran was revealed in the language of Quraysh as found in the Quran itself. Its expressions are therefore to be interpreted according to its own familiar usage wherever that usage is found; one must not depart from that usage when it is available. Resorting to usage outside the Quran is necessary only for an expression that has no parallel within it, such as:

“Ah! Indeed, Allah…”

“But it was no time for escape.”

“An overflowing cup.”

“Fruit and pasture.”

And “an unfair division,” along with other uncommon expressions in the Quran.

Those who advanced these interpretations were led to them by His words, “And a witness follows him.” They supposed that the verb meant reciting, although the Quran had not previously been mentioned. Thus one says that Gabriel recited it, another that Muhammad did, and another that his tongue did. Yet the word tilāwa is used in the Quran and in familiar Arabic to mean following. Many commentators do not mention the correct interpretation of this verse, leaving the perceptive reader bewildered.

---

## Page 7646

and he gives no interpretation of “one who stands upon clear evidence from his Lord” other than that it means the Messenger, while mentioning several views concerning the witness.

More surprising still, he says concerning “Those believe”: “Those are Muhammad’s Companions.” Another view is that it means those among the People of the Book who embraced Islam. Yet, according to his interpretation, they have not previously been mentioned. How, then, can they be referred to in His words, “believe in it”?

Abū al-Faraj mentioned the view that they are the Muslims, but did not mention that the verse encompasses both the Prophet and the believers. When mentioning the view that they are the Muslims, he said: “This accords with al-Dahhāk’s interpretation that the clear evidence is Allah’s Messenger.”

Four interpretations of “clear evidence” have been mentioned: that it is the religion, as Abū Sālih reported from Ibn ‘Abbās; that it is Allah’s Messenger, as al-Dahhāk said; that it is the Quran, as Ibn Zayd said; and that it is clarification, as Muqātil said.

He then said: “If we say that ‘one who stands upon clear evidence from his Lord’ means the Muslims, the meaning is that they follow the Messenger, who is the clear evidence, and that this Prophet is followed by a witness from Him who confirms his truthfulness.”

But when the Muslims stand upon clear evidence, that evidence is faith in the Messenger; the clear evidence is not the Messenger himself. Moreover, the Messenger is not mentioned in the wording. Thus, the pronoun in His words, “and follows him,” must refer back to “from Him” (1), although referring it back to the clear evidence is more appropriate.

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) The printed edition reads min (“from”); the correction follows Ibn Taymiyyah’s al-Tafsīr al-kabīr, ed. Dr. ‘Abd al-Rahmān ‘Umayrah, 5:32.

---

## Page 7647

He interpreted the clear evidence as the Messenger and understood the witness to attest to his truthfulness. The witness, then, is Gabriel or someone else. Had he said, “The witness is the Quran, which bears witness for the believers, for it follows them just as they follow it,” he would have stated the correct interpretation.

He mentioned many views that others did not mention. Concerning “follows him,” he cited two interpretations: the first, “follows him”; and the second, “recites it.” Both are well-known interpretations. Concerning the pronoun in yatlūhu (“follows him/it”), he mentioned two views: that it refers to the Prophet, and that it refers to the Quran.

The correct analysis is that it refers either to “one who” or to the clear evidence. The clear evidence means the Quran, so the meaning would be that the witness is from the Quran. If the pronoun refers to “one who,” and this is understood to mean the Prophet alone, may Allah bless him and grant him peace—a view whose error has already been explained—then the pronoun refers to the clear evidence. If, however, “one who” encompasses every believer who stands upon clear evidence from his Lord—and Allah’s Messenger is foremost among the believers—then it includes them all.

What further clarifies this is that Allah’s Messenger brought the message from Allah, something unique to him. Yet affirming the truth of this message and believing in it is obligatory upon both humankind and jinn. The Messenger is the first obliged to believe in this message that Allah sent him

---

## Page 7648

with. Hence He said in Surah Yūnus:

“Say, ‘O mankind, if you are in doubt about my religion, I do not worship those whom you worship besides Allah. Rather, I worship Allah, who causes you to die, and I have been commanded to be among the believers.’”

And He said:

“Say, ‘I have been commanded to be the first to submit.’”

There are other verses to this effect. Thus, two momentous matters concern him, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

The first is establishing his prophethood and his truthfulness in what he conveyed from Allah. This is specific to him. The second is believing what he brought and affirming that what he brought from Allah is true and must be followed. This is obligatory upon him and upon everyone else.

Among those whom a created being sends, there may be someone who is believed concerning his message but does not himself follow it—either because he disparages the one who sent him or because he disobeys him, even though he was sent with something true. Kings often send a messenger bearing letters or other things. The messengers convey their message and are believed concerning it, yet a messenger may oppose the one who sent him more than do those to whom he was sent.

For this reason, a group, including al-Qādī Abū Bakr, thought that merely being a messenger of Allah does not necessarily entail praise. He then said, “This may be said of someone who accepts the message and conveys it, and of someone who does not accept it.”

But this is mistaken, for Allah never sends a messenger without having chosen him, and he conveys his Lord’s messages. And Allah’s messengers

---

## Page 7649

are the most obedient of all creation to Allah and have the strongest faith in what they were sent with. A created being, by contrast, may send someone who lies about him, disobeys him, or does not believe that obedience to him is obligatory. The Creator is far above such things. Yet those who said this consider it possible for the Lord to send anyone with anything. In their view, reason offers no grounds for ruling this out. They hold the messengers free of what Muslims unanimously agree they are free of only because, in their view, this is established by revelation—not by virtue of their being messengers. This has been discussed at length elsewhere, and this principle has been shown to be mistaken.

Since both matters concern him, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, regarding the first one says, “I believe him,” as Allah Most High says:

“But no one believed Moses except some youths from among his people.”

And He says:

“He believes in Allah and believes the believers.”

And:

“You will not believe us.”

Regarding the second, one says, “I believe in Allah.” We must therefore believe him and believe in what he brought. Allah Most High mentions both matters. First, He mentions what establishes his prophethood and truthfulness, saying:

“Or do they say, ‘He fabricated it’? Say, ‘Then produce ten fabricated surahs like it, and call upon whomever you can besides Allah, if you are truthful.’”

“If they do not respond to you, then know that it was revealed with Allah’s knowledge and that there is no deity except Him.”

This has already been pointed out.

---

## Page 7650

Since a person is prevented from following the Messenger by one of two things—ignorance or corrupt intent—Allah mentioned what dispels ignorance: the signs establishing the Messenger’s truthfulness. He then mentioned those whose intentions are corrupt, saying:

“Whoever desires the life of this world and its adornments, We will repay them in full for their deeds therein, and they will not be given less than their due.”

“Those are the ones who will have nothing in the Hereafter but the Fire. What they have accomplished therein will come to nothing, and all they used to do will be in vain.”

These are the people whose intentions are corrupt. These two things prevent people from following this Messenger. Likewise, in al-Baqarah, Allah mentioned what brings about knowledge and sound intent, saying:

“And if you are in doubt about what We have sent down upon Our servant, then produce a surah like it and call your witnesses besides Allah, if you are truthful.”

Then He said:

“But if you do not—and you will never be able to—then fear the Fire whose fuel is people and stones, prepared for the disbelievers.”

Having established these two foundations, He then proceeded to explain faith in him and the condition of those who believe and those who disbelieve, saying:

“Is one who stands on clear evidence from his Lord”—to the end of the verse.

Then He said:

“And who is more unjust than one who fabricates a lie against Allah? They will be brought before their Lord, and the witnesses will say, ‘These are the ones who lied about their Lord.’”

This includes every disbeliever who lies about Allah by falsely claiming to be a messenger. It also includes everyone who denies a truthful messenger, saying, “Allah did not send this man and did not command this,” thereby lying about Allah. This occurs only in someone with corrupt

---

## Page 7651

intentions because of his love and desire for this world, and in ignorant people who love leadership and seek supremacy in the land.

In the two Sahīh collections, Ibn ʿUmar reports that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah will draw the believer close to Him on the Day of Resurrection and shield him, saying, ‘You did such-and-such on such-and-such a day, and such-and-such on another day.’ He will reply, ‘Yes.’ Allah will then say, ‘I concealed these sins for you in this world, and today I forgive them for you.’ Then he will be given the record of his good deeds in his right hand.”

As for the disbelievers and hypocrites:

“The witnesses will say, ‘These are the ones who lied about their Lord.’ Surely Allah’s curse is upon the wrongdoers.”

Then Allah Most High mentioned those who believe and do righteous deeds, followed by a comparison of the two groups. Whoever reflects upon the Quran, considers what precedes and follows a verse, and understands the Quran’s purpose will grasp the intended meaning, recognize the guidance and the message, and distinguish sound understanding from deviation and distortion.

Interpreting it solely according to what its wording might admit, detached from everything else that clarifies its meaning, is a source of error for those who go astray in interpretation. This applies especially to many who discuss it in terms of linguistic possibilities. They make more mistakes than the well-known Quran commentators, for they do not seek to understand its meaning as those commentators do. More mistaken than either group is someone whose aim is not to understand what Allah intends;

---

## Page 7652

rather, his aim is to interpret the verse in a way that prevents his opponent from citing it as evidence. Such people fall into various forms of distortion. Hence, some of them have permitted interpreting a verse contrary to the interpretation of the early generations, saying:

“If people differ over the interpretation of a verse, holding two views, those who come after them may introduce a third view—unlike when they differ over legal rulings, holding two views.”

This is mistaken. If they agree that the verse means either this or that, then claiming that its intended meaning lies outside these two views contradicts their consensus. Yet this is the approach of someone whose aim is to rebut an argument, not to discover the intended meaning. Otherwise, how could the Muslim community go astray in understanding the Quran, all of them understanding something other than its intended meaning, [and there come](1) later generations who understand what was intended? So much for this, and Allah knows best.

Section

His words, “Is one who stands upon clear evidence from his Lord,” as discussed above, are like His words:

“Say: I stand upon clear evidence from my Lord.”

And His words:

“Is one who stands upon clear evidence from his Lord like those whose evil deeds have been made appealing to them and who follow their own desires?”

And His words:

“Is one whose heart Allah has opened to Islam, so that he is upon light from his Lord.”

And His words:

“They are upon guidance from their Lord.”

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) There is a blank in the original. The addition is taken from the editions of Ibn Taymiyyah’s al-Tafsīr al-kabīr edited by Dr. Muhammad al-Jalnīd, 3/249, and Dr. ʿAbd al-Rahmān ʿUmayrah, 5/37.
> Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd states (p. 132):
> Perhaps the blank should read [then come], making the passage: “And they all understand something other than the intended meaning[, then come] later generations who understand the intended meaning,” or words to that effect. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 7653

For expressions of this kind show that the believer stands upon something from Allah. This wording thus combines the preposition indicating elevation, “upon,” with the preposition (min, “from”), indicating a point of origin. Things described using this preposition of origin—so that one says, “It is from Allah”—fall into two categories. If something is an attribute that subsists neither independently nor in a created being, then it is an attribute of His. But anything that is an entity subsisting independently or in a created being is created.

The first category is illustrated by His words:

“But the word from Me has come into effect.”

And His words:

“They know that it has been sent down from your Lord in truth.”

As the early generations said: “The Quran is the speech of Allah, uncreated. From Him it originated, and to Him it will return.”

The second category is illustrated by His words:

“He has subjected to you whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on earth—all from Him. Indeed, in that are signs for people who reflect.”

And His words:

“Whatever blessing you have is from Allah.”

And:

“Whatever good befalls you is from Allah.”

Likewise, it is said that inspiration and prompting toward good are from Allah, while inspiration and prompting toward evil are from Satan, and whispering is from Satan.

Such attribution is of two kinds: sometimes it is made with regard to the cause, and sometimes with regard to the outcome and end result. Here, good things mean blessings, and bad things mean calamities; all are from Allah. However, those good things are blessings Allah has bestowed upon the servant, so they are from Him as acts of kindness and grace. These calamities, by contrast, are punishment for a sin originating from the servant himself. They are therefore from his own self insofar as his evil deed was

---

## Page 7654

their cause, and they are a punishment for him; for the soul desired those sins and was prompted toward them.

At other times, this attribution concerns good and evil deeds, and the ideas and intentions cast into the heart. Thus, truth is said to be from Allah, who inspired His servant with it; falsehood is said to be from Satan, who whispered it, and also from the soul, because it desired it. Thus, concerning conclusions they reached through independent reasoning, ʿUmar, Ibn ʿUmar, and Ibn Masʿūd said: “If it is right, it is from Allah; if it is mistaken, it is from us and from Satan, and Allah and His Messenger are free of it.”

These are Ibn Masʿūd’s words in the report concerning Birwaʿ bint Wāshiq. He said: “If it is right, it is from Allah; if it is mistaken, it is from me and from Satan.” For he had given a ruling: if it accorded with Allah’s ruling, then it was from Allah, because it accorded with His knowledge and judgment. It was from Him in the sense that He, glorified is He, inspired His servant with it, without the mediation of Satan or the soul. If it was mistaken, however, Satan whispered it, and the soul desired it and whispered it—even though it was created within him and Allah created it within him. Yet Allah did not prescribe it, even if what occurred to me was not inspired by the angel. As Ibn Masʿūd said:

“The angel has a prompting in the heart of the son of Adam, and Satan has a prompting. The angel’s prompting promises good and affirms the truth, while Satan’s prompting promises evil and denies the truth.”

Affirmation pertains to statements of fact, whereas promising good and evil pertains to urging and volition.

The Most High said:

“Satan threatens you with poverty and commands you to commit immorality, while Allah promises you forgiveness

---

## Page 7655

from Him and bounty. And Allah is All-Encompassing, All-Knowing.” These good deeds, then, are from Allah, Mighty and Majestic, in these two respects.

The first is that He commands and loves them; and when they are good, He affirms them and makes them known. Thus they belong to His knowledge and judgment. They also come from His inspiration to His servant and His favor upon him, without the mediation of the soul or Satan.

They are therefore attributed specifically to Allah because they belong to His knowledge and judgment, and because an angel brings them down to the servant—just as the Quran is specifically attributed to Him as His speech, whereas Musaylimah’s “Quran” is attributed to Satan. The true and just inspirations that Allah casts into believers’ hearts are part of Allah’s revelation, as is what He shows them in dreams. ʿUbādah ibn al-Sāmit said: “A believer’s dream is speech by which the Lord addresses His servant in his sleep.” ʿUmar said: “Draw close to the mouths of the obedient and listen to what they say, for true things become manifest to them.” The Most High has said:

“And when I inspired the disciples: ‘Believe in Me and in My Messenger.’”

“And We inspired the mother of Moses.”

“And We revealed to him: ‘You will surely tell them of this deed of theirs.’”

He also said:

“And inspired it with its wickedness and its righteousness.”

According to the majority view, this means that He inspired the wicked soul with its wickedness and the righteous soul with its righteousness. In his view, inspiration means clarification through scriptural and rational evidence.

Ahl al-Sunnah say: Both kinds are from Allah: this common guidance

---

## Page 7656

and that special guidance, although He has called it inspiration, just as He has called it guidance, as in His words:

“As for Thamūd, We guided them, but they preferred blindness to guidance.”

Likewise, His words, “We guided him to the two paths,” have been interpreted to mean: We explained to him the paths of good and evil. This is the general guidance of clarification, shared by all.

Another interpretation is: We guided the believer to the path of good and the disbeliever to the path of evil. According to this interpretation, He has called wickedness guidance, just as those others have called clarification inspiration.

Likewise, His words,

“Indeed, We guided him to the way, whether grateful or ungrateful,”

have been understood as referring to guidance shared by all: He explained to the human being the path he ought to follow and the path he ought not to follow. Another interpretation is that He guided each of the two groups to the path it actually followed, “whether grateful or ungrateful.”

Calling this guidance, however, may be justified on the grounds that it is guidance in a qualified, rather than an unrestricted, sense, as when He says:

“So give them good tidings of a painful punishment,”

and:

“They believe in superstition and false deities.”

And since He “speaks the truth” and “commands justice,” it accords with His speech and command, His knowledge and judgment, just as the Quran and all His other speech do. It is also attributable to Him because He has bestowed a favor upon the servant through His host, the angels.

Of its opposite—error—it is said: This is from Satan and the soul, because Allah neither says it nor commands it, and because the only one who implants it in a person’s heart is

---

## Page 7657

Satan, and the person’s own soul accepts it from Satan, who makes something seem attractive to it, so that it obeys him in that matter. Not everything that comes from Satan is something for which the servant is punished, though it deprives him of certain good deeds. Forgetfulness, for example, comes from Satan, as do wet dreams, drowsiness during remembrance and prayer, and swooning during remembrance. The servant incurs no sin for what overwhelms him involuntarily, provided it was neither intentional on his part nor caused by a sin.

Thus, His words,

“Indeed, I stand upon clear evidence from my Lord,”

and similar statements mentioned earlier belong to this category. The same applies to His words:

“That is because those who disbelieve follow falsehood, while those who believe follow the truth from their Lord.”

For believers affirm what Allah has declared and carry out what Allah has commanded, whether imparted initially or conveyed to them, as with the Quran. There is also the saying:

“Allah sent down trust into the very roots of men’s hearts.”

This trust descends into the hearts of believers from His light and guidance. These are religious good deeds and true religious knowledge, beneficial in this world and the Hereafter. This is faith: the gracious gift of the Benefactor and the greatest of blessings.

As for His words,

“Whatever good befalls you is from Allah,”

this includes all worldly blessings, such as well-being, provision, and victory. These are good things with which Allah tests the servant, just as He tests him with afflictions: will he be grateful or not, and will he be patient or not?

As the Most High says:

“We tested them with good things and bad,”

and:

“We test you with evil and good as a trial,”

and:

“As for man, when his Lord tests him…”

and the verses that follow.

---

## Page 7658

Something may also be said to be from Allah, even though it is created, when it is uniquely attributable to Him, as with the signs of the prophets. Thus He said to Moses:

“These, then, are two proofs from your Lord.”

The transformation of the staff into a snake and the bringing forth of the hand white, without disease, are created by Allah. Yet they are from Him because through them He demonstrated and made clear the truthfulness of His prophet Moses. They constitute His confirmation and His testimony to Moses’ messengership and truthfulness. Thus they are from Allah in the same way that clear evidence and testimony are from Allah. These signs are not things that devils and soothsayers can perform. Similarly, one says, “This is a sign from so-and-so,” or “This is evidence from so-and-so,” even though it is not speech uttered by that person.

Moses himself called this clear evidence from Allah, saying:

“I have come to you with clear evidence from your Lord.”

His words, “with clear evidence from your Lord,” are like His words:

“These, then, are two proofs from your Lord.”

Here, this clear evidence is a created proof, sign, and indication that serves the same purpose as Allah’s testimony and His declaration through His speech. It is like the token a man sends with his agent to his household. Saʿīd ibn Jubayr said concerning such a sign: “It is like a signet ring that you send.” This is equivalent to saying, “Believe what he says,” or “Give him what he asks for.”

Thus the Quran and guidance are from Him, belonging to His speech, knowledge, and judgment, which subsist in Him and are uncreated. These signs point to that, just as His speech is written in

---

## Page 7659

copies of the Quran; what is meant, then, is the medium with which the speech is written and through which it is known. Allah Most High says:

Say, “If the sea were ink for the words of my Lord, the sea would be exhausted before the words of my Lord were exhausted, even if We brought its like as a supplement.”

For this reason, these miraculous signs possess a sanctity, such as the she-camel, the water that flowed from between the fingers of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and other such signs. And Allah, glorified be He, knows best.

---

## Page 7660

Section

Concerning His words, Most High:

“Is one who stands upon clear proof from his Lord, followed by a witness from Him”

and the verses that follow, up to His words:

“Will you not then take heed?”
