Allah, glorified be He, repeatedly describes the distinction between the people of truth and the people of falsehood, and the contrast and differences between them, encouraging the pursuit of bliss and warning against misery.

He begins the surah with this, saying:

“A Book whose verses have been perfected and then explained in detail, from One who is All-Wise, All-Aware.”

“Worship none but Allah. Indeed, I am a warner and a bearer of good tidings to you from Him.”

He thus describes him as a warner and a bearer of good tidings: a warner who warns the people of the Fire of punishment, and a bearer of good tidings who promises bliss to the people of truth.

He then describes the condition of the two groups in prosperity and adversity, saying:

“If We give man a taste of mercy from Us and then withdraw it from him, he becomes utterly despairing and ungrateful.”

“But if We give him a taste of prosperity after adversity has afflicted him, he will surely say, ‘My troubles have left me.’ Indeed, he is exultant and boastful.”

“Except those who are patient and do righteous deeds; for them there is forgiveness and a great reward.”

Then, after this, He recounts the stories of the prophets and the condition of those who followed them and those who rejected them

---

## Page 7661

—how the former found happiness in this world and the Hereafter, while the latter suffered misery in this world and the Hereafter. He recounts what befell them, up to His words:

“That is from the accounts of the towns which We relate to you,”

and on to His words:

“And that is a Day that will be witnessed.”

Then He describes the condition of those who attained happiness and those who suffered misery. He then says:

“Indeed, in that is a sign for whoever fears the punishment of the Hereafter.”

For someone might say, “The worst that befell these people was that they died, and everyone dies.” As for their all being destroyed, their homes becoming desolate, and their becoming cautionary examples, remembered with condemnation and cursed—only those who believe in the Hereafter fear this. For the curses and hatred directed against them by those who believe in the Hereafter—as happened to Pharaoh’s people—add to their punishment, just as the prophets’ honorable remembrance, people’s praise and prayers for them, and their following them add to their reward.

Thus, whoever takes what befell these people as evidence of the prophets’ truthfulness, and consequently believes in the Hereafter, fears its punishment, and this becomes a sign for him. But someone who does not believe in the Hereafter and thinks that those who die will not be resurrected may be unconcerned by such things. Someone who does not fear the Hereafter may nevertheless fear this; but everyone who fears the Hereafter is in this state, and it is a sign for him.

He concludes the surah with His words:

“And say to those who do not believe, ‘Act according to your position; we too are acting,’”

and the verses that follow to its end, just as He began it with His words:

“Worship none but Allah.”

Thus He mentions the worship of Allah alone and belief in the messengers. This, then, is Allah’s religion among the earlier generations

---

## Page 7662

and the later generations. Abū al-‘Āliyah said:

“Two questions will be put to the earlier and later generations: ‘What did you worship?’ and ‘How did you respond to the messengers?’”

For this reason, Allah says:

“On the Day He will call them and say, ‘How did you respond to the messengers?’”

And:

“Where are My partners whom you used to claim?”

The latter concerns associating partners with Allah in worship. These two principles are faith and Islam—submission to Allah. In the two rak‘ahs of Fajr, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, would sometimes recite the two surahs expressing pure devotion to Allah, and at other times the two verses concerning faith and Islam. He would recite the verse beginning:

“We believe in Allah and in what has been sent down to us.”

Its beginning concerns faith, and its ending concerns Islam. In the second rak‘ah, he would recite:

“Say, ‘O People of the Scripture, come to a common word between us and you: that we worship none but Allah.’”

Its beginning concerns devoting worship exclusively to Allah, and its ending concerns submission to Him.

Allah also says:

“Do not argue with the People of the Scripture except in the best manner, unless it be with those of them who do wrong. And say, ‘We believe in what has been sent down to us and what has been sent down to you. Our God and your God is One, and to Him we submit.’”

This verse mentions faith, and Islam at its close. He also says:

“Those who believed in Our signs and were Muslims.”

“Enter Paradise, you and your spouses, rejoicing.”

---

## Page 7663

Section

Concerning the words of the Most High:

“A Book whose verses have been perfected, then explained in detail.”

He explained it in detail after perfecting it, unlike someone who speaks without perfecting his words. Yet even well-crafted speech may contain things its speaker has not explained to others. He, glorified is He, perfected His Book, then explained it in detail and made it clear to His servants, as He says:

“Thus do We explain the signs in detail, so that the way of the criminals may become clear.”

He also says:

“We have certainly brought them a Book which We explained in detail with knowledge, as guidance and mercy for people who believe.”

He, glorified is He, explained it and sent it down to His servants with knowledge, unlike someone who speaks without knowledge.

He mentioned the proofs of Allah’s oneness and prophethood before mentioning the distinction between the people of truth and the people of falsehood, saying:

“Or do they say, ‘He has fabricated it’? Say, ‘Then produce ten fabricated surahs like it,’”

through to His words:

“Will you then submit?”

When He challenged them to produce ten fabricated surahs like it—they and all whom they could enlist besides Him—His challenge implied that no one apart from Allah could produce anything like it. As He says:

“Say, ‘If mankind and the jinn gathered together to produce the like of this Quran, they could not produce its like, even if they helped one another.’”

It thus becomes known that this is a distinctive attribute of one whom Allah has sent. And whatever is

---

## Page 7664

distinctive of a particular kind is evidence of that kind, for it necessarily entails it; everything that entails something else is evidence of it. Such are all the signs of the prophets, which are distinctive of their kind. This Quran is distinctive of their kind and, among them, of their Seal: no one else could have brought it. This was clear proof that Allah sent it down and that it was revealed with Allah’s knowledge. It is He who declared what it declares and commanded what it commands, as He says:

“But Allah bears witness to what He has revealed to you; He has revealed it with His knowledge,”

to the end of the verse.

Establishing the Messenger’s mission entails establishing Allah’s oneness and that none has the right to be worshipped but Allah: first, because the Messenger proclaimed this; and second, because none but Allah can produce this Quran, for some knowledge is known only to Allah. There are other forms of proof within it, as has been explained at length and pointed out elsewhere. This applies particularly to this surah, which contains clear exposition and demonstrations of inimitability whose full extent only Allah knows. It also contains admonitions, wisdom, encouragements, and warnings whose true worth only Allah can measure.

The “purpose here” is to discuss His words:

“Is one who stands upon clear evidence from his Lord and is followed by a witness”

The questioner asked about its interpretation and mentioned the considerable disagreement about it in the Quran commentaries, explaining that this disagreement leaves the student even less able to discern the intended meaning through which guidance and right direction are attained. Allah Most High sent down the Quran so that people would be guided by it, not so that they would dispute over it. Guidance can only be attained when its meanings are understood. Thus, if disagreement arises that contradicts those meanings and cannot be reconciled

---

## Page 7665

then the truth remains unknown, the verse and its meaning are not understood, and the guidance and knowledge intended by the revelation of the Book are not attained.

Abū ‘Abd al-Rahmān al-Sulamī said:

“Those who taught us the Quran—‘Uthmān ibn ‘Affān, ‘Abd Allāh ibn Mas‘ūd, and others—told us that whenever they learned ten verses from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, they would not proceed beyond them until they had learned the knowledge and practice they contained. They said, ‘Thus we learned the Quran, knowledge, and practice together.’”

Al-Hasan al-Basrī said:

“Allah has not revealed any verse without loving that people should know what it was revealed concerning and what was meant by it.”

Allah Most High has said:

“Do they not reflect upon the Quran?”

Reflecting upon speech is beneficial only when it is understood. He also says:

“Indeed, We have made it an Arabic Quran so that you may understand.”

The messengers explain to people what has been sent down to them from their Lord, and their duty is to convey the message clearly. People, in turn, are required to understand what the messengers convey. Such understanding encompasses both knowledge and action. Whoever knows good and evil but neither follows good nor guards against evil is not exercising sound reason. Thus, only someone who does what benefits him and avoids what harms him is considered reasonable. An insane person who cannot distinguish between the two may cast himself into peril and may flee from what benefits him.

---

## Page 7666

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked about Allah Most High’s words:

“As for those who are blessed, they will be in Paradise, abiding therein for as long as the heavens and the earth endure,”

and His words:

“On the Day when We roll up the heaven as a scroll is rolled up with its writings.”

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. Several groups of scholars have said that His words, “for as long as the heavens and the earth endure,” refer to the heaven and earth of Paradise. It is established in the two Sahīh collections that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When you ask Allah for Paradise, ask Him for Firdaws, for it is the highest and most central part of Paradise, and its roof is the Throne of the Most Merciful.”

Concerning Allah Most High’s words, “We have already written in the Psalms, after the Reminder, that My righteous servants shall inherit the earth,” some scholars have said that this means the earth of Paradise.

Accordingly, there is no contradiction between the rolling up of this heaven and the continued existence of the heaven that forms the roof of Paradise. In Arabic, anything overhead may be called a ‘heaven’; thus, clouds may be called a ‘heaven,’ as may a roof.

---

## Page 7667

Furthermore, even if the heavens are rolled up, become like molten metal, and are transformed from their present form, this does not entail their ceasing to exist or being destroyed. Rather, their underlying substance remains as they are transformed from one state to another, as Allah Most High says:

“On the Day when the earth is changed to another earth, and the heavens likewise.”

Even when they are changed, there will still be an enduring heaven and an enduring earth. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 7668

Surah Yūsuf

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section

Concerning the words of Joseph—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—when the wife of al-‘Azīz said to him:

“‘Come to me.’ He said, ‘Allah forbid! He is my master, who has treated me well. Indeed, wrongdoers never prosper.’”

According to the more correct of the two views, “my master” here refers to her husband, Joseph’s master, who bought him in Egypt and said to his wife:

“Treat him well. Perhaps he may benefit us, or we may adopt him as a son.”

Allah Most High said:

“Thus We established Joseph in the land, so that We might teach him the interpretation of dreams. Allah prevails in His purpose, but most people do not know.”

Since he had instructed his wife concerning Joseph, saying, “Treat him well,” Joseph said, “He is my master, who has treated me well.” This is why he said, “Indeed, wrongdoers never prosper.” The pronoun in “He” refers to someone known to both of them: her master.

---

## Page 7669

As for the words of Allah Most High:

“Had he not seen his Lord’s proof,”

this is a statement from Allah Most High that he saw his Lord’s proof. His Lord is Allah, as he said to his two fellow prisoners:

“That is part of what my Lord has taught me. Indeed, I have abandoned the religion of a people who do not believe in Allah.”

His words, “my master,” parallel his words to the man who had the dream:

“Mention me to your master.”

Allah Most High said:

“But Satan made him forget the remembrance of his lord.”

It has been said that Joseph was made to forget the remembrance of his Lord when he said, “Mention me to your master.”

It has also been said that, rather, Satan made the one of the two who was released forget to mention Joseph to his master. This is the correct interpretation, for it corresponds to his words, “Mention me to your master.” Allah Most High said, “But Satan made him forget the remembrance of his lord.” A pronoun refers to the nearest antecedent unless there is evidence to the contrary. Moreover, Joseph did not forget the remembrance of his Lord; rather, he continued to remember Him.

Before interpreting their dreams, he had called them both to believe in his Lord, saying to them:

“O my two fellow prisoners! Are separate lords better, or Allah, the One, the All-Subduing?

“You worship nothing besides Him but names you and your forefathers have devised, for which Allah has sent down no authority. Judgment belongs to Allah alone. He has commanded that you worship none but Him. That is the upright religion, but most people do not know.”

Before that, he had said to them, “No food will come to you as provision”—meaning, in a dream—“unless

---

## Page 7670

I tell you its interpretation before it comes to you”—meaning the interpretation.

“That is part of what my Lord has taught me. Indeed, I have abandoned the religion of a people who do not believe in Allah and who deny the Hereafter.

“And I have followed the religion of my forefathers, Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob. It is not for us to associate anything with Allah. That is part of Allah’s favour upon us and upon mankind, but most people are ungrateful.”

In this way he remembers his Lord, Mighty and Majestic, for this is among the things his Lord taught him because he abandoned the religion of a polytheistic people who did not believe in Allah—even though they acknowledged the Creator—and did not believe in the Hereafter. He followed the religion of his forefathers, the leaders of the believers—whom Allah made leaders calling others by His command—Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob. Thus, he remembered his Lord and then called the two men to believe in his Lord.

After this, he interpreted the dream, saying:

“O my two fellow prisoners! As for one of you, he will serve his master wine,”

and so on to the end of the verse. Then, when he had finished interpreting the dream:

“And he said to the one of the two whom he thought would be released, ‘Mention me to your master.’”

How, then, could Satan have made Joseph forget the remembrance of his Lord? Rather, Satan made the man who was released forget the mention connected with his master—that is, the mention related and ascribed to his master, namely mentioning Joseph in his presence.

Those who advanced that interpretation said: “It would have been better for him to rely on Allah and not say, ‘Mention me to your master.’ When he forgot to rely on his Lord, he was punished by remaining in prison for several years.”

The response is that nothing in his words,

“Mention me to your master,”

contradicts reliance on Allah. Indeed, Joseph had said:

“Judgment belongs to Allah alone.”

Likewise, his father’s words,

“Do not enter through one gate, but enter through separate gates,”

did not contradict his reliance on Allah. Rather, he said:

---

## Page 7671

“I cannot protect you at all against Allah. Judgment belongs to Allah alone. In Him I have placed my trust, and in Him let those who trust place their trust.”

Furthermore, Allah Himself testified that Joseph was among His sincere servants. A sincere servant cannot remain sincere while placing his trust in anyone other than Allah, for that is polytheism. Joseph associated no partners with Allah, whether in his worship or in his reliance. Indeed, he relied on his Lord concerning his own conduct when he said:

“Unless You turn their plotting away from me, I may incline towards them and become one of the ignorant.”

How, then, could he fail to rely on Him concerning the actions of His servants?

His words, “Mention me to your master,” are like his words to the king:

“Appoint me over the storehouses of the land; I am indeed a knowledgeable guardian.”

His request for authority to serve a religious benefit did not contradict reliance on Allah, nor did it constitute the prohibited seeking of leadership. How, then, could his words to the young man, “Mention me to your master,” contradict reliance on Allah? They were merely a request to inform the king about him, so that he would learn of his circumstances and the truth would become clear.

Joseph was among the most steadfast of people. Thus, when he was summoned, and “the king said, ‘Bring him to me,’” he said:

“Return to your master and ask him about the women who cut their hands. Surely my Lord knows their plotting well.”

Joseph remembered his Lord in this situation just as he had remembered Him in the earlier one. He also said:

“Return to your master and ask him about the women.”

Thus, there was nothing in his saying to him, “Mention me

---

## Page 7672

to your master,” that involved neglecting a duty or committing a forbidden act for which Allah would punish him by leaving him in prison for several years. They had already resolved, before this, to imprison him for a time, wronging him despite knowing that he was innocent of the sin.

Allah Most High said:

“Then, after seeing the signs, they decided to imprison him for a time.”

His remaining in prison was an honor bestowed upon him by Allah, so that his patience and piety would thereby be perfected. It was through patience and piety that he attained what he attained. Hence he said:

“I am Joseph, and this is my brother. Allah has indeed favored us. Whoever fears Allah and remains patient—surely Allah does not let the reward of those who do good go to waste.”

Had he not remained patient and feared Allah, but instead obeyed them in what they demanded of him out of dread of imprisonment, he would not have attained this patience and piety. By unanimous agreement, he would have missed what was better.

The scholars, however, differed over whether a man can be coerced into illicit sexual intercourse, holding two views.

One view is that this is not possible, as held by Ahmad ibn Hanbal, Abū Hanīfa, and others. They argued that coercion prevents an erection.

The second view is that it is possible. This is the view of Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, Ibn ʿAqīl, and others among Ahmad’s followers, because coercion does not preclude an erection. Coercion does not preclude the act being chosen; rather, the person under coercion chooses to avert the greater of two evils by accepting

---

## Page 7673

the lesser.

Furthermore, an erection can occur without any action on his part. Indeed, he may be bound and laid down, and a woman may touch him intimately, causing an erection, and then insert his penis into herself. According to the first view, what she demanded of him would not have been permissible for him under any circumstances. According to the second view, it may be argued that imprisonment does not constitute coercion that makes illicit intercourse permissible, unlike a situation in which he believes it most likely that they will kill him or destroy one of his limbs. The disagreement concerns only this latter situation, and they did not go to such lengths with him. Even if it were said that coercion made this permissible for him, he would nevertheless have forfeited what was better.

Furthermore, coercion occurs only the first time; thereafter, he engages in intercourse and retains desire and a willingness to commit the indecent act.

Those who say that coercion into illicit intercourse is inconceivable maintain that a distinction must be drawn between someone who takes no action himself—such as a man who is bound—and someone who actively participates. Likewise, if a woman is laid down and bound, and the indecent act is committed against her, she incurs no sin by unanimous agreement. If, however, she is coerced into engaging in illicit intercourse, there are two views, both reported from Ahmad. The majority, however, hold that she incurs no sin. This is supported by the words of Allah Most High:

“If anyone compels them, then Allah, after they have been compelled, is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

These scholars say that the woman’s participation does not require an erection, so her case is comparable to being coerced into drinking wine, unlike the man’s participation. A fuller discussion of this belongs elsewhere.

---

## Page 7674

The point is that Joseph committed no sin in the account Allah has related of him. Whenever Allah, glorified is He, mentions a sin committed by any prophet, He also mentions that prophet’s seeking forgiveness for it. Yet He mentions no instance of Joseph seeking forgiveness for this statement, just as He mentions none for any acts leading to the indecent deed. It is therefore clear that Joseph committed no sin in either matter. Rather, he entertained an intention which he abandoned for Allah’s sake, and was consequently credited with a good deed, as has been explained fully elsewhere.

As for sins expiated through trials, the person who committed them receives his recompense through afflictions that expiate them, as the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“No illness, fatigue, worry, sorrow, distress or harm afflicts a believer without Allah thereby expiating his sins.”

And when “Allah Most High revealed this verse: ‘Whoever does evil will be recompensed for it,’ Abū Bakr said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, this is a back-breaking blow! Which of us has not done evil?’ He replied, ‘Do you not grieve? Do you not grow weary? Do you not suffer hardship? These are among the things through which you receive your recompense.’”

It thus becomes clear that His words, “But Satan made him forget the mention of his master,” mean that the young man was made to forget the mention of his master—that is, to mention this matter to his master. He was made to forget Joseph’s mention of his master. A verbal noun may stand in a genitive construction with either the agent or the object of the action. Joseph had mentioned the young man’s master, and the young man was made to forget Joseph’s mention of his master. Satan made him forget to mention his master in this particular connection; for although he served his master wine, this particular mention might not occur to him. And he was made to forget

---

## Page 7675

by Satan to remind his master and make him remember. When Joseph said, “Mention me,” he instructed him to make his master remember, but Satan made him forget to do so. Making his master remember means causing him to recall something; thus Satan made him forget to bring Joseph to his master’s mind. The word dhikr (mention or remembrance) is both a verbal noun and a noun in its own right. It may therefore enter a genitive construction in its capacity as a noun, encompassing all these meanings: that is, Satan made him forget the mention relating to his master and expressed in connection with him.

Further evidence that it was the young man, not Joseph, who forgot his master is Allah’s subsequent statement:

“But the one of the two who had been released, remembering after a long time, said, ‘I will inform you of its interpretation, so send me.’”

His words, “remembering after a long time,” show that he had forgotten and then remembered.

If it is said:

There is no doubt that Joseph used the word rabb (lord) for a master when he said, “Mention me to your master,” and “Return to your master,” and in similar statements. This was permissible under his revealed law, just as his parents and brothers were permitted to prostrate before him, and a thief could be taken as a slave under his law, although this has been abrogated in the law of Muhammad, peace and blessings be upon him.

As for his words, “He is my lord, who has treated me well,” if he meant the master, there is no blame upon him. Yet it is known that refraining from the indecent deed out of fear of Allah is obligatory, even if her master approved of it. Joseph, peace be upon him, refrained from it out of fear of Allah.

“She certainly desired him, and he would have desired her

---

## Page 7676

had he not seen the proof of his Lord.” Allah Most High says:

“Thus it was, so that We might avert from him evil and immorality. Indeed, he was one of Our chosen servants.”

Joseph also said:

“My Lord, prison is dearer to me than what they are calling me to. Unless You turn their plotting away from me, I may incline towards them and become one of the ignorant.”

“So his Lord answered him and turned their plotting away from him. Indeed, He is the All-Hearing, the All-Knowing.”

This shows that his fear of Allah was sufficient to restrain him from the indecent deed, even if people approved of it. He had also prayed to his Lord, Almighty and Majestic, to turn their plotting away from him.

His words, “Prison is dearer to me than what they are calling me to,” use the masculine plural, whereas “their plotting” uses the feminine plural. He did not use the feminine plural in “what they are calling me to.” This indicates a distinction between the two: among those urging him, alongside the women, to commit the indecent deed with that woman was a man. The only such man there was her husband. Her husband had little or no protective jealousy; he loved his wife and did her bidding. Thus, when he discovered her attempt to seduce Joseph, he said:

“Joseph, disregard this. And you, seek forgiveness for your sin; indeed, you have been one of the sinners.”

He neither punished her nor separated her from Joseph to prevent her from attempting to seduce him. Out of love for his wife, he instructed Joseph not to tell anyone what had happened. Had he possessed any protective jealousy, he would have punished her.

Nevertheless, the story spread, and people learned of it from someone other than Joseph. Eventually, the women in the city were discussing it, saying that she was attempting to seduce her young servant. Despite this:

“She sent for them, prepared a place for them to recline, and gave

---

## Page 7677

each of them a knife.” She ordered Joseph to appear before them so that they would excuse her attempts to seduce him, saying to them:

“That is the one you blamed me for. I did indeed try to seduce him, but he firmly resisted. If he does not do what I command him, he will surely be imprisoned and be among the humiliated.”

This shows that she remained able to attempt to seduce him and be alone with him, despite her husband’s knowledge of what had happened. This is an extreme instance of indifference to one’s wife’s chastity. Moreover, when Joseph was imprisoned, it was at her command. Yet she could not have had him imprisoned without her husband’s order, so it was her husband who imprisoned him.

It is reported that she said, “This Copt has violated my honour,” whereupon he imprisoned him. He imprisoned him for the woman’s sake, helping her attain what she wanted because of his indifference to her chastity and his lack of protective jealousy. He was thus among those who called Joseph to commit the indecent deed.

It is therefore clear that Joseph did not refrain from the indecent deed out of regard for her husband or fear of him. Indeed, it is known with certainty that he did not fear him, and that had Joseph granted her what she sought, her husband would not have known. Even had he known, perhaps he would not have objected: he knew of her attempts at seduction and their being alone together—which generally leads to such an act—yet he did not object. Even supposing that he had contemplated punishing Joseph, she held sway over her husband and dominated him.

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“I have never seen anyone deficient in reason and religion more capable of robbing a resolute man of his wits than one of you.”

When they pressed him to reconsider having al-Siddīq lead the prayer, he said:

“You are indeed just like the women in Joseph’s story.”

And when al-Aʿshā recited to him

---

## Page 7678

“And they are the worst conquerors of those they conquer,”

he asked him to repeat it, and said:

“And they are the worst conquerors of those they conquer.”

How, then, could she not dominate such a husband and prevent him from punishing Joseph? People have known many men dominated by their wives—Tatar women and others. A wife may harbour an illicit desire for her husband’s young male servant or her own, and do whatever she wishes with him. If her husband wants to investigate or punish him, she prevents him and resists him. Indeed, she humiliates him and unleashes all manner of trouble upon him, through her own actions, her family and her retinue, by demanding her dower, and by other means, until the man wishes only to escape her on even terms. This happens even when the man possesses protective jealousy; how much more so when that jealousy is weak!

All this shows that what prompted Joseph to refrain from the indecent deed was fear of Allah, not fear of his master. Thus he said:

“My lord has indeed treated me well. Surely wrongdoers never prosper.”

It may be said: This illustrates Joseph’s virtues, his regard for the rights of Allah and of His creatures, and his repelling evil in the best manner. For adultery with another man’s wife violates two rights, each independently sufficient to make it forbidden. The indecent deed is forbidden because of Allah’s right, even if the husband consents; and wronging the husband with respect to his wife is forbidden because of his right. Thus, even if repentance absolves the offender of violating Allah’s right, it does not extinguish the husband’s right concerning his wife. Likewise, if someone wronged him, took his wealth, and repented of violating Allah’s right, that would not extinguish

---

## Page 7679

the wronged person’s right on that account. Thus, if a man’s wife commits adultery, he may accuse her of it, engage with her in the procedure of mutual imprecation, and seek to have her punished by stoning. A man other than her husband, by contrast, may not accuse her and cannot invoke mutual imprecation; rather, he incurs the prescribed punishment if he fails to produce four witnesses. Corrupting a woman’s relationship with her husband is among the gravest wrongs against him, and he considers it worse than taking his wealth. Hence, scholars unanimously agree that he may kill the other man to repel him from her if he cannot be repelled except by killing him. According to the stronger of the two views, he may kill him even if he could be repelled by lesser means. This is illustrated by the account of ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb, may Allah be pleased with him: a man came to him holding a bloodstained sword and explained that he had found another man gratifying himself between his wife’s thighs and had struck him with the sword. ʿUmar approved of what he had done, commended him, and accepted his statement that he had killed him for that reason, since the evidence supported his account.

This is comparable to someone peering into a man’s house: the householder may put out his eye immediately, without first warning him. This is the sounder of the two views, as established in the two Sahīh collections, where the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“If a man peered into your house and you put out his eye, you would incur no liability.”

He gave the same ruling concerning someone who bit another person’s hand, whereupon the latter pulled his hand away and the biter’s teeth were dislodged. This is the position of the jurists of the hadith tradition and most of the early generations. There is disagreement on both questions, but this is not the place to discuss it. The point is that someone who commits adultery with another man’s wife wrongs her husband, and the husband has a claim against him. For this reason, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, stated: “Whoever

---

## Page 7680

commits adultery with the wife of a man fighting in Allah’s cause, that man will be allowed, on the Day of Resurrection, to take as many of the adulterer’s good deeds as he wishes.”

The two Sahīh collections also relate that Ibn Masʿūd said:

“I asked, ‘O Messenger of Allah, which sin is greatest?’ He replied, ‘To set up a rival to Allah, although He created you.’ I asked, ‘Then which?’ He replied, ‘To kill your child for fear that he will share your food.’ I asked, ‘Then which?’ He replied, ‘To commit adultery with your neighbour’s wife.’”

He specifically mentioned adultery with a neighbour’s wife, showing that the husband has a right in this matter. Wronging a neighbour is graver because people need to live alongside one another.

And if it is said: “The woman’s husband may be unable to guard against this, and a neighbour has a claim upon him beyond that of a stranger—how much worse, then, if he wrongs him through his wife! Neighbours trust one another, so this involves greater injustice than other cases. A neighbour is obliged to protect the other man’s wife from others; how, then, can he himself corrupt her?”

Since adultery with a married woman is prohibited on two grounds, each independently sufficient to establish its prohibition—like the flesh of a dead pig—Joseph explained his refusal by invoking the husband’s right, although either ground alone would have restrained him. His invoking the husband’s right had several benefits.

One is that this is a restraint the woman understands and accepts as an excuse for his refusal, unlike Allah Most High’s right, since she does not know Allah’s punishment for this.

Another is that this may deter the woman, leading her to respect her husband’s right, either

---

## Page 7681

out of fear or out of regard for his rights. If a slave refrains from this out of regard for his master’s rights, the woman has even greater reason to do so, for she would be betraying the very purpose of her marriage. By contrast, what is required of the slave is service, and his sexual misconduct is comparable to the woman’s stealing from her husband’s wealth.

Another benefit is that this obstacle leaves her no hope of attaining him, whether through marriage or illicit intercourse. An unmarried woman, by contrast, could hope to marry him lawfully.

Another is that, had he explained his refusal merely by saying that it was adultery, she might have sought to leave her husband and marry him. Such a course is forbidden solely on account of the husband’s rights. Thus, if a man willingly divorces his wife, another man may marry her—even if he divorces her so that the other man can marry her. This is what Saʿd ibn al-Rabīʿ offered ʿAbd al-Rahmān ibn ʿAwf, saying: “I have two wives. Choose whichever you wish, so that I may divorce her and you may marry her.” Without the husband’s consent, however, this is not lawful. As related in al-Musnad, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“He is not one of us who turns a woman against her husband or a slave against his masters.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, also forbade a man to propose marriage over his brother’s proposal or to intervene in his brother’s purchase negotiations. If, after a proposal but before the marriage contract, it is unlawful for another man to seek to marry the woman, how much more so after the contract, consummation and married life together!

Had he explained his refusal by saying that this was forbidden adultery, she might have hoped to leave her husband and marry him, for women’s scheming is indeed great; things of this kind have happened. Thus, when he invoked the right of

---

## Page 7682

his master and said:

“He is my master, who has treated me well,”

she lost hope of that and understood that he respected her husband’s rights and would never seek to take his wife from him. Even if, despite this, the husband were to consent to the sexual immorality and make his wife available, this would not make intercourse with her lawful, on account of Allah’s rights and the husband’s own rights as well. A person is not entitled to waive every right he possesses, nor does his waiver necessarily extinguish it. This applies only to what he is permitted to give freely: that which causes him no harm to give, such as surplus wealth or some benefit he provides.

As for what he is not entitled to give, his permission does not make it lawful. An example would be his saying to someone: “Teach me sorcery, disbelief, and soothsaying; I release you from any claim for leading me astray.” Or he might say: “Sell me into slavery and keep the price paid for me; I release you from any claim over that.”

Likewise, if he says, “Commit sexual immorality with me, my son, my wife, or my slave women,” his permission does not waive his rights in this matter, for he is not entitled to offer such things. It is well known that Allah punishes her for sexual immorality even if both consent to it. The point, however, is that this also involves a wrong against this person which his permission does not remove, just as he is wronged if someone makes him a disbeliever or a slave. Committing sexual immorality with him or his family causes him harm which he has no authority to permit, just like the harm he suffers through becoming a disbeliever.

It is as though he were to say to someone: “Deprive me of my sanity; I release you from any claim over that.” A person has no authority to surrender this; rather, he is forbidden to do so, just as someone lacking sound judgment is prevented from disposing of his wealth or waiving his rights. The same applies to an insane person and a minor: such people are placed under legal restrictions to protect their own rights.

---

## Page 7683

For this reason, if a minor or someone lacking sound judgment permits another person to take his wealth, that person is not entitled to do so. Anyone who permits another to make him a disbeliever, drive him insane, render him effeminate, or commit sexual immorality with him and his family is among the most foolish of people. The same applies to usury: even if the person charged usury consents, being an adult of sound judgment, it remains unlawful because of the wrong done to him. He therefore has the right to demand repayment of the excess taken from him and to give the lender only his principal, even though he paid the excess voluntarily. If the prohibition concerned solely the right of Allah Most High, it would lapse with his consent. And if his own right were extinguished by his waiving it, he would not be entitled to reclaim the excess.

The prohibition against killing oneself is even more severe than the prohibition against killing another. If someone says to another, “Kill me,” that person acquires no greater authority over him than he himself possesses over his own life. Thus, on the Day of Resurrection, people will complain of the wrongs done to them by their leaders, even though those leaders did not compel them to disbelieve; rather, they disbelieved of their own choice.

Allah Most High says:

“On the Day their faces are turned about in the Fire, they will say, ‘If only we had obeyed Allah and obeyed the Messenger!’”

“And they will say, ‘Our Lord, we obeyed our masters and our leaders, and they led us astray from the way.’”

“‘Our Lord, give them double punishment and curse them with a mighty curse.’”

He also says:

“Until, when they have all gathered together in it, the last of them will say of the first, ‘Our Lord, these led us astray, so give them double punishment in the Fire.’ He will say, ‘Each will have double, but you do not know.’”

Allah Most High also says:

“Those who disbelieve will say, ‘Our Lord, show us those two who led us astray from among the jinn and mankind, so that we may put them beneath our feet and they may be among the lowest.’”

Likewise, people curse Satan even though he did not compel them to sin;

---

## Page 7684

rather, they sinned of their own free choice.

If it is said:

“These people say to the devils among mankind and the jinn, ‘We did not know that this would harm us. Rather, you made it appealing and attractive to us until we did it, while we were ignorant of the matter.’”

The reply is:

As we know, no weight is given to the consent or permission of someone who is unaware of the harm an act will cause him. Consent and permission are valid only when given by someone who understands what he is permitting and consenting to. A person consents to something whose harm to him outweighs its benefit only because of his ignorance; otherwise, the soul naturally recoils from such harm. Thus, someone who buys a defective item, an item whose condition has been misrepresented, or an item whose price is unknown, without knowing its true circumstances, has not genuinely consented to it; rather, he is entitled to rescind the transaction afterward. Likewise, no one consents to disbelief, insanity, or sexual immorality involving his family unless he is unaware of the harm these entail for him. If he permits them, his right is not thereby extinguished; rather, he remains a victim of wrongdoing. Even if he were to say, “I know what punishment these entail, and I consent to it,” that would be a lie. Indeed, he is among those most ignorant of what they are saying.

For this reason, if someone utters words whose meaning he does not understand and says, “I intended whatever legal effect they have before Allah,” this is not valid according to the stronger of the two views. An example is someone saying “bihashimi” without knowing what it means, or saying, “You are divorced if you enter the house,” and intending its legal effect

---

## Page 7685

as determined by the Arabic wording, although he does not understand it. Intention, purpose, and consent all depend on knowledge. A person cannot consent to what he does not know unless he would also consent to it with knowledge. Anyone who consents to becoming a disbeliever, losing his sanity, or having sexual immorality committed with him and his family does not understand the harm this entails for him. Rather, he is foolish, and no weight is given to his consent or permission. He still has a claim against whoever wronged him and did this to him, distinct from Allah’s right, although his claim is less than that of someone who objects and resists. For this reason, Joseph, peace be upon him, said:

“Indeed, he is my master, who has treated me well. Indeed, wrongdoers will not succeed.”

He means: “If I corrupt his wife, I would be a wrongdoer in any event. This is no way to repay his kindness to me.”

When people cooperate in sin and transgression, they come to hate one another, even if they acted by mutual consent. Tāwūs said:

“No two men ever come together for anything other than Allah’s sake without parting in mutual hatred.”

Abraham, Allah’s close friend, peace be upon him, said:

“You have taken idols besides Allah only as a bond of affection among yourselves in the life of this world. Then, on the Day of Resurrection, you will disown one another and curse one another. Your refuge will be the Fire, and you will have no helpers.”

These people do not disown and curse one another merely because the other disobeyed Allah, but because of the harm each suffered through the other’s participation and assistance. The Most High said of the owners of the garden that had become like a barren wasteland:

“Then they turned to one another, blaming each other.”

That is, they reproached one another.

And He said:

“Close friends, on that Day,

---

## Page 7686

will be enemies to one another, except the righteous.”

When a close friendship does not serve the interests of both parties, it ends in enmity. It serves their interests only when it is for Allah’s sake. Even if each helps the other obtain what he seeks, and seeks the other’s assistance with his consent, this mutual consent carries no weight. Rather, it turns into mutual hatred, enmity, and cursing. Each says to the other: “Were it not for you, I would not have done this on my own. My ruin was brought about by both you and me.”

The Lord does not forbid them to hate, oppose, and curse one another. Were either of them wronging the other by doing so, he would have been forbidden to do it. Each says to the other: “You brought me into this to satisfy your own desire.” This is like two people who commit fornication together: each says to the other, “You did this with me to satisfy your own desire. Had you refused, I would not have done it.” Yet each has the same claim against the other, so their claims balance one another. Thus, when one pursues and solicits the other more insistently, the other complains more of being wronged and curses that person more. If their pursuit is equal, they stand on equal terms.

When a husband acquiesces in his wife’s infidelity, he does so only to please the man or the woman, seeking some other end of his own. For example, he may love her, while she will remain with him only on these terms. He therefore says to the man committing adultery with her: “You corrupted my wife to satisfy your own desire, whereas I consented only to satisfy hers. So when you corrupted my wife and wronged me,

---

## Page 7687

you did to me what you did.”

Another example is that, had he said, “I fear that Allah will punish me,” or something similar, she would have said: “You are only setting aside my wishes for the sake of your own wish to be saved. I am your mistress, so you ought to put my wishes before your own.” But when he said:

“Indeed, he is my master, who has treated me well,”

he based his refusal on his master’s rights, which both he and she were obliged to respect.

Section

Joseph’s words contain two lessons:

“He said, ‘My Lord, prison is dearer to me than what they invite me to. Unless You turn their plotting away from me, I may incline toward them and become one of the ignorant.’”

The first is choosing imprisonment and affliction over sins and acts of disobedience. The second is asking Allah and supplicating Him to keep the heart firm upon His religion and turn it toward obedience to Him. For unless He keeps the heart firm, it will incline toward those who urge it to sin and become one of the ignorant. This entails trusting in Allah and seeking His help to keep the heart firm in faith and obedience. It also entails patiently enduring the trials, afflictions, and harm that result from remaining steadfast in faith and obedience.

---

## Page 7688

This is like the words of Moses, peace be upon him, to his people:

“Seek Allah’s help and be patient. Indeed, the earth belongs to Allah; He grants it as an inheritance to whomever He wills among His servants. The final outcome belongs to the God-fearing.”

When Pharaoh said:

“We will kill their sons and spare their women. Indeed, we have overwhelming power over them,”

“Moses said to his people, ‘Seek Allah’s help and be patient. Indeed, the earth belongs to Allah; He grants it as an inheritance to whomever He wills among His servants. The final outcome belongs to the God-fearing.’”

Likewise, Allah says:

“As for those who emigrated for Allah’s sake after suffering oppression, We will surely provide them with a good home in this world. Yet the reward of the Hereafter is greater, if only they knew.”

“Those who are patient and put their trust in their Lord.”

Another example is the statement of Joseph, peace be upon him:

“Indeed, Allah does not let the reward of those who do good go to waste.”

This parallels His words:

“If you are patient and mindful of Allah, their plotting will not harm you at all.”

And His words:

“If you are patient and mindful of Allah, that is indeed a matter requiring firm resolve.”

And His words:

“Yes, if you are patient and mindful of Allah, and they come upon you suddenly, your Lord will reinforce you with five thousand angels bearing distinguishing marks.”

Thus, one must be mindful of Allah by doing what He commands and patiently endure what He decrees, as Joseph, peace be upon him, did. He feared Allah by abstaining from sexual immorality and patiently endured the harm they inflicted on him through their attempts to seduce him and their imprisonment of him. He sought Allah’s help and supplicated Him to keep him steadfast in chastity. He trusted Him to turn the women’s plotting away from him and patiently endured imprisonment.

---

## Page 7689

This is as Allah Most High says:

“Among people are those who say, ‘We believe in Allah,’ but when they suffer harm for Allah’s sake, they regard people’s persecution as though it were Allah’s punishment.”

And as He says:

“Among people are those who worship Allah on the fringe: if good comes their way, they are content with it, but if a trial befalls them, they turn back on their faces, losing both this world and the Hereafter. That is the clear loss.”

“They invoke besides Allah what can neither harm nor benefit them. That is straying far indeed.”

“They invoke those whose harm is closer than their benefit. What an evil protector and what an evil companion!”

Everyone in this world must inevitably suffer harm. If a person will not patiently endure harm in obedience to Allah, but chooses disobedience instead, the evil that befalls him will be far greater than what he fled from.

“Among them are those who say, ‘Give me leave, and do not put me to trial.’ Surely, into trial they have fallen!”

Whoever chooses to endure humiliation and harm in obedience to Allah rather than enjoy honor and prestige in disobedience to Him—as Joseph, peace be upon him, and other prophets and righteous people did—will have a favorable outcome in this world and the Hereafter. The harm he suffered will turn into bliss and joy, just as the pleasure sinners derive from their sins will turn into sorrow and ruin.

Joseph, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, feared to sin against Allah, but did not fear people’s harming or imprisoning him for obeying Allah. Rather, he preferred imprisonment and harm accompanied by obedience to honor, prestige, the gratification of desires, and the attainment of authority and wealth accompanied by disobedience. For had he yielded to the wife of al-‘Azīz, he would have satisfied his desire, and the woman would have honored him with wealth and authority

---

## Page 7690

and her husband was under her sway. Yet Joseph chose humiliation, imprisonment, abstaining from desire, and forgoing wealth and authority in obedience to Allah over honor, authority, wealth, and the gratification of desire in disobedience to Him. Rather, he put fear of the Creator before fear of any created being, even if that person harmed him through imprisonment and false accusations. She did indeed lie about him, claiming that he had tried to seduce her, and then had him imprisoned.

It has been said that she told her husband, “He has dishonored me.” She could not tell him, “He tried to seduce me,” because her husband already knew what had happened. Instead, she fabricated an accusation against Joseph that her husband would believe: that he had dishonored her by publicizing her conduct. This was a lie against Joseph, for he had said nothing about her. She lied both at the outset and afterward: she falsely accused him of soliciting indecency, then falsely accused him of publicizing it. Yet she herself had solicited it and made it public, for she said to the women:

“This is the one you blamed me for. I did try to seduce him, but he firmly resisted.”

This was the utmost publicity for her own indecency; she did not conceal her conduct. Women are the most given to spreading reports of such things, and even before hearing her words, they had already said in the city:

“The wife of al-‘Azīz is trying to seduce her young servant.”

How much more so, then, once she had admitted it and sought to have their blame lifted from her?

---

## Page 7691

It has also been said that they helped her in her attempt to seduce him and reproached her for holding back. This is indicated by his words:

“Unless You turn their scheming away from me, I will incline toward them.”

And his words:

“Return to your master and ask him about the women who cut their hands. Surely my Lord knows their scheming well.”

This indicates that there was scheming on their part. The king had said to them:

“‘What was your purpose when you tried to seduce Joseph?’ They replied, ‘Allah forbid! We know of no evil on his part.’ The wife of al-‘Azīz said, ‘Now the truth has come to light. I tried to seduce him, and he is indeed telling the truth.’”

They were not trying to seduce him for themselves, since that was not possible while he was living in the woman’s house under her authority. Rather, they may have helped her obtain what she wanted.

If this is so concerning sexual immorality, other sins are graver still, such as committing grave injustice against people by killing someone whose life is inviolable, associating partners with Allah, or speaking about Allah without knowledge. Allah Most High says:

“Say, ‘My Lord has only forbidden shameful deeds, whether open or secret, sin, unjust aggression, associating with Allah that for which He has sent down no authority, and saying about Allah what you do not know.’”

These are the categories of prohibited acts that are never permissible under any circumstances or in any revealed law. Other acts, even if forbidden in one situation, may be permissible in another.

---

## Page 7692

Section

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, chose confinement for himself and his family in the ravine of Banū Hāshim for several years, during which they neither bought nor sold, while their children cried out from hunger. Their own people and others had shunned and forsaken them. This was more perfect than Joseph’s situation, peace be upon him. For these people were calling upon the Messenger to associate partners with Allah and to speak falsely about Him—to say, “He did not send me, nor did He forbid associating partners with Him.”

Allah Most High has said:

“They almost tempted you away from what We have revealed to you, so that you would fabricate something else against Us; then they would certainly have taken you as a close friend.”

“Had We not made you steadfast, you would almost have inclined toward them a little.”

“Then We would have made you taste double punishment in life and double punishment in death, and you would have found no one to help you against Us.”

“They almost drove you from the land to expel you from it; then they would have remained after you only a little while.”

“That was Our established way with the messengers We sent before you, and you will find no change in Our way.”

The lies these people told about the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, were graver than the lies told about Joseph. For they said that he was a sorcerer, that he was a soothsayer, that he was a madman, and that he was

---

## Page 7693

a fabricator. Each of these charges is graver than adultery or slanderous accusations of unchastity, especially adultery committed in secret, unknown to anyone. Joseph was falsely accused of committing adultery, of accusing her of unchastity, and of spreading reports of her indecency. Thus, the lies told about the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, were graver than those told about Joseph.

The same applies to the lies told about the messengers of firm resolve, such as Noah and Moses, when one of them is described as a madman and a liar who fabricates lies against Allah.

The harm that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his Companions suffered at the hands of the polytheists was greater than mere confinement. Joseph was imprisoned and otherwise left alone, whereas the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his Companions suffered verbal and physical abuse, besides being prevented from going about their usual affairs. This is what confinement means: its purpose is not to make someone reside in a prison, but to prevent him from going about his usual affairs. Neither the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, nor Abū Bakr maintained a prison. Rather, the first to establish a prison was ʿUmar. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would “hand a debtor over to his creditor and say, ‘What has your captive done?’” He would thus make the debtor a captive in his creditor’s custody until he paid him what he was owed. This is the purpose of confinement.

The Companions—may Allah be pleased with them—were prevented from going about their affairs in Mecca as a means of harming them, until many of them left for Abyssinia. They chose to live among those Christians under a just king rather than remain among their own people. Those who remained

---

## Page 7694

were also driven from their homes and deprived of their property, besides the other harm inflicted on them. Their persecutors even killed some of them, beat others, denied others what they needed, and would place a boulder on one of them on the scorching ground of Mecca, among other forms of abuse.

Likewise, a believer from the community of Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, chooses to suffer harm while obeying Allah rather than receive honour while disobeying Him. Ahmad ibn Hanbal, for example, chose shackles, imprisonment, and beating rather than agree with the ruler and his soldiers to speak falsely about Allah concerning His speech, or to say what he did not know. They would put forward statements which he knew contradicted the Quran and Sunnah and were therefore false, as well as statements whose meaning required clarification.

Imam Ahmad would say to them, “I do not know what this means.” Thus, he would neither agree to speak falsely about Allah nor to say about Allah what he did not know.

---

## Page 7695

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—also said, after some discussion: (1)

“[One of them contemplates] (2) committing a sin, but remembers his standing before Allah and abandons it. Thus Joseph was among those who feared standing before their Lord and restrained themselves from desire.

“Moreover, Joseph, peace and blessings be upon him, was a young, unmarried captive in enemy territory, where he had neither relatives nor friends before whom he would feel ashamed if he committed an indecent act. Many people are kept from committing shameful deeds by their sense of shame before those who know them; once away from home, however, they do whatever they desire.

“He was also alone, with no human being to fear. A soul that urges evil—had his soul been such—would have prompted him to approach her himself, indeed, to devise ways to seduce her, as many who desire the wives of powerful men habitually do when they cannot proposition them directly. If invited, however, even by a servant girl, such a man would be the quickest to respond. How much more so if the woman inviting him were his mistress, who held authority over him, and he feared being harmed for disobeying her!

“Furthermore, her husband, whose usual role would have been to rebuke his wife, did not punish her; rather, he ordered

## Page 7696

Joseph to overlook the matter, blustering like a shameless cuckold. Then she enlisted the women’s help and had him imprisoned, while he said:

“My Lord, prison is dearer to me than what they invite me to. Unless You turn their plotting away from me, I may incline towards them and become one of the ignorant.”

Let the discerning reader reflect on these powerful inducements confronting Joseph: despite their number and strength, there was no one to deter him from yielding, nor any created being to save him if he did. He will then understand that Joseph’s trial was among the gravest, and that his piety and steadfast restraint from sin—refusing to commit it and enduring his oppressors’ wrongdoing rather than complying with them—were among the greatest good deeds and most momentous acts of obedience. Joseph’s soul, peace and blessings be upon him, was among the purest of souls. How, then, could he say:

“I do not absolve myself. Indeed, the soul persistently urges evil,”

when Allah knows that his soul was innocent and did not persistently urge evil? Rather, his was a pure soul, among the purest of all. The inclination he experienced increased his soul’s purity and piety. Through experiencing it yet renouncing it for Allah’s sake, he would be credited with one of the greatest good deeds that purify the soul.

“The sixth argument”

If the statement:

“That was so that he would know that I did not betray him in his absence,”

means, as they claim, that Joseph wanted al-ʿAzīz to know, “I did not betray him with his wife”—as most of them maintain—or that the king should know, or that Allah should know, then there was nothing here to which “that” could refer. Joseph had made no preceding statement to which he could thus refer, nor had there previously been

---

## Page 7697

any mention of his chastity and refusal to yield. For what the women said was:

“We know of no wrongdoing on his part.”

And the wife of al-ʿAzīz said:

“I sought to seduce him.”

This exposes the falsehood of her earlier statement; it does not describe what Joseph himself actually did. Thus, the claim that the word “That” was spoken by Joseph—when no preceding statement or action of his appears here—is wholly untenable.

“The seventh argument”

On this interpretation—even if there were some preceding statement or action of Joseph to which “that” could refer—the meaning would be: “I refrained from indecency so that al-ʿAzīz would know that I had not betrayed him.” Yet Joseph, peace and blessings be upon him, refrained from it only out of fear of Allah, hope for His reward, and the knowledge that Allah saw him—not merely so that a created being would know.

Allah Most High said:

“She certainly desired him, and he would have inclined towards her had he not seen the proof of his Lord. Thus We turned evil and indecency away from him. Indeed, he was one of Our chosen servants.”

Allah thus states that Joseph saw the proof of his Lord and that he was one of His chosen servants. Someone who abstains from forbidden acts so that a created being will know of it has not done so because of a proof from his Lord, nor does that make him a chosen servant. If an ordinary person acted in the manner they attribute to Joseph, he would receive no reward from Allah; rather, his reward would rest with the one for whose sake he acted.

---

## Page 7698

If it is objected that Joseph had earlier said:

“Indeed, my lord has given me a good home. Surely, wrongdoers will never prosper.”

The reply is: If by this he meant his master, then the meaning is: “He has treated me kindly and honoured me, so it is unlawful for me to betray him with his wife. I would then be a wrongdoer, and wrongdoers will never prosper.” Thus, he refrained from betraying him with his wife out of fear of Allah, not so that his master would know of it.

If it is objected: His meaning was, “I sought to establish my innocence so that al-ʿAzīz would know that I had not betrayed him in his absence.” Thus, the reason given concerns his establishing his innocence, not his chastity itself.

The reply is: His purpose in establishing his innocence was not merely for one person to know, but for the king and others to know. Hence he said to the messenger:

“Return to your master and ask him about the women who cut their hands.”

Had these been Joseph’s words, he would have said: “That was so that they would know that I am innocent and have been wronged.” Moreover, it is inappropriate to attribute this statement to Joseph: his innocence had already become clear and his purpose had been achieved, so he had no need to say this to achieve it. They already knew that he had delayed only so that his innocence would become clear; there was no need for him to state such a thing.

---

## Page 7699

The Eighth Argument

In such circumstances, people ordinarily tell someone what they have done when that person appreciates its worth. This would make sense if al-ʿAzīz had been protective of his wife’s honour and greatly rewarded chastity. Yet al-ʿAzīz had shown so little protective jealousy, and had given his wife such power over someone of his kind, alongside the wrongdoers and despite Joseph’s clear innocence, that the ordinary human impulse would be to repay such a man by sleeping with his wife.

For in such circumstances, the soul that urges evil says:

“This man has not appreciated my kindness towards him, my safeguarding his wife’s honour, or my restraint. Instead, he has given her authority and enabled her to act against me.”

Thus, many people, even if they had no inclination towards sexual immorality, would commit it upon encountering someone who behaved like this—either to hurt him and repay his injustice, or out of disregard for him because he lacks protective jealousy and openly tolerates his wife’s unchastity. In such circumstances, only someone who acts for Allah’s sake, fearing Him and hoping for His reward, will steadfastly refrain from sexual immorality—not someone who merely wants people to know what he has done.

The Ninth Argument

Betrayal is the opposite of trustworthiness, and the two belong to the same category as truthfulness and lying. Hence people speak of “the truthful and trustworthy” and “the lying and treacherous.”

This applies to the wife of al-ʿAzīz. Had she lied about Joseph in his absence and said, “He tried to seduce me,” she would have been both lying and treacherous. But when she admitted that she was the one who had tried to seduce him, she was truthful and trustworthy in her account. Hence she said:

“And he is surely one of the truthful.”

She thus affirmed that he was truthful in declaring himself innocent, rather than her.

---

## Page 7700

As for committing sexual immorality, this is not a matter of betrayal and trustworthiness, but of wrongdoing, evil, and indecency. Allah describes it in these terms when He, Most High, relates Joseph’s words:

“I seek refuge in Allah! My lord has made my stay a good one. Surely the wrongdoers will not prosper.”

He did not say here, “the treacherous.” Then Allah, Most High, says:

“Thus We turned evil and indecency away from him. Indeed, he was one of Our chosen servants.”

He did not say, “to turn betrayal away from him.” Let the discerning reader ponder these subtleties in the Book of Allah, Most High.

The Tenth Argument

Among the words Allah, Most High, relates and endorses are:

“Indeed, the soul persistently urges evil, except those upon whom my Lord has mercy.”

This indicates that not every soul urges evil. Rather, among those upon whom my Lord has mercy, there is no soul that urges evil.

Some people have stated that the soul has three states: it first urges evil, then becomes self-reproaching—that is, it commits a sin and then reproaches itself for it, or it hesitates, wavering between sin and repentance. Then it becomes tranquil.

The point here is that the souls upon which my Lord has mercy do not urge evil. Since souls are divided into those shown mercy and those that urge evil, we know with certainty that the soul of al-ʿAzīz’s wife was among those that urge evil. For she urged it time after time, sought to seduce him, fabricated lies, enlisted the women’s help, and had him imprisoned; and this is among

---

## Page 7701

the gravest instances of urging evil. As for Joseph, peace and blessings be upon him, if his soul was not among those shown mercy and spared from persistently urging evil, then no soul has been shown mercy. Whoever reflects on Joseph’s story knows that the mercy he received, through having evil and indecency turned away from him, was among the greatest imaginable. Otherwise, Allah would not have mentioned it in the Quran and made it a lesson. Every righteous person, great or small, has a soul that, if tested by such inducements, would be less likely to receive mercy than Joseph’s.

On this assumption, if Joseph’s soul was not shown mercy, then no soul has been shown mercy. All souls would therefore persistently urge evil, contrary to what the Quran states.

No weight should be given to the story related about Muslim ibn Yasār: a Bedouin woman invited him to have relations with her while they were in the desert. He refused and wept. His brother arrived while he was weeping and began to weep too. The woman also wept and left. Muslim then slept and saw Joseph in his dream, who said:

“I am Joseph, who entertained the thought, and you are Muslim, who did not.”

Someone hearing this story might suppose that Muslim’s spiritual state was more perfect. This is ignorance, for two reasons:

The first is that Muslim was not under the control of the woman seeking to seduce him. She had no authority over him, nor did she have the power to falsely accuse him and enlist the women’s help

---

## Page 7702

and have him imprisoned, while neither her husband nor anyone else would help him remain chaste. By contrast, when Muslim wept, that woman left. Even had she refused to leave, his cries against her or her fear of other people would have driven her away from him. How could this compare with what Joseph, peace and blessings be upon him, endured?

Second:

When Joseph abandoned the thought he had entertained for Allah’s sake, it earned him a good deed and did not detract from his standing. It is established in both Sahīh collections, in the hadith concerning the seven whom

“Allah will shade with His shade on the Day when there is no shade but His: a man whom a woman of rank and beauty invites to herself, but he says, ‘I fear Allah, Lord of the worlds.’”

This is for merely being invited; what, then, of persistent attempts at seduction, enlisting others’ help, and imprisonment? It is known that she was a woman of rank, and it has been related that she was beautiful. This is indeed what seems likely, for the wife of Egypt’s chief minister would presumably be beautiful.

As for the Bedouin woman who invited Muslim to herself, she was undoubtedly beneath that level. His dream, and Joseph’s words in it,

“I am Joseph, who entertained the thought, and you are Muslim, who did not,”

could, at most, carry the same weight as Joseph saying this to him while awake. If Joseph said this, it would be to Muslim’s credit—a commendation and praise—and an expression of humility on Joseph’s part. When someone of high standing shows humility toward someone beneath him, this does not diminish his own standing.

Eleventh point:

This statement contains—alongside the acknowledgment

---

## Page 7703

of sin—an explanation offered by mentioning its cause. Her words,

“It was I who sought to seduce him, and he is indeed telling the truth,”

contain an acknowledgment of sin. Her words,

“I do not absolve myself. Indeed, the soul persistently urges evil,”

correspond to her statement, “It was I who sought to seduce him”: that is, I acknowledge my sin; I do not absolve myself. She then explained the cause, saying:

“Indeed, the soul persistently urges evil.”

My own soul is no exception, so it is not surprising that this should have come from me. She then mentioned what calls for seeking forgiveness and mercy, saying:

“Indeed, my Lord is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

If it is said, “These are the words of someone who acknowledges that fornication is a sin and that Allah may forgive the one who commits it,” I say: Yes. The Quran indicates this, for her husband said:

“Joseph, overlook this. And you, seek forgiveness for your sin.”

His command that she seek forgiveness for her sin proves that they regarded it as a sin and sought forgiveness for it, even though they were also polytheists. The Arabs, too, were polytheists, yet they prohibited sexual immoralities and sought Allah’s forgiveness for them. Indeed,

“When the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, took from Hind bint ʿUtba ibn Rabīʿa the pledge required of women—that she would associate nothing with Allah, would not steal, and would not commit fornication—she said, ‘Would a free woman commit fornication?’”

Among them, fornication was known to occur among slave women. This is why, in their language, freedom came predominantly to mean the opposite of slavery, whereas the original meaning of

---

## Page 7704

the word is chastity. Chastity, however, was customary among women who were not slaves. Indeed, al-Bukhārī related in his Sahīh that Abū Rajā al-ʿUtāridī saw, during the pre-Islamic period, a male monkey committing fornication with a female. The other monkeys gathered against it and stoned it.

One of the trustworthy elders told me that he had seen a bird that had laid eggs in a congregational mosque. Some people took an egg away and brought in eggs of another species of bird. When the eggs hatched, the chicks were of a different species. The male began seeking out birds of its own kind until a number of them had gathered. They kept attacking the female until they killed her. Such behaviour is well known among animals.

People everywhere agree in regarding sexual immoralities as reprehensible and abhorrent. Those people acknowledged the Creator despite their polytheism. This is why Joseph said to them:

“O my two companions in prison, are separate lords better, or Allah, the One, the All-Subduing?

“You worship besides Him nothing but names that you and your forefathers have devised, for which Allah has sent down no authority. Judgment belongs to Allah alone. He has commanded that you worship none but Him. That is the upright religion, but most people do not know.”

“The Twelfth Argument”

It may be said: Allah, glorified and exalted be He, has never mentioned a sin committed by any prophet without also mentioning his repentance from it. Hence, people have held two views concerning the prophets’ protection from sin: either they hold that prophets are protected from committing sins, or

---

## Page 7705

they hold that prophets are protected from being left to persist in them, especially in matters concerning the conveyance of the message. The Muslim community unanimously agrees that a prophet is protected from being left uncorrected in any error in this regard, since that would contradict the purpose of the message and what the miracle attests to. This is not the place to discuss this at length. The point here is that Allah has never mentioned in His Book a sin committed by any prophet without also mentioning his repentance from it, as in the accounts of Adam, Moses, David, and other prophets.

This is how those who uphold the majority position—that prophets are protected from being left to persist in sin—answer those who deny that prophets commit any sins whatsoever. One of the latter’s strongest arguments is that relied upon by al-Qādī ʿIyād and others, who said:

“We are commanded to follow their example in their actions, and admitting this possibility undermines our following their example.”

The response is that we follow their example only in actions that were allowed to stand. Likewise, the commands and prohibitions they convey may be abrogated, yet admitting this possibility does not negate the obligation to obey, since obedience is obligatory in whatever has not been abrogated. Thus, the absence of abrogation confirms the ruling, and the absence of censure confirms the action. The presumption is that neither abrogation nor censure has occurred.

As for Joseph, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, Allah Most High has never mentioned in the Quran that he did anything with the woman for which he repented or sought forgiveness. Everyone agrees that he did not commit the indecent act, but some people maintain that he committed

---

## Page 7706

some of the acts leading up to it, such as their claim that he untied his trousers and sat before her in the position of a circumciser, and similar allegations. What they transmit concerning this does not come from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Their only basis is reports from some of the People of the Book. What the Jews have said about the prophets, and how they have disparaged them, is well known—as in their statements about Solomon and David. Even if we had nothing to refute their reports, we would not believe them in matters whose truth we did not know. How, then, could we believe them concerning something the Quran has shown to be otherwise?

The Quran describes Joseph’s resistance to temptation, fear of Allah, and patience in this affair in terms unparalleled in its accounts of anyone else. Had Joseph sinned, he would either have persisted in it or repented. Persistence is impossible, so he would necessarily have repented. Yet Allah mentions neither repentance nor seeking forgiveness on his part concerning this, as He does concerning other prophets.

This shows that Joseph’s actions were virtuous deeds and commendable efforts, as Allah relates concerning him in His words, Most High:

“Indeed, whoever fears Allah and is patient—Allah does not let the reward of those who do good go to waste.”

Since this is the case with Joseph, the statement:

“Indeed, the soul constantly urges evil, except those to whom my Lord shows mercy”

fits only the situation of al-ʿAzīz’s wife, not Joseph’s. Attributing sins to Joseph in this affair is therefore a fabrication against the Book and the Messenger. It involves distorting words from their proper places, and it involves

---

## Page 7707

backbiting a noble prophet, speaking falsely about him without evidence, and attributing to him things from which Allah has declared him free. It is quite possible that this originated with the Jews, those given to slander who accused Moses of things of which Allah exonerated him. How, then, would they treat the other prophets? Their reports were accepted by those who trusted them and made their interpretation of the Quran conform to this belief.

Know that those who deviate concerning the prophets’ protection from sin fall into two opposing extremes, both of which conflict with the Book of Allah in certain respects:

One group went to excess in claiming that the prophets could not sin, to the point of distorting the Quranic texts that recount their repentance from sins, Allah’s forgiveness of them, and His raising their ranks thereby. Another group went to excess by relating things about them of which the Quran declares them innocent, attributing to them sins and faults from which Allah has declared them free. The former contradict the Quran, and the latter contradict the Quran.
