Whoever follows the Quran as it is, without distortion, belongs to the community of the middle way and is guided to the straight path—the path of those whom Allah has blessed: the prophets, the utterly truthful, the martyrs, and the righteous.

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The Jews have incurred wrath, and the Christians have gone astray.”

It is also authentically reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“You will surely follow the ways of those before you, matching them exactly, so that even if they entered a lizard’s hole, you would enter it. They said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, the Jews and the Christians?’

---

## Page 7708

He replied, ‘Who else?’”

In another hadith recorded in the Sahīh:

“My community will surely follow the course of the nations before it, span by span and cubit by cubit.” They asked, “O Messenger of Allah, the Persians and the Romans?” He replied, “Who else among the peoples could it be?”

There is no doubt that many people acquired learning from the People of the Book and from the Persians and Romans, which they unwittingly incorporated into the Muslims’ learning and religion. Likewise, many teachings of the polytheists of India, Greece, and elsewhere, of the Magians and Persians, and of the Sabians of Greece and elsewhere found their way into the thinking of many later generations, particularly those engaged in philosophy and speculative theology.

Many teachings of the People of the Book—the Jews and Christians—also found their way into a group who were better than these, since the People of the Book were better than the others. When the Muslims conquered these lands, Syria, Egypt, and similar regions were full of Christians and Jews from among the People of the Book. They related to the Muslims accounts from the People of the Book, some true and some false. Among the most prolific transmitters of such accounts was Kaʿb al-Ahbār.

Muʿāwiyah—may Allah be pleased with him—said: “Among those who relate accounts to us from the People of the Book, we have seen no one more truthful than Kaʿb, although we would sometimes find falsehood in his reports.”

It is well known that the bulk of Kaʿb’s material consisted of what he transmitted from their books. And if

---

## Page 7709

someone were to transmit what he found in books about our Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—his reports would contain much falsehood. How much more, then, would this apply to the books of the People of the Book, given the long passage of time, the alteration of their religion, the divisions among its adherents, and the many followers of falsehood among them?

This is a matter to which Muslims should pay close attention. They should examine the way of the Companions of the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—who knew best what he brought and knew best which teachings of the People of the Book, the polytheists, the Magians, and the Sabians opposed it. This is indeed a fundamental principle. Hence the leading scholars, such as Ahmad ibn Hanbal and others, said: “The foundations of the Sunnah lie in adhering to the way of the Companions of the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace.”

Whoever reflects on this subject will find that many religious innovations were introduced on the strength of reports originating with those peoples. Examples include reports about the merits of particular places in Syria—mountains, caves, sites associated with the prophets, and the like. Among these are the accounts concerning Mount Qāsiyūn, the sites associated with the prophets there, and the merit of visiting them. Some fabricators among the shaykhs have even claimed that visiting a cave there three times is equivalent to performing Hajj. They call it “the Stations of the Prophets.” The reports related on this subject do not go back to the Companions, but come only from those

---

## Page 7710

below them in standing who took these accounts from the People of the Book. Had there been any basis for this, it would have been known to the leading Companions who came to Syria, such as Bilāl ibn Rabāh, Muʿādh ibn Jabal, and ʿUbādah ibn al-Sāmit—indeed, such as Abū ʿUbaydah ibn al-Jarrāh, the trustworthy one of this community, and others like them. The eminent Companions who entered Syria were, in fact, superior to those who entered the other regions, apart from the Hijaz. Yet none of them is reported to have sought out any sites associated with the prophets, whether their graves or the places where they had stayed. They neither made them places of worship nor deliberately sought to pray there or offer supplications at them.

Indeed, it is established that ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb—may Allah be pleased with him—was once travelling when he saw people repeatedly visiting a place to pray there. He asked, “What is this?”

They replied, “This is a place where the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—prayed.”

He said, “And what if it is a place where the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—prayed? Do you wish to turn the sites associated with your prophets into places of worship? This is precisely what destroyed those before you. Whoever happens to be there when prayer becomes due should pray; otherwise, he should continue on his way.”

When he entered Jerusalem and wished to build a place of prayer for the Muslims, he asked Kaʿb, “Where should I build it?”

He replied, “Build it behind the Rock.”

ʿUmar said, “Judaism has influenced you, son of a Jewish woman! Rather, I shall build it in front of it.” For this reason, whenever ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar entered the sanctuary in Jerusalem, he prayed in its front part and did not go to the Rock.

They also rejected as false Kaʿb’s report that Allah had said to it, “You are My lower Throne.” They would say, “He whose Footstool encompasses the heavens and the earth—how could

---

## Page 7711

the Rock be His lower Throne?” The Companions did not venerate it. They said that it was only when ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān was at war with Ibn al-Zubayr that he built the dome over it. People would go on pilgrimage and meet Ibn al-Zubayr, so he exalted the Rock to occupy them with visiting it rather than travelling to Ibn al-Zubayr. Otherwise, nothing in our revealed law warrants venerating the Rock, building a dome over it, or draping it with leather coverings and woollen cloth.

Had this been part of our revealed law, ʿUmar, ʿUthmān, and Muʿāwiyah—may Allah be pleased with them—would have had greater claim to doing so than those who came after them. They were, after all, Companions of the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and knew his sunnah better and followed it more faithfully than later generations. Likewise, the Companions did not repeatedly visit the tomb of Abraham, the close friend of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Indeed, they did not even open it, nor did they build a mosque over the grave of any prophet. They knew that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—had said:

“Those before you used to take graves as places of worship. Beware! Do not take graves as places of worship, for I forbid you to do so.”

When Daniel’s grave was discovered at Tustar, Abū Mūsā wrote about it to ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb—may Allah be pleased with him. ʿUmar wrote back: “During the day, dig thirteen graves. Then bury him at night in one of them and cover his grave with earth, lest people be led astray through it.”

I have examined the reports transmitted concerning deliberately visiting these sites and supplicating

---

## Page 7712

there or praying there, and have found no basis for them in reports from the Companions. Rather, they originate with those who took reports from the People of the Book.

Among the foundations of Islam is distinguishing the Book and wisdom with which Allah sent Muhammad—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—from everything else, neither mixing them with other things nor confounding truth with falsehood as the People of the Book did. For Allah, glorified is He, has perfected our religion for us, completed His favour upon us, and approved Islam as our religion. The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“I have left you upon a clear path, whose night is as clear as its day. None will deviate from it after me except one who is doomed.”

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd—may Allah be pleased with him—said:

“The Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—drew a line for us, then drew other lines to its right and left. He then said: ‘This is Allah’s path, and these are the other paths. At the head of each is a devil calling people to it.’ Then he recited the words of Allah Most High: ‘This is My straight path, so follow it, and do not follow other paths, lest they lead you away from His path.’”

All of this comes down to safeguarding two principles:

The first is to establish precisely what the Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—brought, without mixing it with things that do not belong to it, such as weak reports and false interpretations. Rather, due attention must be given to understanding both its transmission and its meaning.

---

## Page 7713

And the second is that it must not be opposed with specious arguments, whether based on opinion or transmitted reports. Allah Most High says, in His commands to the Children of Israel—which also contain a lesson for us:

“Believe in what I have sent down, confirming what is with you, and do not be the first to disbelieve in it. Do not exchange My verses for a small price, and fear Me alone.”

“Do not mix truth with falsehood or conceal the truth while you know.”

The truth brought by the Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—must neither be concealed, nor mixed with falsehood, nor opposed with anything else. Allah Most High says:

“Follow what has been sent down to you from your Lord, and do not follow other protectors besides Him. How little you take heed!”

He Most High also says:

“Who is more unjust than one who invents a lie about Allah, or says, ‘Revelation has come to me,’ when nothing has been revealed to him, or one who says, ‘I will send down the like of what Allah has sent down’?”

These three categories are the enemies of the messengers. When one of them brings something contrary to the truth, he either says, “Allah has sent it down to me,” thereby inventing a lie about Allah; or he says that it has been revealed to him, without naming who revealed it; or he says, “I composed it myself, and I send down the like of what Allah has sent down.” Thus, he either attributes it to Allah, attributes it to himself, or attributes it to no one. These three categories belong to the devils among mankind and jinn who inspire one another with embellished speech in order to deceive. Allah Most High says:

“The Messenger said, ‘My Lord, my people have indeed abandoned this Quran.’”

“Thus We have appointed for every prophet an enemy from among the wicked. But your Lord suffices as a guide and helper.”

And Allah knows best. All praise belongs to Allah.

---

## Page 7714

He—may Allah be pleased with him—was asked about the words of Allah Most High:

“Say, ‘This is my way: I call to Allah with clear insight—I and those who follow me.’”

Is this call a general obligation incumbent upon every Muslim man and woman, or not? Does enjoining what is right and forbidding what is wrong fall within this call, or not? Whether or not they fall within it, are they duties incumbent upon every individual Muslim, as mentioned above? If they are obligatory, are they obligatory without qualification, even when they entail hardship? May someone who enjoins what is right and forbids what is wrong exact retribution from a person who harms him for doing so, lest that person be emboldened to transgress against the truth? If he may do so, is refraining from it always preferable, or not?

He—may Allah be pleased with him and grant him contentment—answered:

All praise belongs to Allah, Lord of all worlds. Calling to Allah means calling people to believe in Him and in what His messengers brought, by believing what they reported and obeying what they commanded. This includes calling people to the two testimonies of faith, establishing prayer, giving zakat, fasting Ramadan, and performing pilgrimage to the House; and calling them to believe in Allah, His angels, His Books, and His messengers,

---

## Page 7715

resurrection after death, and divine decree, both its good and its evil; and calling the servant to worship his Lord as though he sees Him. These three degrees—Islam, faith, and excellence in worship—are all included in religion, as the Prophet said in the authentic hadith:

“This is Gabriel, who came to teach you your religion.”

He said this after answering his questions about these three, thus making clear that they all belong to our religion.

The word dīn (religion) is a verbal noun, and a verbal noun may be ascribed either to the subject or to the object of the action. One says dāna fulānun fulānan to mean that one person worshipped and obeyed another, just as dānahu may mean that he humbled him. Thus, the servant worships and obeys Allah. When religion is ascribed to the servant, it is because he is the one who worships and obeys; when it is ascribed to Allah, it is because He is the One worshipped and obeyed. As Allah Most High says:

“Fight them until there is no more persecution and worship is devoted to Allah.”

Calling to Allah therefore means calling the servant to His religion, whose foundation is worshipping Him alone, without partner. This is what Allah sent His messengers and revealed His Books to establish. Allah Most High says:

“He has ordained for you the religion He enjoined upon Noah, that which We have revealed to you, and what We enjoined upon Abraham, Moses, and Jesus: ‘Establish the religion and do not become divided in it.’”

And Allah Most High says:

“Ask those of Our messengers whom We sent before you: did We appoint any gods besides the Most Merciful to be worshipped?”

And Allah Most High says:

“We certainly sent

---

## Page 7716

to every nation a messenger, proclaiming, ‘Worship Allah and shun false gods.’ Among them were those Allah guided and those upon whom misguidance was decreed.” Allah Most High also says:

“We never sent a messenger before you without revealing to him: ‘There is no god but Me, so worship Me.’”

It is authentically reported from Abū Hurayrah that the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“We prophets share one religion. The prophets are brothers with one father and different mothers. Indeed, I am the closest of all people to the son of Mary; there is no prophet between him and me.”

Thus, their religion is one; only their laws and ways differed, as Allah Most High says:

“For each of you We have prescribed a law and a way.”

The messengers therefore agree on the religion that encompasses the fundamental principles of belief and practice. The principles of belief include faith in Allah, His messengers, and the Last Day. The practical principles include the general rules of conduct mentioned in Sūrat al-Anʿām, Sūrat al-Aʿrāf, and Sūrat Banī Isrā’īl, such as His words, Most High:

“Say, ‘Come, I will recite what your Lord has forbidden you,’”

through to the end of the three verses; and His words:

“Your Lord has decreed that you worship none but Him,”

through to the end of these injunctions; and His words:

“Say, ‘My Lord has commanded justice. Turn your faces towards Him at every place of worship, and call upon Him, devoting your worship sincerely to Him.’”

And His words:

“Say, ‘My Lord has forbidden only indecencies, whether open or hidden; sin; unjust aggression; associating with Allah that for which He has sent down no authority; and saying about Allah what you do not know.’”

---

## Page 7717

These matters, then, belong to the religion upon which the revealed laws agree, as does most of what is contained in the Meccan surahs. These surahs encompass the fundamental principles upon which Allah’s messengers agreed, since their discourse includes the call to those who do not acknowledge messengership in principle. The Medinan surahs, by contrast, address those who acknowledge messengership in principle, such as the People of the Book, who believe in some and disbelieve in others, and the believers, who believe in Allah’s Books and messengers.

Accordingly, these surahs establish the laws through which Allah perfected the religion: the direction of prayer, pilgrimage, fasting, devotional retreat, jihad, the rules governing marriage and related matters, and financial rulings concerning justice, as in trade; benevolence, as in charity; and injustice, as in usury—as well as other matters that complete the religion.

Thus, the form of address in the Meccan surahs is:

“O mankind!”

This is because the call to the fundamental principles is universal: someone who does not acknowledge the foundation is not called to its subsidiary matters. When the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, migrated to Medina, where the believers gained strength and where the People of the Book also lived, both groups were addressed. The former were addressed with:

“O you who believe!”

And the latter with:

“O People of the Book!”

or:

“O Children of Israel!”

None of these forms of address was revealed in Mecca. The Medinan surahs, however, also contain the address:

“O mankind!”

This occurs in Sūrat al-Nisā’ and Sūrat al-Hajj, both of which are Medinan, and likewise in Sūrat al-Baqarah. This poses a difficulty for the statement of the learned scholar Ibn ʿAbbās, because the ruling in question encompasses humanity as a whole, and addressing people by a specific designation does not conflict with addressing them by a general one.

---

## Page 7718

so the believers are included both in the address “O mankind!” and in the address “O you who believe!”

Calling to Allah therefore entails enjoining everything Allah has commanded and forbidding everything He has prohibited. This is enjoining all that is right and forbidding all that is wrong. The Messenger, peace and blessings be upon him, fulfilled this call: he commanded people to do everything Allah had commanded and forbade them everything Allah had prohibited. He enjoined all that is right and forbade all that is wrong.

Allah Most High says:

“My mercy encompasses all things. I will decree it for those who fear Me and give zakah, and those who believe in Our signs—those who follow the Messenger, the unlettered Prophet, whom they find mentioned in the Torah and the Gospel they possess. He enjoins upon them what is right and forbids them what is wrong, makes lawful for them good things and forbids them evil things.”

His call to Allah was by His permission; he did not institute any religion that Allah had not authorized. As Allah Most High says:

“Indeed, We have sent you as a witness, a bearer of glad tidings and a warner, and as one who calls to Allah by His permission, and as an illuminating lamp.”

This contrasts with those whom He censured in His words:

“Or do they have partners who have prescribed for them in religion that which Allah has not authorized?”

Allah Most High also says:

“Say, ‘Have you considered the provision Allah has sent down for you, some of which you have declared unlawful and some lawful?’ Say, ‘Has Allah given you permission, or are you fabricating lies against Allah?’”

---

## Page 7719

Another point that clarifies what we have stated is that Allah, glorified be He, sometimes mentions commanding him to call to Allah, and at other times to call to His path. As He Most High says:

“Call to the way of your Lord with wisdom and good instruction.”

For it is understood that whenever someone calls others to something, that call necessarily involves two things: first, the intended goal; and second, the means and path leading to that goal. This is why He sometimes speaks of calling to Allah and at other times of calling to His path. For He, glorified be He, is the One to be worshipped, the intended goal of the call.

Worship is a term encompassing the utmost love for Him and the utmost humble submission to Him. Whoever submits humbly to another while hating him is not thereby worshipping him; nor is someone who loves another without humbly submitting to him thereby worshipping him. Allah, glorified be He, deserves to be loved with the utmost love. Indeed, He is to be the One loved unconditionally, such that nothing is loved except for His sake. He deserves to be revered and shown the utmost humble submission. Indeed, nothing is to be submitted to except for His sake. Whoever associates another with Him in this love and submission has not attained true love and reverence, for associating partners with Him necessarily diminishes love.

He Most High says:

“Yet among mankind are those who take others besides Allah as equals, loving them as they love Allah. But those who believe are stronger in their love for Allah.”

That is, their love for Allah is stronger than the love these people have

---

## Page 7720

for the rivals they set up beside Him. He Most High also says:

“Allah presents an example: a man owned by quarrelling partners and a man belonging exclusively to one man—are the two equal in comparison?”

Likewise, arrogance prevents true humble submission to Allah; indeed, it prevents true love for Allah, for complete love necessarily entails humble submission and obedience: a lover obeys the one he loves. Thus, love has degrees, the highest of which is enslavement by love, which is worshipful devotion. Taym Allāh means “servant of Allah”; a heart enslaved by love is one bound in servitude to its beloved. None but Allah alone deserves this.

Islam means that a servant submits to Allah and to no one else, as expressed by the declaration:

“There is no god worthy of worship except Allah.”

Whoever submits to Him and to another is a polytheist, while whoever does not submit to Him is arrogant. Both attitudes are contrary to Islam. Associating partners with Allah is prevalent among Christians and those who resemble them from among the misguided and those affiliated with the Muslim community.

We have discussed the matters related to this subject at length in several places. These include establishing Allah’s divinity and oneness; the impossibility of His having any partner; the ruin of the heavens and the earth if another god besides Him were assumed to exist; the distinction between associating partners with Him in lordship and associating partners with Him in worship; explaining that human beings are naturally disposed to acknowledge Him, love Him, and revere Him; and that hearts cannot be sound unless they worship Allah alone, and have no

---

## Page 7721

perfection, soundness, pleasure, delight, joy, or happiness without this. We have also discussed the true nature of the straight path—the path of those whom Allah has blessed: the prophets, the steadfastly truthful, the martyrs, and the righteous—and other matters connected with this subject. Fully realizing it fulfills the purpose of the prophetic call and the divine message; it is the heart and essence of the Quran. We have also explained Allah’s oneness in knowledge and affirmation, mentioned in His words:

“Say, ‘He is Allah, the One.’”

“Allah, the Eternal Refuge.”

And His oneness in intention and action, mentioned in His words, Most High:

“Say, ‘O disbelievers.’”

We have also discussed related matters, for this explains the foundation, true nature, and purpose of calling to Allah. The intention in this answer, however, is to mention these matters in outline, as there is insufficient scope here to discuss them in detail.

Enjoining everything that Allah and His Messenger love—whether obligatory or recommended, inward or outward—is part of calling to Allah. Likewise, forbidding everything that Allah and His Messenger hate, whether inward or outward, is part of calling to Allah. Calling to Allah is not complete unless it includes calling people to do what Allah loves and abandon what He hates, whether this concerns words or deeds, inward or outward.

This includes believing what the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, has conveyed concerning Allah’s names and attributes, the Hereafter, and the details of these matters, as well as what he has conveyed concerning all other created beings, such as the Throne, the Footstool, the angels, the prophets, their nations, and their enemies. It also includes devoting one’s religion sincerely to Allah alone; loving Allah and His Messenger more than anyone or anything else; placing one’s trust in Him; and hoping for His mercy,

---

## Page 7722

and fearing His punishment, patiently accepting His judgment, and the like. It also includes speaking truthfully, fulfilling trusts, keeping covenants, maintaining ties of kinship, treating neighbours well, and striving in His cause with the heart, hand, and tongue.

Once this is clear, calling to Allah is obligatory upon those who follow him—his community. They call to Allah just as he called to Allah. This likewise entails commanding them to do what he commanded, forbidding them from what he forbade, and informing them of what he conveyed. Calling includes enjoining, and this encompasses enjoining everything that is right and forbidding everything that is wrong. Allah has described his community in these terms in several places, just as He described him. He, Most High, says:

“You are the best nation produced for mankind. You enjoin what is right and forbid what is wrong.”

And He, Most High, says:

“The believing men and believing women are allies of one another. They enjoin what is right and forbid what is wrong.”

And so the verse continues. This duty is incumbent upon the community as a whole. It is what the scholars call a collective obligation: if a group among them fulfils it, the rest are relieved of it. Thus, the entire community is commanded to undertake it, but if a group fulfils it, the rest are relieved of the obligation.

He, Most High, says:

“Let there be among you a group who call to goodness, enjoin what is right, and forbid what is wrong. It is they who will be successful.”

Thus, his community as a whole takes his place in calling to Allah; for this reason, their consensus is

---

## Page 7723

conclusive proof. His community does not agree upon misguidance, and whenever they dispute over something, they refer their dispute to Allah and His Messenger. Every member of the community must undertake whatever calling he is able to carry out if no one else undertakes it. Whatever others fulfil ceases to be his obligation, and whatever lies beyond his ability is not required of him. But whatever no one else undertakes, and he is capable of doing, he must undertake.

For this reason, one person may be obliged to undertake something that another is not. The work of calling is apportioned among the community, sometimes on this basis and sometimes on other grounds. One person may call to an obligatory belief, another to an obligatory outward deed, and another to an obligatory inward deed. Thus, calling varies both in what is obligatory and in what actually takes place.

This makes clear that calling to Allah is obligatory upon every Muslim, but it is a collective obligation. A particular individual is required to undertake only what he is capable of doing when no one else fulfils it. The same applies to enjoining what is right and forbidding what is wrong, conveying what the Messenger brought, striving in Allah’s cause, and teaching faith and the Quran.

This also makes clear that calling itself constitutes enjoining what is right and forbidding what is wrong. For the one who calls seeks, requests, and calls for that to which he invites people, and this is precisely what it means to enjoin it. Enjoining means seeking the performance of the deed enjoined, requesting it, and calling to it. Thus, calling

---

## Page 7724

to Allah is calling to His way, and thus enjoining people to follow His way. His way consists in believing what He has reported and obeying what He has commanded. It has become clear that both are incumbent upon every individual Muslim as collective obligations, not as individual obligations like the five daily prayers, but rather like jihad.

Fulfilling obligations—whether the obligation of calling to Allah or other duties—requires certain conditions, as stated in the hadith:

“Whoever enjoins what is right and forbids what is wrong should understand what he enjoins and understand what he forbids; be gentle in what he enjoins and gentle in what he forbids; and be forbearing in what he enjoins and forbearing in what he forbids.”

Understanding must precede enjoining, so that what is right may be identified and what is wrong rejected. Gentleness must accompany enjoining, so that one may take the most direct path to achieving the intended purpose. Forbearance must follow enjoining, so that one may patiently endure the harm inflicted by those whom one enjoins and forbids, for one is often harmed on that account.

For this reason, Allah Most High says:

“Enjoin what is right, forbid what is wrong, and patiently endure whatever befalls you.”

He has commanded our Prophet to be patient in many passages, as Allah Most High says at the beginning of Sūrat al-Muddaththir:

“Arise and warn!”
“Magnify your Lord!”
“Purify your garments!”
“Shun defilement!”
“Do not give in order to receive more!”
“And be patient for the sake of your Lord!”

Allah Most High also says:

“Be patient with your Lord’s decree, for you are under Our Eyes.”

And He says:

“Patiently endure what they say.”

---

## Page 7725

And Allah Most High says:

“Messengers before you were indeed denied, yet they patiently endured rejection and persecution until Our help came to them.”

And He says:

“So be patient with your Lord’s decree, and do not be like the man of the fish.”

He, glorified be He, has coupled fear of Allah with patience in such statements as:

“You will certainly be tested in your wealth and your lives, and you will certainly hear much abuse from those who were given the Scripture before you and from the polytheists. But if you are patient and fear Allah, that is indeed a matter requiring firm resolve.”

The believers used to call people to faith in Allah and to the good He commanded, and forbid the evil Allah prohibited. The polytheists and the People of the Book would therefore harm them. He had informed them of this before it happened, telling them:

“But if you are patient and fear Allah, that is indeed a matter requiring firm resolve.”

Joseph, peace be upon him, said:

“I am Joseph, and this is my brother. Allah has indeed favoured us. Whoever fears Allah and remains patient—Allah certainly does not let the reward of those who do good go to waste.”

Fear of Allah thus includes obedience to Him, which encompasses enjoining what is right and forbidding what is wrong. Patience includes patiently enduring afflictions, among them the harm inflicted upon the person who enjoins and forbids by those whom he addresses.

Nevertheless, the person who enjoins and forbids may ward off harm to himself, just as a person may defend himself against an aggressor. If the person whom he enjoins or forbids seeks to strike him, take his property, or do something similar, and he is able to repel him, he may do so—unlike the case where the harm has already occurred

---

## Page 7726

and the offender has repented of it; this is a situation calling for patience and forbearance. The perfect example in this regard is our Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, as reported in the two Sahīh collections from ‘Ā’ishah, who said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, never struck a servant of his, a woman, an animal, or anything at all with his hand, except when fighting in Allah’s cause. Nor did he ever take revenge for a wrong done to him personally, unless Allah’s sacred limits were violated. When Allah’s sacred limits were violated, nothing could withstand his anger until he exacted retribution for Allah’s sake.”

His sublime character thus included never taking revenge for personal wrongs, while, when Allah’s sacred limits were violated, nothing could withstand his anger until he exacted retribution for Allah’s sake. It is well known that harming the Messenger is among the gravest prohibitions, for whoever harms him has harmed Allah. By the unanimous agreement of the Muslim community, anyone who reviles him must be put to death—whether this is held to be because such conduct constitutes apostasy, or because it constitutes aggravated apostasy that makes the execution of the offender a prescribed legal penalty.

Numerous reports describe the Prophet’s forbearance and his pardoning of those who harmed him. As Allah Most High says:

“Many of the People of the Scripture wish they could turn you back to disbelief after you have believed, out of envy within themselves, after the truth has become clear to them. So pardon and overlook until Allah brings His command.”

When someone who enjoins what is right and forbids what is wrong is harmed, and that harm transgresses Allah’s limits and involves a right belonging to Allah, everyone is obliged to forbid it, and its perpetrator deserves punishment. However, because a human right is also involved, the injured person may pardon the offender, just as he may pardon someone guilty of sexual slander, a killer, or another offender. His pardoning the offender does not

---

## Page 7727

remove the punishment incurred for violating Allah’s right. Rather, it perfects, for the one who enjoins right and forbids wrong, the patience and forgiveness Allah has prescribed for someone in his position, bringing him within the scope of His words, Most High:

“If you remain patient and mindful of Allah, that is indeed a matter requiring firm resolve.”

And His words:

“So pardon and overlook until Allah brings His command.”

There is, however, a subtle distinction here. Patience is commanded unconditionally, and its obligation is therefore not abrogated. Pardoning and overlooking, by contrast, were prescribed until a specified point: until Allah brought His command. When He brought His command by granting the Messenger power and victory, the Messenger became able to wage jihad against those people, compel them to do what is right, and prevent them from doing wrong. It then became obligatory for him to take physical action in these matters where he had previously been unable to do so. He remained commanded to exercise patience in this, just as he had been commanded to exercise patience before.

The purpose of jihad is that Allah’s word should be supreme and that religion should be wholly for Allah. Its purpose is to establish Allah’s religion, not to satisfy an individual’s personal claims. Thus, the reward for whatever harm a fighter suffers in his person or property rests with Allah, for Allah has purchased from the believers their lives and their wealth in exchange for Paradise. This is so much the case that, when disbelievers embrace Islam or enter into a treaty, they are not held liable for the Muslim lives they have taken or the property they have destroyed.

Indeed, even if they embrace Islam while still holding property they have seized from Muslims as spoils, it belongs to them according to the majority of scholars, including Mālik, Abū Hanīfah, and Ahmad. Such was the established practice of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and of his rightly guided caliphs.

---

## Page 7728

If someone who enjoins right and forbids wrong is attacked and harmed, and the person he is addressing then repents and accepts the truth from him, he should not exact retribution from that person or punish him for the harm he caused. Through repentance, that person’s liability for violating Allah’s right has been removed, just as a disbeliever’s liability for violating Allah’s rights is removed when he embraces Islam. As authentically reported, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Islam wipes away what came before it, and repentance wipes away what came before it.”

When a disbeliever embraces Islam, Islam wipes away what came before it. This includes his transgressions against Muslims’ lives and property, because he did not believe those acts to be forbidden; rather, he regarded them as lawful. When he repents of that, he is forgiven both for regarding those acts as lawful and for the actions that followed from that belief.

Likewise, the person being commanded or forbidden may regard harming the one who enjoins right and forbids wrong as lawful. Such is the case with people of religious innovation and desires who believe themselves to be right and regard the person commanding or forbidding them as an aggressor against them. If they repent, they are not punished for their transgressions against someone from Ahl al-Sunnah who enjoins right and forbids wrong. An example is a Rāfidī who believes the Companions to have been disbelievers or evildoers and reviles them on that basis. If he repents of this belief and comes to love them and give them his allegiance, they no longer have any claim against him. Rather, their right is encompassed by Allah’s right, arising and lapsing with it, because it stems from his belief.

For this reason, the majority of scholars—including Abū Hanīfah, Mālik, Ahmad according to the sounder of the two reports from him, and al-Shāfiʿī according to one of his two opinions—held that rebels acting on a religious interpretation are not liable to compensate those on the side of justice for what they destroy on the basis of that interpretation. Likewise, by scholarly consensus, those on the side of justice are not liable to compensate the rebels for what they destroy on the basis of a religious interpretation.

---

## Page 7729

The same applies to apostates according to the sounder of the two scholarly opinions. An apostate, a rebel acting on a religious interpretation, and a person of religious innovation each believes himself to be right and acts on that interpretation. If he repents of this, his repentance is like that of a disbeliever repenting of his disbelief: he is forgiven for his past actions based on that interpretation. This differs from someone who believes that what he is doing is wrongful and aggressive, such as a Muslim who wrongs another Muslim, a non-Muslim under Muslim protection who wrongs a Muslim, or an apostate who destroys another person’s property while not engaged in hostilities, but outwardly appearing to be a Muslim or someone protected by a covenant. By scholarly agreement, such people are liable to compensate for what they destroy.

Thus, if the person being commanded or forbidden believes that he is permitted to harm the one who enjoins right and forbids wrong, he is among those acting on a religious interpretation. The right of the one who enjoins right and forbids wrong is encompassed by the right of Allah Most High. If the offender repents, both claims lapse. If he does not repent, he remains answerable for violating Allah’s right, which includes the right of the human being. He may be a disbeliever, an evildoer, or a sinner; each deserves the punishment prescribed by Islamic law appropriate to his case.

If, however, he has exercised independent scholarly judgment and erred, Allah has pardoned his error. If his mistaken judgment has caused unjust harm to the one who enjoins right and forbids wrong, he is like a judge who exercises scholarly judgment but errs, thereby causing harm to a Muslim, or like a witness or a mufti. If the error has not become apparent to the person whose judgment was mistaken, this is among the trials through which Allah tests the one who enjoins right and forbids wrong.

The Most High says:

“We have made some of you a trial for others—will you be patient? And your Lord is ever All-Seeing.”

In such a case, he is in fact free of sin, and likewise

---

## Page 7730

he is exempt from punitive requital. However, it may be argued:

Retribution, which is prescribed for intentional wrongdoing, may lapse, while liability for compensation, which applies to mistakes, remains. Thus, blood money is due for accidental killing, and compensation for property destroyed by a child or an insane person must be paid from that person’s own wealth. Although the blood money for accidental killing is payable by the killer’s kin group to assist him, the right of the person wronged by mistake must nevertheless be fulfilled. The same therefore applies to this person who has wronged another by mistake.

The answer, however, is that a distinction must be drawn between cases in which the right belongs to Allah and the human being’s right is subordinate to it, and cases in which the right belongs exclusively or predominantly to a human being. Enjoining right, forbidding wrong, and jihad come under this heading. This accords with the position of the majority, who do not require rebels to compensate the lawful side for what they have destroyed on the basis of a religious interpretation, even though their conduct was mistaken, rather than disbelief or evildoing. When the lawful side gains power over them, they do not pursue those who flee, finish off their wounded, take their women captive, or seize their property as spoils. Thus, they do not fight them in retaliation for the lives and property they have destroyed, if they have inflicted comparable losses or gained dominion over them.

It is therefore clear that retribution lapses in this case, because this belongs to the category of jihad, for which reward is due from Allah. This concerns the personal right of the one who enjoins right and forbids wrong.

As for the questioner’s words: “Should retribution be exacted from him, lest this embolden him against

---

## Page 7731

the cause of truth?”

The answer is: Whenever his conduct undermines the cause of truth, the right at stake belongs to Allah and His Messenger. He should therefore be dealt with as others in comparable circumstances, even if he has caused no harm to the one who enjoins right and forbids wrong. The course that best serves the interests recognized by the Sharia varies: sometimes it is fighting, sometimes making a truce, and sometimes refraining from fighting and making preparations without a truce. This case is similar. Yet a person’s own soul makes him think that pardoning someone who has wronged him will embolden that person against him. This is not so. Rather, it is authentically established that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“There are three things I would swear to: Allah only increases a servant in honor through pardoning; charity never diminishes wealth; and no one humbles himself before Allah without Allah raising him.”

What one should do in this matter is therefore forgo one’s personal right and ensure that Allah’s rights are fulfilled as far as possible. Allah Most High says:

“And those who, when tyranny strikes them, defend themselves.”

Ibrāhīm al-Nakhaʿī said: “They disliked being humiliated, but when they had the power to retaliate, they would pardon.”

Allah Most High says:

“They defend themselves.”

He praises them for their resolve to defend the truth and their zeal for it. They are not like those who pardon out of helplessness and humiliation; rather, that is something for which a person is blameworthy. What is praiseworthy is pardoning when one has the power to retaliate, while fulfilling one’s duty to uphold the truth—not neglecting the rights of Allah and the rights of people. And Allah Most High knows best.

---

## Page 7732

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah sanctify his soul—also said:

Section

On the words of Allah Most High:

“Until, when the messengers despaired and thought that they had been lied to, Our help came to them.”

There are two readings of this verse: one with the consonant undoubled, and the other with it doubled.

ʿĀ’ishah, may Allah be pleased with her, would recite it with the consonant doubled and reject the undoubled reading. As reported in al-Sahīh, al-Zuhrī said:

ʿUrwah informed me that ʿĀ’ishah said to him—when he asked her about the words “and thought that they had been lied to,” with the consonant undoubled—“Allah forbid! The messengers would never think that of their Lord.”

I asked, “What, then, is this help?”

“Until, when the messengers despaired”—of those among their people who had rejected them—and the messengers thought that their followers had rejected them as liars, Allah’s help came to them at that point. By my life, they knew for certain that their people had rejected them as liars; it was not a matter of conjecture.

It is also reported in al-Sahīh that Ibn Jurayj said:

I heard Ibn Abī Mulaykah say: Ibn ʿAbbās recited, “Until, when the messengers despaired and thought that they had been lied to,” with the consonant undoubled. He understood it in that sense and recited:

“Until the Messenger and those who believed with him said, ‘When

---

## Page 7733

will Allah’s help come?’ Surely Allah’s help is near.” I then met ‘Urwah and mentioned this to him. He said:

‘Ā’ishah said, “Allah forbid! By Allah, whenever Allah promised His Messenger anything, he knew it would come to pass before it happened. Rather, the messengers continued to suffer affliction until they thought and feared that those with them might reject them as liars.”

She therefore recited:

“And thought that they had been rejected as liars,”

with the consonant doubled.

Thus, ‘Ā’ishah understood the messengers’ despair as concerning the unbelievers who rejected them, and their apprehension of being rejected as liars as concerning those who believed in them. However, the other reading is firmly established and cannot be denied. Ibn ‘Abbās interpreted it accordingly, and the apparent meaning of the wording supports him. The verse that follows it concerns only the sense that help was slow in coming, expressed in their words:

“When will Allah’s help come?”

This expresses impatience at the delay and asks that help be hastened.

His words:

“And thought that they had been lied to,”

may be comparable to His words:

“When he recited, Satan cast something into his recitation, but Allah abolishes what Satan casts.”

In the Quran and Sunnah, conjecture does not mean a belief judged more likely to be true, as it does in the terminology used by a group of speculative theologians when discussing knowledge; they call a belief judged less likely to be true a misapprehension. Rather, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Beware of suspicion, for suspicion is the most false of speech.”

And Allah Most High said:

“Indeed, conjecture is no substitute for the truth.”

---

## Page 7734

Thus, a belief judged less likely to be true is conjecture and a misapprehension. Such thoughts may fall under the inward thoughts that are pardoned, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah has pardoned my community for what passes through their minds, so long as they neither speak of it nor act upon it.”

They may also fall under the whisperings described as pure faith, as is established in the Sahīh:

“The Companions said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, one of us finds within himself thoughts that he would rather be burned to a cinder or fall from heaven to earth than utter.’

He asked, ‘Have you really experienced this?’

They replied, ‘Yes.’

He said, ‘That is pure faith.’”

In another hadith:

“One of us finds within himself thoughts that he considers too dreadful to utter.”

He said:

“Praise be to Allah, who has reduced his scheming to mere whisperings.”

These thoughts that arise are of three kinds. Some are sinful and weaken faith, though they do not extinguish it. Certainty in the heart has degrees. Some are pardoned, and the person who experiences them is forgiven. Others are accompanied by pure faith.

A parallel to this is the report in the Sahīh, narrated by Ibn Shihāb, from Sa‘īd ibn al-Musayyib and Abū Salamah ibn ‘Abd al-Rahmān, from Abū Hurayrah, who said: The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“May Allah have mercy on Lot! He certainly had recourse to a mighty support. Had I remained in prison as long as Joseph did, I would have answered the one who summoned me. We have more right to doubt than Abraham, when his Lord said to him, ‘Do you not believe?’ He replied, ‘Yes, but to reassure

---

## Page 7735

my heart.’” Al-Bukhārī omitted the words “to doubt,” fearing that some people might misunderstand them.

It is well known that Abraham was a believer, as Allah tells us:

“‘Do you not believe?’ He replied, ‘Yes.’”

Yet he sought reassurance for his heart, as he said:

“But to reassure my heart.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, called this difference between faith and reassurance “doubt” in connection with the raising of the dead. The same applies to the promise of victory in this world: a person may believe in it, yet his heart may become unsettled and lack reassurance. This lack of reassurance may take the form of a suspicion that he has been lied to. There is thus reason to think that such doubt belongs to the same category.

These matters do not undermine obligatory faith, even though some of them may be sinful. The Prophets, peace be upon them, are protected from being left uncorrected in such matters, just as they are concerning their actions, in accordance with the established principles of the Sunnah and hadith.

The accounts of these matters contain a lesson for those who believe in the Prophets, for they will inevitably face trials greater than these. They should not despair when tested in this way, but should know that those better than themselves underwent such trials and that the outcome was good. Let the doubter attain certainty, the sinner repent, and the believers grow stronger in faith. These accounts thus enable people to follow the Prophets’ example properly, as Allah says:

“Indeed, in the Messenger of Allah you have an excellent example, for whoever hopes in Allah and the Last Day.”

---

## Page 7736

The Quran also contains accounts of the messengers that offer comfort and strength, so that the Prophet may follow their example in patiently enduring rejection and harm, as the Most High says:

“Messengers before you were indeed rejected, but they patiently endured rejection and persecution until Our help came to them.”

. . . (1) and for us, since he is an example in this regard. Such accounts abound in the Quran. Hence He says:

“Indeed, in their stories there is a lesson for people of understanding.”

And He says:

“Nothing is said to you that was not said to the messengers before you.”

And He says:

“So be patient, as were the messengers of firm resolve, and do not seek to hasten their punishment.”

“We relate to you all these accounts of the messengers to strengthen your heart.”

Since following their example is prescribed in both respects, what is prescribed includes repenting of sin, trusting Allah’s promise even if some suspicion enters the heart, and seeking further signs to reassure the heart. This accords with following their example and taking them as models, unlike the notion that the one being followed is absolutely protected from sin. The follower would then say: “I am not like him, for no sin is ascribed to him.” Then, when he sins, he despairs of following and emulating him, because he has committed a sin which, according to the doctrine of absolute protection from sin, invalidates such emulation. By contrast, if it is said that repentance makes amends for this, following their example remains possible despite the sin. As has been said:

“The first to sin and transgress, then repent and feel regret, was Adam, the father of humankind; and whoever takes after his father has done no wrong.”

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank in the original.

---

## Page 7737

Allah Most High has related to us the accounts of the prophets’ repentance so that we may follow their example in repenting. As for what He, glorified be He, has mentioned concerning following their example in deeds they were allowed to continue—deeds they were neither forbidden to perform nor repented of—this is what is prescribed. But deeds they were forbidden to perform and repented of are no less excluded from emulation than their abrogated practices. If following their example ceases with regard to what they were commanded or permitted to do once it is abrogated, then this applies all the more to what they were not commanded to do.

Furthermore, regarding His words:

“And they thought that they had been lied to.”

They may, through their own exercise of judgment, have supposed that what was promised entailed something it did not, only for matters to turn out otherwise. This is possible for them, as we shall explain. Thus, if someone supposes that what was promised entails something it does not, and matters then turn out otherwise, he thinks that it was a lie. Its falsity arose from his supposing that the report entailed something it did not necessarily entail. As for doubting what he knows Allah has communicated, this does not occur. We shall clarify this, if Allah Most High wills.

It should also be understood that He, glorified be He, mentions two things here: first, the messengers’ loss of hope; and second, their thinking that they had been lied to. We have already discussed the expression “thought.” As for the expression istay’asū (“lost hope”), He, glorified be He, says:

“Until, when the messengers had lost hope.”

He did not say ya’isa al-rusul (“the messengers despaired”), nor did He mention what they had lost hope of. He also uses this expression in this surah:

“When they had lost hope of him, they withdrew to confer privately. Their eldest said,

---

## Page 7738

‘Do you not know that your father took from you a solemn pledge before Allah, and that you previously failed in your duty concerning Joseph? I will not leave this land until my father gives me permission or Allah judges in my favour, and He is the best of judges.’”

It may be said that istī’ās (losing hope) is not the same as īyās (despair), for several reasons.

The first is that Joseph’s brothers had not entirely lost hope of him. Their eldest’s words:

“I will not leave this land until my father gives me permission or Allah judges in my favour, and He is the best of judges”

show that he hoped Allah would judge in his favour. His judgment here must necessarily entail our securing Joseph’s release from them; otherwise, a judgment in his favour concerning something else would have no bearing on his remaining in Egypt for that purpose.

Furthermore, despair concerns something that will not happen, yet nothing had occurred to warrant that conclusion. For they said:

“They said, ‘O governor, he has a father who is a very old man, so take one of us in his place. Indeed, we see that you are among those who do good.’”

“He said, ‘Allah forbid that we should take anyone except the one with whom we found our property! We would then indeed be wrongdoers.’”

Thus, he refused to hand him over to them. It is well known that this does not warrant certainty that he will never be handed over to them. His resolve and intention may change—and how often hearts are turned! Circumstances may also change through someone else, so that authority passes to another. The captive may also gain his freedom without his captor’s consent. Such things commonly happen when someone holds another person in his custody and says he will not hand him over. For he may

---

## Page 7739

hand him over, or the captive may escape his custody against his will. His captor may also die, leaving him free. The world is full of such cases.

The second argument

Jacob said to them:

“O my sons, go and seek news of Joseph and his brother, and do not despair of relief from Allah. Indeed, none despair of relief from Allah except the disbelieving people.”

He forbade them to despair of relief from Allah, but did not forbid istī’ās (losing hope), which was what they had experienced. He stated that none despair of relief from Allah except the disbelieving people. It is well known that they were not disbelievers. This also constitutes the third argument: he stated that

“None despair of relief from Allah except the disbelieving people.”

It is therefore impossible for prophets to despair of relief from Allah or to fall into istī’ās. Indeed, believers, so long as they remain believers, do not despair of relief from Allah. This surah mentions those who experienced istī’ās and how joy came to them afterwards, so that the believer should not despair. Hence it says:

“Indeed, in their stories is a lesson for people of understanding.”

Thus, the account of the brothers’ losing hope concerning Joseph’s brother, and that of the messengers’ losing hope, can encompass both what Ibn ‘Abbās mentioned and what ‘Ā’ishah mentioned.

The fourth argument

Istī’ās is derived from ya’s (despair) on the istif‘āl (Form X) pattern, and this pattern

---

## Page 7740

has several uses:

It may express asking another to perform an action. Thus, istikhrāj (extracting), istifhām (requesting clarification), and isti‘ lām (seeking information) involve transitive verbs. One says, “I extracted the money from someone else,” and similarly, “I requested clarification.” This cannot be the meaning of istī’ās, for no one seeks despair or calls it upon himself. Moreover, istay’asa is an intransitive verb, not a transitive one.

The istif‘āl pattern may also indicate that its subject acquires the characteristics of something else. This occurs with intransitive verbs, as in the expressions istahjara al-tīn, meaning that the clay became like stone, and istanwaqa al-fahl, meaning that the male camel became like a she-camel.

As for considering what they had lost hope of, Allah Most High specified this in the account of Joseph’s brothers, saying:

“So when they had lost hope of him.”

Regarding the messengers, however, He did not specify what they had lost hope of; rather, He described them as losing hope without qualification. No one, therefore, may restrict this to their having lost hope of what they had been promised and told would happen. Nor did Ibn ‘Abbās say this.

It has also been established that His words:

“And they thought that they had been lied to”

do not indicate, in their apparent meaning, let alone any deeper meaning, that anything resembling an equal balance between the two possibilities arose in their hearts concerning what they had been told. The Arabic word zann (supposition) does not entail this. Indeed, something may be called zann even when it is among the falsest assertions made by the person entertaining it, because it is, in itself, the less likely possibility. And the term

---

## Page 7741

“certainty,” “misgiving,” “doubt,” and the like encompasses the heart’s knowledge and action, its assent and lack of assent, and its tranquility and lack of tranquility. These matters are not merely questions of knowledge, as some people suppose, as we have explained elsewhere.

Our purpose here is to discuss His words:

“Until, when the messengers had lost hope.”

Since the statement that they had lost hope is unqualified, it is well known that when Allah promises the messengers and the believers victory without qualification—as is generally the case with His declarations—He does not specify its time, place, year, or manner. People often attribute further particulars to what has been promised, particulars not conveyed by Allah’s words, but which they have come to believe for other reasons.

Thus, some of the Companions understood the Prophet’s statement—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—that they would enter the Sacred Mosque and circumambulate it to mean that this would happen in the year of al-Hudaybiyya. For the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—had set out to perform ‘umrah, hoping to enter Mecca that year, circumambulate the House, and walk between Safā and Marwa. When they lost hope of his entering Mecca that year—after the polytheists barred their way, until the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, concluded the well-known peace treaty with them—some of them remained troubled at heart, so much so that:

“‘Umar said to the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—‘Did you not tell us that we would enter the House and circumambulate it?’

He replied, ‘Yes. But did I tell you that you would enter it this year?’

He said, ‘No.’

He replied, ‘You will indeed enter it and circumambulate it.’”

Abū Bakr told him the same thing. Abū Bakr—may Allah be pleased with him—had greater knowledge and faith than ‘Umar, until he repented

---

## Page 7742

of what he had done, even though ‘Umar—may Allah be pleased with him—was an inspired man, as stated in the authentic hadith in which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Among the nations before you there were inspired men. If there is anyone like that in my community, it is ‘Umar.”

He—may Allah be pleased with him—was indeed an inspired man, upon whose tongue and heart Allah had established the truth. Yet the distinction of affirming the truth, with its more complete following of the Messenger and fuller knowledge of and faith in what he brought, is of a higher rank than this.

For this reason, al-Siddīq (the steadfast affirmer of truth), devoted to following the prophetic traditions, was the best of this community. He was a teacher to ‘Umar and a guide to the inspired individual among them who receives inspiration and communication from his Lord. Thus Abū Bakr taught and guided ‘Umar when he asked him:

“Did he tell you that you would enter it this year?”

He replied, “No.”

He said, “You will indeed come to it and circumambulate it.”

Al-Siddīq thus explained to him that the Prophet’s promise—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—was unrestricted, not tied to any particular time. His efforts and intention to enter that year did not necessarily mean that this was what his announcement referred to. He might intend something that does not happen, while something else happens instead. It is not a requirement of prophethood that events unfold as he intends. Rather, part of the fullness of his Lord’s favor upon him is that He restrains him from what he intends and directs him toward something else more beneficial than what he intended. Thus, the Treaty of al-Hudaybiyya was more beneficial to the believers than their entering that year. This is unlike what the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—reports: it is true, and what he reports must necessarily occur and be fulfilled.

---

## Page 7743

This distinction also applies to the Prophet’s suppositions, as he said regarding the pollination of date palms:

“I merely formed a supposition, so do not hold me accountable for a supposition. But when I tell you something from Allah, I will never lie about Allah.”

Thus, when ‘Umar and others lost hope of entering that year, they were losing hope of something they supposed had been promised, although it had not been promised. It is not impossible for prophets to suppose something of this kind and for matters to turn out otherwise. They may suppose that a promise made to them specifies certain particulars and characteristics, yet matters may not be as they supposed. They then lose hope of what they supposed the promise meant, not of what the promise actually specified. Thus, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“‘I saw that Abū Jahl had embraced Islam.’ When Khālid embraced Islam, they thought he was the one meant; then, when ‘Ikrima embraced Islam, it became known that he was the one.”

Muslim related in his Sahīh:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, passed by some people pollinating date palms and said, ‘If you did not do this, they would thrive.’ The palms then produced poorly developed dates. He passed by them and asked, ‘What is wrong with your date palms?’

“They replied, ‘You said such-and-such.’

“He said, ‘You know better about your worldly affairs.’”

It is also related from Mūsā ibn Talha, from his father, Talha ibn ‘Ubayd Allah, who said:

“I passed with the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, by some people at the tops of date palms. He asked, ‘What are these people doing?’ He was told, ‘They are pollinating them: they put the male into the female, and it is pollinated.’ The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘I do not think that this does any good.’ They were informed of this, so they stopped doing it.

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was informed of this and said, ‘If that benefits them, let them do it, for I

---

## Page 7744

merely formed a supposition, so do not hold me accountable for a supposition. But when I tell you something from Allah, accept it, for I will never lie about Allah.’”

Since the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, commands us to accept whatever he tells us from Allah, because he will never lie about Allah, he himself has all the more reason to abide by what he tells us from Allah: he is the most mindful of Allah among us and knows best what must be guarded against. Thus, if Allah informs him of a promise, we must believe it, and his belief in it is greater than ours. We have no right to doubt it, and he—may my father be his ransom—has all the more reason not to doubt it.

He may, however, form a supposition, as he said:

“I merely formed a supposition, so do not hold me accountable for a supposition.”

Even if he reports it without qualification, his basis is supposition, as when he said in the hadith of Dhū al-Yadayn:

“I have neither shortened the prayer nor forgotten.”

He may suppose something, and then Allah makes the truth of the matter clear. This happened on various occasions, as with the words of the Most High:

“If a wicked person brings you news, verify it.”

This was revealed concerning al-Walīd ibn ‘Uqba, whom the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had appointed to a task. Believing his report to be true, the Prophet was about to march against the people concerned, until Allah revealed this verse.

Likewise, concerning the incident involving Banū Ubayriq, Allah revealed:

“Indeed, We have sent down to you the Book in truth so that you may judge between people by what Allah has shown you. And do not advocate for the treacherous.”

This was when some people came, leaving aside the thief who had been stealing and bringing forward an innocent man instead;

---

## Page 7745

so the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, believed them to be telling the truth, until the matter later became clear.

In the hadith concerning the shortening of the prayer, he said:

“I have neither forgotten nor has the prayer been shortened.” They replied, “Yes, you have forgotten.”

He had indeed forgotten, and had spoken according to his supposition and belief, until the matter later became clear.

It is also narrated that he said:

“Indeed, I forget so that I may establish the Sunnah.”

Moreover, Allah’s words in the Quran:

“Our Lord, do not hold us accountable if we forget or make a mistake”

include both the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his community. For He says at the beginning of these verses:

“The Messenger believes in what has been sent down to him from his Lord, as do the believers. All believe in Allah, His angels, His Books, and His messengers,”

and the verses that follow.

In Sahīh Muslim, ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Īsā al-Ansārī narrates from Sa‘īd ibn Jubayr, from Ibn ‘Abbās, who said:

“While Gabriel was sitting with the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, he heard a creaking sound above him. He raised his head and said, ‘This is a gate in heaven that has been opened today; it has never been opened before today.’ An angel descended through it, and he said, ‘This is an angel who has come down to earth; he has never come down before today.’ The angel offered greetings and said, ‘Rejoice in two lights you have been given, which no prophet before you was given: the Opening of the Book and the concluding verses of Sūrat al-Baqara. You will not recite any part of them without being granted what it contains.’”

In Sahīh Muslim, Ādam narrates from Sa‘īd ibn Jubayr, from Ibn ‘Abbās, who said:

“When this verse was revealed: ‘Whether you reveal what is within yourselves or conceal it, Allah will call you to account for it,’ it troubled their hearts as nothing had before. Then the Prophet said:

---

## Page 7746

may Allah bless him and grant him peace: ‘Say: “We hear, we obey, and we submit.”’ He said: Allah then instilled faith in their hearts and revealed:

‘Allah does not burden a soul beyond what it can bear. It will have the good it has earned and bear the evil it has committed,’

and the subsequent verses, up to His words, ‘or make a mistake.’ Allah said, ‘I have done so.’ And so on to the end of the surah, He said, ‘I have done so.’”

In Sahīh Muslim, al-‘Alā’ ibn ‘Abd al-Rahmān narrates from his father, from Abū Hurayra, who said:

“When the following was revealed to the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

‘To Allah belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on earth. Whether you reveal what is within yourselves or conceal it, Allah will call you to account for it,’

this weighed heavily upon the Companions of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. They knelt down and said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, we have been charged with deeds within our capacity: prayer, fasting, fighting in Allah’s cause, and charity. But this verse has now been revealed to you, and we cannot bear it.’

The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘Do you wish to say what the People of the Book said: “We hear and we disobey”? Rather, say: “We hear and we obey. We seek Your forgiveness, our Lord, and to You is the final return.”’

When the people recited it and their tongues readily uttered it, Allah, Mighty and Majestic, revealed immediately thereafter:

‘The Messenger believes in what has been sent down to him from his Lord,’

up to His words, ‘and to You is the final return.’

When they did that, He, glorified be He, abrogated it, and Allah revealed:

‘Allah does not burden a soul beyond what it can bear,’

up to His words, ‘before us.’ He said, ‘Yes.’

‘And do not burden us with what we have no strength to bear.’ He said, ‘Yes.’

And so on to the end of the surah, He said, ‘Yes.’”

The position held by the majority of hadith scholars and jurists is that it is possible for them to err in

---

## Page 7747

independent judgment; however, they are not left uncorrected. If this applies to commands and prohibitions, then what of statements of fact?

In the two Sahīhs, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is reported to have said:

“You bring your disputes before me, and some of you may be more eloquent in presenting their case than others. I judge only according to what I hear, taking the speaker to be truthful. If I award anyone something that rightfully belongs to his brother, he must not take it, for I am only allotting him a piece of the Fire.”

What Allah truly promises the prophets and believers is itself something they do not doubt, as He, Most High, says in the account of Noah:

“And Noah called upon his Lord,”

to the end of the verse.

Such an assumption may arise from Satan’s insinuation mentioned in His words:

“We never sent before you any messenger or prophet,”

through to His words:

“a straight path.”

We have discussed this passage elsewhere. There are two well-known views concerning it, following agreement that tamannī here means recitation and reading, as held by the Quranic commentators among the early generations. This is likewise its meaning in His words:

“And among them are unlettered people who know the Scripture only through recitation, and they do nothing but conjecture.”

As for those who interpret the prohibition as referring to wishing in the heart, that calls for a separate discussion, even if it is said that the verse encompasses both senses. The first, however, is the familiar and well-known interpretation in Quranic exegesis. It is the apparent meaning of the Quran and is certainly intended by the verse, because He says thereafter:

“But Allah abolishes what Satan casts in; then Allah makes His verses firm. And Allah is All-Knowing, All-Wise,”

“so that He may make what Satan casts in a trial for those in whose hearts is disease.”

None of this can take place merely within the heart if

---

## Page 7748

the Prophet does not utter it; it may, however, occur in a supposition he expresses, some of it concerning date palms and the like. This accords with what we have stated.

Since tamannī must include spoken words, there are two views concerning it:

The first is that what Satan casts in reaches the listeners’ hearing, but the Messenger does not utter it. This is the view of those who interpret the verse as precluding the possibility of anything being cast into his speech.

The second—the prevailing view among the early generations and their followers—is that the casting occurs within the recitation itself, as the verse and its context indicate in several ways, and as numerous transmitted reports relate. There is nothing objectionable in this unless he is left uncorrected. But if Allah abolishes what Satan casts in and makes His verses firm, there is nothing objectionable in it. Nor does it constitute an error or mistake in conveying the message unless he is left uncorrected.

There is no doubt that, in conveying the message, he is protected from being left in error, as he said:

“When I tell you anything from Allah, accept it, for I will never lie about Allah.”

Otherwise, no binding proof could be established through him. His being Allah’s Messenger necessarily means that he is truthful in what he reports from Allah, and truthfulness excludes both lying and error in such reports. If he could err in what he reports from Allah and be left uncorrected, not everything he reported would be from Allah. Those who ruled out anything being cast into his conveyance of the message sought to avoid this and intended

---

## Page 7749

good, and they did well in this regard. Yet they may be answered: Satan cast something in, then Allah made His verses firm; there is nothing objectionable in that. In some respects, this resembles abrogation for someone who has received a command or prohibition. He is then certain of, and accepts, the withdrawal of words that slipped from his tongue; this is no greater a matter than his announcing their abrogation. Thus, concerning abrogation, He said:

“And indeed, it was difficult except for those whom Allah guided.”

Their supposition that they had been called liars follows from their understanding of the promise. This is possible and entails nothing objectionable, provided they are not left uncorrected. This is a sound interpretation, consistent with the apparent meaning of the verse and with all the other principles established by Quranic verses and hadiths.

What confirms this is that promises and warnings are no greater a matter than commands and prohibitions. If, concerning commands and prohibitions, they may suppose something and subsequently discover that the matter is otherwise, then this is all the more possible concerning promises and warnings.

Indeed, concerning commands and prohibitions, when they rely on the presumption that the prior state continues, they do not thereby suppose anything contrary to the actual state of affairs. Obligation and prohibition are established only through divine address; if they deny their existence before that address, their belief accords with the actual state of affairs. As for a promise, if they have not been informed of it, they may suppose that it does not exist. Thus Abraham, Allah’s close friend, supposed that his father might be forgiven and therefore sought forgiveness for him, though we were forbidden to follow his example. Likewise, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to Abū Tālib:

“I shall certainly seek forgiveness for you unless I am forbidden to do so.”

He even asked his Lord for permission to seek forgiveness for his mother, but he was not granted permission for

---

## Page 7750

that. He even offered funeral prayers for the hypocrites before being forbidden to do so, hoping that they would be forgiven, until Allah, Mighty and Majestic, revealed:

“It is not for the Prophet and those who believe to seek forgiveness for the polytheists,”

through to His words:

“tenderhearted and forbearing.”

Concerning the hypocrites, He said:

“And never offer funeral prayer for any of them who dies,”

to the end of the verse. He also said:

“It makes no difference to them whether you seek forgiveness for them or do not seek forgiveness for them: Allah will never forgive them.”

If, then, he offered funeral prayers for the hypocrites and sought forgiveness for them, hoping that they would be forgiven, before learning this, this explains why scholars permitted the transmission, concerning promises and warnings, of hadiths not known to be false, even if their chains of transmission were weak. This differs from matters of commands and prohibitions, in which only reports established as true may be relied upon. For, concerning promises and warnings, if a report could be true and could also prove false, it is impermissible to deny it—especially without knowledge—just as it is impermissible to affirm its authenticity categorically without knowledge, since there is nothing objectionable in it.

People’s origins (1). The wording specifies the promise and warning. It is therefore impermissible to prevent this by prohibiting transmission of the hadith if it could be true, for that would invalidate something true, which is impermissible. This is why the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Relate accounts from the Children of Israel

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) Thus in the original.

---

## Page 7751

and there is no harm in doing so.”

This subject—the subject of promises and warnings—is expressed in the Book through unqualified designations: believers, the patient, those who strive in Allah’s cause, and those who do good. How many people imagine themselves to be among those covered by the promise! As they understand the wording, they suppose themselves to possess the qualities that bring one within the promise; the issue is not their belief in the truth of the promise itself.

This is illustrated by His words:

“We will certainly support Our messengers and those who believe, both in the life of this world and on the Day when the witnesses stand forth.”

And the two verses beginning with His words:

“Our word has already gone forth to Our servants, the messengers.”

A person may suppose that he or someone else possesses the fullness of faith that merits victory, and thus belongs to Allah’s victorious forces, when the reality is otherwise. Conversely, some of the promised victory may occur without being recognized as part of what was promised. Mistaken suppositions in understanding these matters are exceedingly common—far more common than in matters of commands and prohibitions, despite the many errors that occur there. Only Allah Most High can encompass all the errors made in this regard.
