This applies to all human beings. The prophets, however—may Allah’s blessings and peace be upon them—are not allowed to persist in error; rather, the truth is made clear to them. Those who are not prophets may never have this made clear to them in this world. Hence the Quran frequently commands His Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to affirm the truth of the promise

---

## Page 7752

and to have faith, and to do what this requires: remain patient until the appointed time comes and seek forgiveness so that sins may be removed and he may truly possess the quality to which the promise applies.

As Allah Most High says:

“So be patient. Surely Allah’s promise is true. And do not let those who lack certainty unsettle you.”

And He Most High says:

“So be patient. Surely Allah’s promise is true. Whether We show you some of what We promise them or cause you to die…”

—to the end of the verse. The verses concerning this subject are numerous and well known. And Allah Most High knows best.

---

## Page 7753

Surah al-Ra‘d (Thunder)

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—said:

Section on the words of Allah Most High:

“And they have ascribed partners to Allah. Say, ‘Name them.’”

One interpretation is: Give them true names whose meanings entitle them to be partners with Him and to be worshipped. If you cannot, your claim is disproved.

Another interpretation is: If you call them gods, then call them by a name belonging to God, such as the Creator or the Provider. If these names are falsely applied to them, then so too is the name “gods.” Many commentators have touched upon its meaning, yet they have neither healed the ailing nor quenched the thirsty, even though what they have said is correct.

Reflecting on what precedes and follows this verse will reveal its true meaning to you. For He, glorified is He, says:

“Is He who watches over every soul and what it has earned…”

This is a question

---

## Page 7754

intended to elicit acknowledgment, establishing the proof against them and denying any deity alongside Allah, who watches over every soul and what it has earned through His knowledge, power, and recompense in this world and the Hereafter. He watches over each soul, records its deeds, and recompenses it for whatever good or evil it has earned. If you claim that those beings are His partners, then call them by the names of the One who watches over every soul and what it has earned. For He, glorified is He, is called the Ever-Living, the Self-Subsisting Sustainer, the Giver of Life, the Bringer of Death, the All-Hearing, the All-Seeing, the One who has no need of anything besides Him, while everything needs Him and owes its existence to Him. Do your gods deserve any of these names? If they truly are gods, then call them by one of these names. Yet that would be a blatant falsehood. Once it is established that these names do not apply to them, the falsity of their names becomes clear, as does the falsity of what those names signify.

On the other hand, suppose they call them by names that truly apply to them: stones and other inanimate objects; the names of animals they have worshipped besides Allah, such as cattle and others; the names of devils they have associated with Allah, majestic and exalted is He; or the names of celestial bodies subjected to the Lord’s commands. The names encompassing them all are those of created beings: needy, governed, and subjugated. The same applies to human beings worshipping one another. These are their true names, and they invalidate their divinity, because the names inseparable from divinity cannot possibly apply to them. Thus, it becomes clear that calling them gods is itself among the strongest proofs that their divinity is false and that they cannot possibly be partners with Allah, mighty and majestic is He.

---

## Page 7755

Surah al-Hijr

Shaykh al-Islām Ahmad ibn ‘Abd al-Halīm ibn ‘Abd al-Salām ibn Taymiyyah al-Harrānī—may Allah sanctify his soul, illuminate his grave, and have mercy on him—said:

Section

On three related passages, similar in wording and meaning, whose meaning eludes most people. Allah Most High says:

“He said, ‘This is a straight path to Me.’”

“Indeed, you have no authority over My servants, except those of the misguided who follow you.”

And He Most High says:

“The straight path leads to Allah, while some paths deviate.”

And He Most High says:

“Indeed, guidance rests with Us.”

“And indeed, to Us belong the Hereafter and this worldly life.”

---

## Page 7756

Thus, the wording of these verses indicates that the path of guidance rests with Allah. Abū al-Faraj ibn al-Jawzī mentioned three interpretations of the first verse, whereas for the other two verses he mentioned only one interpretation.

Concerning that verse, he said:

“They held three views concerning the meaning of these words.

First: By ‘this,’ He means sincere devotion. The meaning is therefore: ‘Sincere devotion is a straight path to Me,’ with ‘upon Me’ meaning ‘to Me.’

Second: This is a path whose travelers must pass before Me, for I lie in wait and shall recompense them for their deeds. This is expressed as a threat, just as you might say to someone with whom you are disputing, ‘Your path passes by me.’ It is thus like His saying:

‘Indeed, your Lord is ever watchful.’

Third: This is a path whose straightness rests with Me; that is, I guarantee its straightness through clarification and proof.”

He said: “Qatādah and Ya‘qūb read: ‘This is a lofty path,’ meaning ‘elevated.’”

I say: These three interpretations were mentioned by earlier scholars, such as al-Tha‘labī, al-Wāhidī and al-Baghawī, who also mentioned a fourth interpretation. They said—the wording is al-Baghawī’s, whose work is an abridgment of al-Tha‘labī’s:

---

## Page 7757

“Al-Hasan said: ‘It means a straight path to Me.’ Mujāhid said: ‘The truth returns to Me, and its path leads to Him without turning aside to anything else.’ Al-Akhfash said: ‘It means that pointing out the straight path rests with Me.’ Al-Kisā’ī said: ‘This is a threat and a warning, as when a man says to someone with whom he is disputing, “Your path passes by me”—meaning, “You will not escape me”—as Allah Most High says, “Indeed, your Lord is ever watchful.”’ It has also been said: ‘It means that its straightness rests with Me, through clarification, proof, the granting of success, and guidance.’”

Thus, they mentioned the three interpretations and also al-Akhfash’s interpretation: “Pointing out the straight path rests with Me.” This resembles the last interpretation, but there is a distinction between them. One says: “Its straightness rests with Me, through establishing the evidence; whoever follows it is therefore on a straight path.” The other says: “It rests with Me to direct creation to it by establishing the proofs.”

Both interpretations therefore affirm that He has made the straight path clear by setting forth the evidence. However, one makes pointing out the path His responsibility, while the other makes its straightness His responsibility—that is, making its straightness clear. The two necessarily entail one another. This is why—and Allah knows best—Abū al-Faraj did not count it as a fourth interpretation.

They also mentioned the alternative reading reported from Ya‘qūb and others, meaning “lofty.” Al-Baghawī said: “Some expressed its meaning as: ‘Too lofty to be reached, too straight to be bent.’”

---

## Page 7758

I say:

The correct view is that of the leading scholars of the early generations—Mujāhid and others like him—for they were the most knowledgeable about the meanings of the Quran, especially Mujāhid. He said: “I recited the Quran to Ibn ‘Abbās from beginning to end, stopping him at every verse and asking him about it.” Al-Thawrī said: “If an explanation of the Quran reaches you from Mujāhid, that is sufficient for you.” Leading scholars such as al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad, al-Bukhārī, and others rely on his commentary. Indeed, most of the Quranic commentary that al-Bukhārī quotes in his Sahīh comes from him. Al-Hasan al-Basrī was the most knowledgeable of the Successors in Basra.

The explanation they quoted from Mujāhid is reliably established from him. Scholars such as Ibn Abī Hātim and others transmitted it through Warqā’, from Ibn Abī Najīh, from Mujāhid, concerning His words:

“This is a straight path leading to Me.”

He explained: “The truth returns to Allah, and its path leads to Him without turning aside to anything else.” He also reported that Qatādah explained it according to his own reading—he read it as ‘alayya—saying: “That is, lofty and straight.”

Likewise, Ibn Abī Hātim reported the early generations’ explanations of the verse in Sūrat al-Nahl. Through Warqā’, from Ibn Abī Najīh, he narrated that Mujāhid explained His words, “the straight path,” by saying: “The path of truth leads to Allah.” He said: “It was also narrated from al-Suddī that he said: ‘Islam.’” ‘Atā’ said: “It is the path to Paradise.”

These explanations of this verse—those of Mujāhid, al-Suddī, and ‘Atā’—are like the explanations given by Mujāhid and al-Hasan of the other verse. Ibn Abī Hātim also quoted, from al-‘Awfī’s commentary transmitted from Ibn ‘Abbās, concerning His words:

---

## Page 7759

“And it is for Allah to show the straight path.”

He explained: “It is for Allah to provide clarification—that is, to make guidance and misguidance clear.” Ibn Abī Hātim mentioned two interpretations of this verse, whereas for the verse in Sūrat al-Hijr he mentioned only Mujāhid’s interpretation.

For the verse in Sūrat al-Nahl, Ibn al-Jawzī mentioned only this second interpretation, attributing it to al-Zajjāj. He said:

“‘And it is for Allah to show the straight path.’ Qasd means the straightness of a path. A path is described as qasd or qāsid (direct) when it leads you to your intended destination. Al-Zajjāj said: ‘The meaning is that it is for Allah to make the straight path clear and to call people to it through arguments and proofs.’”

Likewise, al-Tha‘labī, al-Baghawī, and others mentioned only this interpretation, though they expressed it in two ways. Al-Baghawī said:

“It means distinguishing the path of guidance from misguidance. It is also said to mean making the truth clear through signs and proofs.”

He said:

“Qasd is the straight path. ‘And some paths deviate’ means that some paths turn away from straightness and are crooked. Thus, the straight path is the religion of Islam, while the deviating paths are Judaism, Christianity, and all other religions of disbelief.”

---

## Page 7760

Jābir ibn ‘Abd Allāh said:

“Qasd al-sabīl (the straight path) means explaining the religious laws and obligations.”

‘Abd Allāh ibn al-Mubārak and Sahl ibn ‘Abd Allāh said:

“‘The straight path’ is the Sunnah, while ‘And some paths deviate’ refers to whims and religious innovations. The evidence for this is His saying, Exalted is He: ‘Indeed, this is My straight path, so follow it, and do not follow other paths, for they will lead you away from His path.’”

However, al-Baghawī also mentioned the other interpretation, citing it from al-Farrā’ in his commentary on the words of the Exalted, “Indeed, it is for Us to give guidance,” as will be discussed below. Thus, in discussing the three verses, he mentioned both interpretations, following his predecessors such as al-Tha‘labī and others. Al-Mahdawī mentioned two of the three interpretations concerning the first verse; concerning the second, he mentioned what al-‘Awfī reported and another interpretation.

He said:

“His words, ‘He said: This is a straight path incumbent upon Me,’ mean: in accordance with My command and will. It is also said that this is a threat, as when someone says, ‘Your road leads to me, and to me you will ultimately return.’”

Concerning His words, “And it is for Allah to show the straight path,” he said:

“Ibn ‘Abbās said: ‘It means making guidance distinct from misguidance.’ It is also said that the path is Islam. ‘And some paths deviate’ means that some paths turn away from the truth. Another interpretation is that the meaning is ‘And there are paths that deviate from it’—that is, from the path—so min (from/among) is used in the sense of ‘an (away from).

“It is also said that qasd al-sabīl means your going and returning, and that ‘the path’ is singular in form but plural in meaning.”

---

## Page 7761

I say:

This is the view of one of the later scholars, who took qasd to mean “intention”: that is, your intention to take the path as you go and return rests with Him. These are the words of someone who has not understood the verse. For “the straight path” means the upright path; in other words, “Upon Him is the straight path.” “Path” is a generic noun, which is why He says:

“And some paths deviate.”

That is, upon Him is the straight among paths, while some paths deviate. Thus, He joins the term to a generic noun in a genitive construction relating a particular kind to its genus: “the straight among paths,” just as you say “a garment of khazz fabric.” This is why He says:

“And some paths deviate.”

As for anyone who supposes that the implied wording is “your intending to take the path,” this fails to accord with the verse’s wording and construction in several respects.

Regarding the verse in Sūrat al-Hijr, Ibn ‘Atiyya mentioned only al-Kisā’ī’s interpretation, which is the weakest of the interpretations. He mentioned the correct meaning as an explanation of the alternative reading, noting that a group of the early generations read the words as “exalted and straight,” with the first word signifying loftiness and elevation.

He said:

“On this reading, ‘this’ refers to sincerity. When Iblīs excepted those who were sincere, Allah said to him: ‘This sincerity is an exalted, straight path; you cannot reach those who follow it through your attempts to mislead them.’”

He said:

“The majority read it as ‘a straight path incumbent upon Me.’ On this reading, ‘this’ refers to the division of people into those who go astray and those who are sincere. When Iblīs distinguished these two

---

## Page 7762

categories, Allah said, ‘This is a path incumbent upon Me,’ meaning, ‘This is a matter whose outcome rests with Me.’ The Arabs say, ‘Your way in this matter lies through so-and-so,’ meaning that consideration of your case ultimately rests with him. This is akin to His saying:

‘Indeed, your Lord is ever watchful.’”

He said:

“On this reading, the verse is a statement that contains a warning.”

I say:

This interpretation has not been transmitted from any scholar of Quranic exegesis, whether concerning this verse or its counterpart. Al-Kisā’ī only proposed it because he had difficulty understanding the verse’s meaning as understood by the early generations and indicated by the context and parallel passages. Nor does Arab usage support this interpretation. Although a man may say to someone who threatens and menaces him, “Your path passes by me,” he does not say, “Your path is straight.”

Moreover, a warning is directed at the wrongdoer, not at those who are sincere. How, then, can His word “this” be “a reference to the division of people into those who go astray and those who are sincere,” when the path of one group differs from that of the other? One group has followed the straight path that leads to Allah, while the other has followed the deviating path.

Furthermore, someone only says to another, “Your path passes by me,” as a threat when he cannot get at him at that moment, but the other will pass his way of his own accord, when he will have him within his power—just as the people of

---

## Page 7763

Medina used to threaten the people of Mecca by saying, “Your route passes through our territory,” when the latter threatened them for having sheltered Muhammad and his Companions. Thus, when Sa‘d ibn Mu‘ādh went to Mecca, Abū Jahl said to him, “I will not have you circumambulating the House in safety when you have sheltered the renegades and claimed that you would support them!” Sa‘d replied, “If you prevent me from this, I will deny you something whose loss will hurt you far more—your route past Medina,” or words to that effect. He pointed out that their trading route to Syria passed through their territory, where they would then be able to retaliate against them.

Such a meaning cannot be applied to Allah Most High, for Allah has power over His servants wherever they may be. As the jinn said:

“We have realized that we can never escape Allah on earth, nor can we escape Him by fleeing.”

And He said:

“You cannot escape Him on earth.”

If the Arabs do indeed use the expression he mentioned, “Your way in this matter lies through so-and-so,” meaning that your affair ultimately rests with him, then this accords with the interpretation of Mujāhid and others among the early generations. As Mujāhid said, “The truth leads back to Allah, and its path leads to Him, without turning aside to anything else.”

The path of truth thus leads to Allah. It is the straight path concerning which Allah said:

“This is a straight path to Me.”

The other reading has likewise been interpreted in this sense. Thus, in both readings, the path is this straight path which Allah commanded the believers

---

## Page 7764

to ask Him for in their prayers, saying:

“Guide us to the straight path—the path of those upon whom You have bestowed favor, not of those who have incurred Your anger, nor of those who have gone astray.”

It is the path He enjoined in His words:

“And this is My path, which is straight, so follow it. Do not follow other ways, lest they lead you away from His way. This He has enjoined upon you, so that you may fear Him.”

His word “this” refers to what was mentioned earlier in His words:

“Except Your chosen servants among them.”

Thus, the servants’ worship of Him, devoting their religion sincerely to Him alone, is a path that points to Him, and it is a straight path. This is why He said immediately afterward:

“Indeed, you have no authority over My servants.”

When explaining the other verse, Ibn ‘Atiyyah stated that this was the meaning of this verse, citing it as evidence, even though he had not mentioned this meaning in his commentary on this verse itself. Thus, he recognized this meaning through his innate disposition. Yet when he explained the verse itself, he mentioned that other interpretation, seemingly because it happened to be the one he had seen someone else give there.

He said—may Allah have mercy on him:

“His words, ‘It is for Allah to show the straight way, though some ways deviate,’ also describe one of Allah Most High’s greatest blessings. That is, it is for Allah to set the path of guidance straight and make it clear, by establishing proofs and sending messengers. This is the interpretation adopted by the commentators.”

He said:

“It may also mean that whoever follows the straight way has a path leading to Allah, and that is his ultimate destination. This would then be like His words, ‘This is a path to Me

---

## Page 7765

straight,’ and would be the opposite of the Prophet’s words—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—‘And evil does not lead to You,’ meaning that it does not lead to Your mercy. A ‘straight way’ means a clear, straight, and short route. Hence the line of a poet composing in the rajaz metre:

Far from the course of the straight way.”

He said: “The definite article in ‘the way’ refers to a particular way: the way prescribed by divine law. It does not refer to ways in general; had it done so, none of them would have deviated. His words, ‘And some ways deviate,’ mean the ways of the Jews, Christians, and others, such as idolaters. The pronoun in ‘among them’ refers to the ways implied by the verse’s meaning, as though He had said, ‘Among the ways are those that deviate.’ Thus, the pronoun refers back to them even though they have not been explicitly mentioned, because they are implicit in the meaning of the word ‘way.’”

He said: “It is also possible that the pronoun in ‘among them’ refers to the aforementioned way prescribed by divine law, with ‘among’ indicating a part of the whole. What is meant would then be the misguided sects within Muhammad’s community—as though He had said: ‘Among the byways and offshoots of this way are some that deviate.’”

(I say: The way of those who follow religious innovations deviates and lies outside the straight path in those matters in which they have introduced innovations. It cannot be said that this is part of the divinely prescribed way.

---

## Page 7766

As for his statement that His words, “the straight way,” refer to the way prescribed by divine law—the way of guidance and the straight path—and that, were the definite article generic, none of the ways would deviate: this is one of the two interpretations of the verse, but it is the weaker one. The correct interpretation is the other: “the way” is a generic term, but only the straight way among them leads to Allah, and that is a single way. Since the term is generic, He says, “And among them are ways that deviate.” The pronoun thus refers back to what has been mentioned without any strained interpretation.

His statement, “Were it generic, none of the ways would deviate,” is incorrect. Not all of them lead to Him; rather, only the straight way among them does—the way of guidance. A deviating way is not straight. It seems he thought that, if the term were generic, the straight course of every way would lead to Him. That is not so. Rather, only one way leads to Him: the straight path, which points to Him. All the others are ways of Satan, as He says:

“This is My straight path, so follow it, and do not follow other ways, lest they lead you away from His way.”

He did well—may Allah have mercy on him—to mention this possible interpretation and illustrate it with His words:

“This is a straight path leading to Me.”

As for the verse in Sūrat al-Layl, His words, “Indeed, upon Us is guidance,” Ibn ‘Atiyyah likened it to this verse, but explained it according to the first interpretation, saying:

---

## Page 7767

“Then the Exalted informs us that guiding all people rests upon Him—that is, acquainting them with all the ways and granting them understanding—as He says, ‘And upon Allah is the straight way.’ Each person then earns what has been decreed for him. This guidance does not mean leading people to faith; were that the case, no unbeliever would exist.”

I say:

This is what Ibn al-Jawzī mentioned, also attributing it to al-Zajjāj. Al-Zajjāj said: “It is for Us to distinguish the way of guidance from the way of misguidance.” This interpretation is reliably transmitted from Qatādah. ‘Abd ibn Humayd narrated it, saying: “Yūnus narrated to us, from Shaybān, from Qatādah, concerning ‘Indeed, upon Us is guidance’: ‘It is for Us to explain what He has made lawful and unlawful, and what constitutes obedience and disobedience to Him.’”

Ibn Abī Hātim likewise narrated it in Sa‘īd’s commentary, from Qatādah, concerning His words, “Indeed, upon Us is guidance.” He said: “It is for Allah to make things clear—to explain what He has made lawful and unlawful, and what constitutes obedience and disobedience to Him.” However, Qatādah explained that this is the clarification with which Allah sent His messengers and revealed His books, thereby making clear what He has made lawful and unlawful, and what constitutes obedience and disobedience to Him.

As for al-Tha‘labī, al-Wāhidī, al-Baghawī and others, they mentioned both interpretations and added others. They said—the wording is al-Baghawī’s:

---

## Page 7768

“‘Indeed, upon Us is guidance’ means clarification. Al-Zajjāj said: ‘It is for Us to distinguish the way of guidance from the way of misguidance.’ This is also Qatādah’s view; he said: ‘It is for Allah to explain what He has made lawful and unlawful.’ Al-Farrā’ said: ‘It means that whoever follows guidance, his path leads to Allah, as in His words, “And upon Allah is the straight way.” That is, whoever seeks Allah is on the straight path.’ He said: ‘It has also been said that it means, “Indeed, upon Us are guidance and leading astray,” as in His words, “In Your hand is all good.”’”

I say: This is one of the newly introduced interpretations that were not known among the early generations, as are other interpretations of this kind. For they said that its meaning is: ‘In Your hand are good and evil,’ whereas the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, says in the authentic hadith:

“All good is in Your hands, and evil is not ascribed to You.”

Allah Most High is the Creator of everything—nothing occurs in His dominion except what He wills—and divine decree is true. Yet understanding the Quran, putting everything in its proper place, and explaining the Lord’s wisdom and justice while believing in divine decree is the way of the Companions and those who followed them in righteousness.

Al-Mahdawī mentioned the three interpretations, saying: “Indeed, upon Us is guidance

---

## Page 7769

and misguidance, with the latter omitted. Qatādah’s interpretation is: ‘It is for Us to explain what is lawful and unlawful.’ It has also been said: ‘The meaning is that it is for Us to guide whoever follows the path of guidance.’”

I say: This is al-Farrā’s view, but al-Farrā’s wording conveys it more clearly. It has thus become clear that most of the early scholars interpreted the three verses to mean that the straight path leads only to Allah. Some interpreted them to mean that it is for Him to explain the straight path. The first meaning is agreed upon among Muslims.

As for the second, some may say: “Nothing is incumbent upon Allah—neither this clarification nor that.” For they disagree over whether He has imposed obligations upon Himself, as in His words:

“Your Lord has prescribed mercy for Himself,”

and His words:

“And it was incumbent upon Us to help the believers,”

and His words:

“There is no creature on earth but that its provision rests with Allah.”

If it is incumbent upon Him to distinguish guidance from misguidance and to explain what He has made lawful and unlawful, and what constitutes obedience and disobedience to Him, then this accords with the view of those who say that sending messengers is incumbent upon Him and obligatory for Him, since such clarification can only be achieved through this. This relates to another fundamental principle: that everything He does is necessary on His part

---

## Page 7770

, made necessary by His will and wisdom, and that whatever He wills occurs, while whatever He does not will does not occur. Whatever He wills must therefore exist, and whatever He does not will cannot exist. A fuller discussion of this belongs elsewhere. Whether these verses indicate this meaning, however, is open to question.

As for the agreed-upon meaning, it is certainly intended in all three verses: through them He directs people to the straight path—the direct way, which is guidance and points only to Him. This is the truth: its course leads to Allah without turning aside from Him.

The difficulty arose, however, because He said “upon Us,” using the preposition denoting elevation, rather than “to Us.” Ordinarily, when someone is indicated as the destination, one says, “This road leads to so-and-so”; but when passing someone along the way, one says, “Our route goes by so-and-so.” He expressed this meaning using the preposition denoting elevation. This is one of the beauties of the Quran, whose wonders never cease and of which scholars never tire.

All creatures ultimately return to Allah, whichever path they follow, as He, Most High, says:

“O man, you are indeed toiling toward your Lord, and you will meet Him.”

And He says:

“To Allah is the final return.”

“Indeed, to Us is their return.”

That is, to Us they will return. And He says:

---

## Page 7771

“It is He who takes your souls by night and knows what you have earned by day, then awakens you during it, so that an appointed term may be fulfilled. Then to Him is your return, and He will inform you of what you used to do.”

“He is the Subduer, above His servants, and He sends guardians over you until, when death comes to one of you, Our messengers take his soul, never neglecting their duty.”

“Then they are returned to Allah, their true Master.”

And He says:

“Or has he not been informed of what is in the scriptures of Moses,”

“And of Abraham, who fulfilled his duty:”

“That no bearer of burdens shall bear the burden of another,”

“And that a person will have only what he has striven for,”

“And that his striving will be seen,”

“Then he will be recompensed for it with the fullest recompense,”

“And that to your Lord is the final destination.”

And He says:

“Whether We show you some of what We promise them or take you in death, to Us is their return; then Allah is Witness to what they do.”

Whatever path a servant follows, his return and ultimate destination are to Allah. He must inevitably meet Allah:

“So that He may recompense those who do evil for what they have done and reward those who do good with the best.”

The verses discussed earlier, however, are intended to show that the way of truth and guidance—the straight path—is the way whose followers attain happiness and, through it, receive Allah’s protection, mercy, and honor. Allah, rather than Satan, thus becomes their protector. This is the way of those who worship Allah alone and obey His messengers. Hence He says:

“Indeed, upon Us is guidance.”

“And upon Allah is the direction of the way.”

“He said, ‘This is a straight path upon Me.’”

Thus guidance, the right course, and the straight path point only to worshipping Him and obeying Him—not to disobeying Him and obeying Satan.

---

## Page 7772

The wording thus conveys the meaning of “pointing the way,” since the intention is not to speak of recompense in the Hereafter, for recompense encompasses all creation. Rather, the aim is to explain what Allah has commanded—worshipping and obeying Him, and obeying His messengers: what points the way to this?

It is as though it were said: the straight path points to Allah—to worshipping and obeying Him. This shows that Arabic speakers say, “This road is upon so-and-so,” when it points to him and he is its intended destination. This differs from saying that it is “upon him” in the sense that someone travelling along it passes by him.

As the poet said:

Such are the fates of death: whatever valley I take,
My path leads to them, or theirs leads to me.

This is as al-Farrā’ said: “Whoever follows guidance, his path is upon Allah.” What is meant is the path that points to Him and brings one to Him, just as one says, “If you follow this path, you will come upon your intended destination,” and similar expressions. Likewise, one says, “You have come upon an expert.” When the destination sought is momentous, the traveller comes upon it and casts himself upon it.

Moreover, whoever follows Allah’s path puts his trust in Him. He must therefore both worship Him and put his trust in Him. Thus, saying, “Upon Him is the straight path,” implies that whoever follows it puts his trust in Him

---

## Page 7773

and that the path points him to Him, and he comes to rest in worshipping and obeying Him, never turning aside from that—among other such meanings conveyed by the preposition “upon,” but not by the preposition “to.”

He, glorified be He, has stated that He is on a straight path. Thus, the straight path is upon Him, and He is on a straight path. Glory be to Him, and exalted is He far above what the wrongdoers say. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 7774

Surah al-Nahl

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section

Clothing has two benefits: first, adornment through covering the private parts; second, protection against harmful heat, cold, or enemies.

Allah mentioned clothing in (Surah al-A‘rāf) for its benefit as adornment. It is this aspect that matters in prayer and circumambulation, as indicated by His words:

“Take your adornment at every place of prayer.”

He also said:

“O children of Adam, We have bestowed upon you clothing to conceal your private parts.”

And He said:

“Say, ‘Who has forbidden the adornment Allah has brought forth for His servants, and the good things He has provided?’”

This refuted their pre-Islamic prohibitions against circumambulating in the clothes in which those outside the Hums had arrived, and against eating the fats they had rendered.

---

## Page 7775

He also mentioned clothing in Surah al-Nahl for its protective benefit, saying:

“And He has made for you garments to protect you from the heat and armour to protect you in battle. Thus He perfects His favour upon you, so that you may submit to Him.”

Since this benefit meets a natural bodily need indispensable to human life, He counted it among His blessings. Since the former benefit enhances human life, He linked it to a religious command. The former brings benefit through adornment, whereas the latter wards off harm; people therefore have greater need of the latter.

As for His words:

“Garments to protect you from the heat,”

without mentioning “cold,” it has been said that this is because revelation came down in a hot land, whose people feared the heat. It has also been said that the other was omitted because it was already understood. Another explanation is that this points to the greater case through the lesser: if He reminds them of His favour in providing protection against heat, His favour in providing protection against cold is greater still. Heat causes discomfort, whereas cold causes hardship; severe cold kills, while heat seldom does so.

Such reasoning by implication and analogy applies to passages concerning blessings, promises and warnings just as it applies to passages concerning rulings. As I have said regarding His words:

“‘Do not go forth in the heat.’ Say, ‘The Fire of Hell is more intense in heat.’”

The same applies to someone who says, “Do not go forth in the cold,” for Hell is far more bitterly cold. Likewise:

“Whoever’s feet become dusty in Allah’s cause, Allah forbids the Fire to touch them.”

Mud and snow present an even stronger case, and so forth.

In this verse, He prescribed wearing protective armour in battle. This is why some have linked the chapter on clothing and adornment with prayer: warfare has its own distinctive clothing in addition to clothing used generally. Their expression “clothing and adornment” also corresponds to His words:

“They will be adorned therein with bracelets of gold

---

## Page 7776

and pearls, and their garments therein will be of silk.”

A better explanation is that protection against cold has already been mentioned at the beginning of the surah, in His words:

“And He created the livestock for you; in them are warmth and other benefits, and from them you eat.”

One may ask: Why were these mentioned separately? The answer—and Allah knows best—is that the beginning of the surah mentions the essential blessings without which people cannot live: food, plain drinking water, protection against cold, and riding animals indispensable for travel. Towards its end, He mentions blessings that complete these: pleasant drinks, dwelling in houses and hide tents, taking shelter in the shade, and garments that protect against heat and the dangers of battle. These can, generally speaking, be dispensed with. Thus, the beginning mentions the essentials, while the end mentions what completes them. This is why He says:

“Thus He perfects His favour upon you, so that you may submit to Him.”

Furthermore, dwellings have two benefits. The first is living in them for privacy; in this respect, they resemble clothing worn for adornment. The second is protection against harm from the sun, rain, wind and the like. Allah mentions both when reminding people of His blessings, saying:

“And Allah has made your homes a place of rest for you.”

These are earthen houses.

“And He has made for you, from the hides of livestock, dwellings that you find light to carry on the day you travel and the day you settle.”

These are tents supported by poles.

“And from their wool, fur and hair, furnishings and articles for use for a time.”

This includes household equipment such as rugs, containers, coverings and the like. He said, “Your homes a place of rest,” rather than “houses out of earth,” whereas He said, “from the hides of livestock, dwellings.” This is because rest explains the benefit of a house, thereby making the blessing apparent; and building

---

## Page 7777

houses out of earth is customary, so the blessing becomes apparent through its benefit. Livestock are different: the guidance to make dwellings from their hides is more evident than the guidance to construct dwellings as such.

As for the benefit of protection, He says:

“And Allah has made for you shade from what He has created, and has made for you shelters in the mountains.”

“Shade” encompasses everything that provides shade, including the canopies, tents and roofs that people make. His words, “shelters in the mountains,” refer to the protection a mountain affords a person from above, from the right and left, and from below. The purpose here is not to obtain shade, unlike the shade-giving structures, whose purpose is precisely that. This is why He mentions alongside these the protective benefit of garments. Thus, this verse brings together the protection afforded by clothing, which moves with the body, and the protection afforded by shade-giving structures fixed to the ground.

For this reason, people in the pre-Islamic period treated the two alike in the case of a pilgrim in ihrām (ritual consecration): just as covering his head was forbidden, they forbade him to enter beneath a roof, until Allah revealed:

“Righteousness does not consist in entering houses from the back.”

A pilgrim in ihrām was thus permitted to seek shade beneath stationary things such as tents and trees. As for something attached that moves along with him, such as a litter, that presents its own difficulties, since it falls between garments and stationary shade-giving structures and shelters.

Likewise, before these verses, He mentions the different kinds of drinks—milk, wine and honey—while at the beginning of the surah He mentions mounts and foods. These encompass the main categories of food, drink, clothing, dwellings and mounts.

---

## Page 7778

Shaykh al-Islām also said:

Concerning the words of Allah, the Almighty:

“Say: The Holy Spirit has brought it down from your Lord in truth,”

and the following verse: The expression “sending down” occurs in the Quran both with and without qualification. It may be qualified as being from Him, as with the Quran, or as being from the sky—meaning from above—as with rain. When unqualified, it is not restricted to any particular kind (*); rather, it includes bringing iron down from mountains, bringing things down from the backs of animals, and other instances.

Thus, His words, “The Holy Spirit has brought it down from your Lord,” explain that Gabriel descended with it from Allah. Similarly, He says:

“The Trustworthy Spirit has brought it down.”

This means that he is entrusted with it and neither adds nor omits anything, whereas someone unfaithful to his trust might alter the message. This indicates several points.

Among them is the invalidity of the claim that it was created within a body, as maintained by the Jahmiyyah among the Muʿtazilah and others. The early generations called anyone who held that it was created and denied the divine attributes and the vision of Allah a Jahmī. Jahm was the first to become known for the innovation of denying the divine names and attributes, and he took this to extremes. He thus has the distinction of taking it to extremes and pioneering its widespread propagation, although Jaʿd had preceded him in some of this. However, although the Muʿtazilah agreed with him in some respects, they differed with him on such matters as faith, divine decree, and certain attributes. And Jahm says that Allah does not

---

## Page 7779

speak—or, alternatively, that He speaks only figuratively—whereas they say that He truly speaks. In substance, however, their position is the same as his. He also denies the divine names, as do the Bātiniyyah and the philosophers.

Another point is the invalidity of the claim that it emanated from the active intellect or something else. This involves even greater disbelief and misguidance than the preceding view.

Another is the refutation of the Ashʿarī claim that Allah’s speech is a meaning, and that this Arabic text was created to signify it—whether they say that it was created within some body, that He inspired Gabriel with it, or that Gabriel took it from the Tablet. For this Arabic text must have had a speaker who first uttered it. This agrees with the view of those who say that it is created, but differs from it in two respects.

First, the latter say that what is created is Allah’s speech, whereas the former say that it is speech only figuratively. This is worse than the position of the Muʿtazilah; indeed, it is the position of the pure Jahmiyyah. Yet the Muʿtazilah agree with them in substance.

Second, they say that Allah has speech subsisting in His essence, whereas the Khilqiyyah say that it does not subsist in His essence. Thus, the Kullābiyyah are outwardly better than them; in reality, however, they have not affirmed any speech of His other than what is created.

The point is that the verse refutes this, and that “the Quran” is a name for the Arabic text, as indicated by His words:

“When you recite the Quran.”

Furthermore, His words, “has brought it down,” refer back to His words, “And Allah

---

## Page 7780

knows best what He sends down.” Thus, what Allah sent down is what the Holy Spirit brought down. He also says:

“And We certainly know that they say”—and the rest of the verse.

They claim that a human being teaches this Arabic Quran, as indicated by His words, “The tongue of the one to whom they allude,” and so forth. Thus, it is known that Muhammad did not compose its wording, but heard it from the Holy Spirit. It was the Holy Spirit who brought it down from Allah; thus, it is known that he heard it from Him and did not compose it himself.

A similar passage is His statement:

“He is the One who has sent down to you the Book, explained in detail.”

“The Book” is a name for the Quran, as is necessarily known and universally agreed. For they—or some of them—distinguish between Allah’s Book and His speech. The word “book” may refer to what is written in it, in which case it is the speech itself; or it may refer to that in which something is written, as in His words:

“In a well-guarded Book,”

and His words:

“On the Day of Resurrection, We shall bring forth for him a book which he will find spread open.”

His statement, “They know that it has been sent down from your Lord in truth,” invokes their testimony. If anyone among us does not acknowledge this, they are better than he in this respect.

This does not contradict what has been reported from Ibn ‘Abbās and others: that it was sent down on the Night of Decree to Bayt al-‘Izzah (the House of Honour) in the lowest heaven. Nor does it contradict its being written in the Tablet before its descent, whether Allah wrote it before sending Gabriel with it or afterwards. Thus, if it was sent down in its entirety to the House of Honour, He had written all of it before sending it down. Allah knows what has been, what will be, and how what will not be would be if it were to occur. He wrote the decrees and the deeds of His servants before they performed them, then commands that they be recorded after they have performed them, and makes a comparison between

---

## Page 7781

the earlier and later records, with no discrepancy between them. This is what Ibn ‘Abbās and others stated. If He has recorded what He creates, which is separate from Him, before creating it, how would He not record His speech with which He sends His angels before sending them?

As for the claim that Gabriel took it from the Book and did not hear it from Allah, it is false on several grounds.

One is that He, glorified be He, wrote the Torah for Moses with His own hand. The Children of Israel thus received His speech from the Book He wrote, whereas Muhammad would have received it from Gabriel, who took it from the Book. They would therefore rank one degree higher.

Whoever says that He conveyed meanings to Gabriel, who then expressed them in Arabic, is saying that He inspired him. Yet such inspiration is granted to individual believers, as in His words:

“And when I inspired the disciples, ‘Believe in Me and in My Messenger.’”

“And We inspired the mother of Moses.”

This would then be superior to the way Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, received it.

Furthermore, He, glorified be He, said:

“Indeed, We have revealed to you as We revealed to Noah and the prophets after him”—up to His words—“And Allah spoke to Moses directly.”

This indicates several things, including that He speaks to a servant in a way that goes beyond revelation, which is a distinct category alongside speaking in its specific sense. For both terms—speaking and revelation—have a general and a specific sense. Thus, speaking

---

## Page 7782

in its general sense is the overarching category divided in His words:

“It is not for any human being that Allah should speak to him except by revelation or from behind a veil”—to the end of the verse.

Thus, speaking, when mentioned without qualification, is a category alongside revelation in its specific sense, not a subdivision of it. Likewise, revelation can have a general sense that includes speaking in its specific sense, as in His words:

“So listen to what is revealed.”

It can also be a category alongside speaking, as in Surah al-Shūrā. This invalidates the claim that it is a single meaning subsisting in the Essence, for on that view there is no distinction between the general case and what was granted to Moses. In al-Shūrā, He, glorified be He, distinguishes between imparting revelation, speaking from behind a veil, and sending a messenger who reveals, by His permission, whatever He wills.

---

## Page 7783

Surah al-Isrā’

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said, while discussing the words of the Most High:

“Say, ‘Call upon those whom you claim [to be gods] besides Him,’”

and the following verse, after mentioning that some of the early generations identified them as angels, others as humans, and others as jinn:

The early generations indicate the category intended by the verse through examples, just as an interpreter shows a loaf to someone who asks him about bread. Here, the verse is intended to encompass everything called upon besides Allah. Thus, anyone who calls upon a dead or absent prophet or righteous person—whether he phrases it as an appeal for rescue or otherwise—is covered by this verse, just as it covers anyone who calls upon angels or jinn.

It is known that these beings serve as intermediaries in what Allah decrees through their actions. Nevertheless, He has forbidden calling upon them and has made clear that they have no power to remove harm from those who call upon them or to alter it. They can neither remove it altogether nor shift it from one place to another, nor change it from one state to another by altering its nature or extent. Hence His words:

“Nor any alteration.”

He thus used an indefinite noun encompassing every kind of alteration.

---

## Page 7784

And the Most High said:

“There were men among humankind who sought refuge with men among the jinn, so they only increased them in burden.”

Whenever one of them encamped in a valley, he would say, “I seek refuge with the chief of this valley from its foolish inhabitants.” The jinn then said, “Humans seek refuge with us,” so they increased them in burden.

The leading scholars—such as Ahmad and others—have explicitly stated that seeking refuge in a created being is impermissible. This is among the proofs they cited that Allah’s speech is uncreated, since it is authentically established that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, sought refuge in Allah’s words and instructed others to do so. If seeking refuge in a created being is impermissible, then saying, “You are the best one in whom to seek refuge,” is all the more impermissible. Seeking refuge, seeking protection, and appealing for rescue are all forms of supplication or petition, and these expressions are close in meaning.

Since the Kaaba is Allah’s House, where He is invoked and remembered, it is in Him, glorified be He, that protection is sought there. One may cling to the Kaaba’s coverings just as someone seeking protection clings to the hem of the person whose protection he seeks. As ʿAmr ibn Saʿīd said:

“The Sanctuary offers no refuge to a sinner, to anyone fleeing on account of bloodshed, or to anyone fleeing on account of a crime.”

An authentic hadith states:

“Someone seeking refuge will take refuge in this House.”

The point is that many misguided people appeal for rescue to those of whom they think highly, even though it is inconceivable that these beings could fulfill most of their requests. Likewise, most of what the devils report about unseen matters is false: they tell the truth about one thing and lie about many times as many. They meet one of their needs but prevent many times as many from being met.

---

## Page 7785

They lie concerning what they report and what they help accomplish, seeking to ruin people’s religious and worldly affairs. This gives the polytheists a semblance of proof, as do the reports of soothsayers and the like.

Allah, glorified be He, appointed the Messenger to convey His commands and prohibitions, His promises and warnings. These people, however, portray the messengers and shaykhs as governing the world by fulfilling needs and relieving distress. This is not part of the religion of the Muslims. Rather, the Christians claim this for Christ alone, on the basis of their erroneous notions of union and indwelling.

For this reason, they have not made the same claim about Abraham, Moses, or others, despite their utter ignorance in this matter. The signs with which Moses was sent are greater; and if this were possible, it would not be exclusive to Christ. Rather, Moses would have a stronger claim.

Thus, I would meet Christian scholars on their own terms and ask them to explain the distinction between Christ and others with regard to divinity. They could find no distinction. Indeed, I would explain to them that the signs Moses brought were greater: if such signs constituted evidence for a claim to divinity, Moses would have a stronger claim. As for Christ’s birth without a father, it demonstrates the Creator’s power, not that the created being is superior to others.

---

## Page 7786

Surah al-Kahf

Section

The hadith of ‘Alī—may Allah be pleased with him—recorded in the two Sahīh collections relates that the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—came to him and Fātimah at night while they were asleep and said, “Will you not both pray?”

‘Alī replied, “O Messenger of Allah, our souls are only in Allah’s hand. If He wills, He retains them; if He wills, He releases them.”

The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—turned away, striking his thigh with his hand and repeatedly saying:

“But man is, above all else, most prone to dispute.”

This hadith explicitly condemns those who invoke divine decree to oppose a command. His statement, “Our souls are only in Allah’s hand,” and the words that follow invoke divine decree to justify not complying with the command. The statement is true in itself, but it cannot legitimately be used to oppose the command. Rather, using it to oppose the command is a form of the blameworthy disputation concerning which Allah said:

“But man is, above all else, most prone to dispute.”

These people constitute one category of the Qadariyya, and I have [classified them] elsewhere. Thus, false disputation . . . (1).

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank in the original.
> Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd states (p. 73):
> Two points should be noted here:

## Page 7787

Surah Maryam

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section

“Surah Maryam”

Its central theme is the realization of worship devoted to Allah alone, and the affirmation that the elect of creation are His servants. Every honor and lofty rank lies in this relationship to Him. It also refutes those who exaggerate this relationship to Allah, going so far as to attribute Jesus to Him as His offspring, and those who err concerning the reality of servitude and the honor it entails, denying the blessings Allah has bestowed upon His chosen servants.

He opens it with His words:

“An account of your Lord’s mercy to His servant Zechariah.”

It recounts his calling upon his Lord in a quiet voice and Allah’s gift of John to him, followed by the account of Mary and her son, and his words:

“Indeed, I am a servant of Allah,”

and so forth. In this account, He sets out a refutation both of those who go to extremes concerning the Messiah and of those who disparage him, denying the blessings Allah bestowed upon him. He then commands His Prophet to recount Abraham, his call to worship Allah alone, his forbidding his father to worship Satan, and Allah’s bestowal

---

## Page 7788

of Isaac and Jacob upon him, and how He granted him an exalted reputation, meaning honorable praise. He tells of John, Jesus, and Abraham honoring their parents while worshipping Allah alone. He mentions Moses and His bestowal upon him of his brother Aaron as a prophet, just as He bestowed John upon Zechariah, Jesus upon Mary, and Isaac upon Abraham.

Thus, this surah is “the Surah of Gifts”: the righteous offspring, righteous deeds, and beneficial knowledge that Allah bestowed upon His prophets. He then mentions the descendants of Adam on account of Idrīs, “and of those We carried with Noah”—referring to Abraham—and the descendants of Abraham and Israel, continuing to the end of the account.

Then He says:

“But there came after them successors who neglected prayer and pursued desires,”

and the rest of the verse.

Such is the condition of those who fall short in worshipping Allah. He then makes an exception for those who repent, explaining that Paradise is for those who repent, and that the Gardens of perpetual residence are what the Most Merciful has promised His servants in the unseen. They are those who truly fulfill their servitude to Him. Then He says:

“That is Paradise, which We give as an inheritance to those of Our servants who are mindful of Allah.”

Then He says:

“So worship Him and persevere in His worship.”

He then mentions the condition of those who deny the resurrection and of those who attribute offspring to Him. He links the two in the hadith narrated by al-Bukhārī from Abū Hurayrah:

“The son of Adam has denied Me, though he has no right to do so, and the son of Adam has reviled Me, though he has no right to do so,”

and the rest of the hadith.

“And man says, ‘When I am dead, will I really be brought forth alive?’”

Then He mentions His oath concerning

---

## Page 7789

gathering them and the devils, and bringing them, kneeling, around Hell. This surah also indicates that anyone who reports something that will happen in the future can do so only in one of two ways: either he has access to the unseen—that is, knowledge of what will happen—or he has secured a covenant from the Most Merciful, and Allah fulfills His covenant. The first is knowledge of what is reported; the second is knowledge of what is commanded. The first is knowledge of His words of universal decree; the second is knowledge of His words of religious instruction. The man who swore that, on the Day of Resurrection, he would have what he mentioned was lying in his oath, for he neither had access to the unseen nor had secured a covenant from the Most Merciful.

This resembles what has been said about prayers being answered: sometimes this is due to sound belief, which accords with what is reported, and sometimes to complete obedience, which accords with what is commanded. As He says:

“So let them respond to Me and believe in Me.”

Thus, He describes the condition of someone who entertains baseless hopes concerning Allah, with neither knowledge of what will occur nor a covenant grounded in what He has prescribed.

He then mentions the condition of those who say that the Most Merciful has taken a child. He denies that He begets and refutes those who affirm this, while affirming love in refutation of those who deny it. He says:

“The Most Merciful will grant them love.”

That is, He loves them and makes His servants love them. This agrees with what is reported in the two Sahīh collections:

“When Allah loves a servant, He calls Gabriel: ‘I love so-and-so, so love him.’ Gabriel then loves him and calls out in heaven: ‘Allah loves so-and-so, so love him.’ The inhabitants of heaven then love him, and he is granted acceptance on earth.”

---

## Page 7790

And concerning hatred, he said the reverse.

Abraham’s words:

“Indeed, He has always been gracious to me,”

and Allah’s words concerning Moses:

“We called him from the right side of the Mount and brought him near for private conversation,”

together with His mention of love for the believers, affirm Allah’s love and His speaking, which the deniers reject, just as the earlier passage denies the claim of those who falsely assert that He has taken a child.

---

## Page 7791

He—may Allah be pleased with him—was asked about the words of Allah, Mighty and Majestic:

“Then they were succeeded by a generation who neglected prayer and followed their desires; so they will meet with ruin.”

Does this concern those who neglect the appointed time of prayer and pray outside it, or those who neglect prayer and do not perform it? And concerning His words, Exalted is He:

“So woe to those who pray,”

“those who are heedless of their prayer.”

Does this mean heedlessness about performing prayer, or distraction during it, as commonly occurs in the prayers of heedless people who comprehend nothing of their prayer? Give us a ruling; may you be rewarded.

He—may Allah be pleased with him—answered:

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of the worlds.

Rather, these two verses refer to those who neglect what is obligatory in prayer, not merely those who abandon it altogether. This is how the Companions and the Successors explained them, and it is the plain meaning of the wording. For He said:

“So woe to those who pray,”

“those who are heedless of their prayer.”

Thus, He affirmed that they pray, while describing them as heedless of their prayer. It is therefore understood that they were praying despite being heedless of it. A group of the early generations said: “Rather, it means heedlessness of what is obligatory within prayer, such as failing to observe stillness.” Both meanings are correct, and the verse encompasses both. Thus, in Sahīh Muslim, Anas reports that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said: “That is the hypocrite’s prayer! That is the hypocrite’s prayer!

---

## Page 7792

That is the hypocrite’s prayer! He watches the sun until, when it is between Satan’s two horns, he gets up and pecks out four units of prayer, remembering Allah only a little during them.”

In this hadith, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, explained that the hypocrite’s prayer involves both delaying it beyond the time at which he is commanded to perform it and pecking through it, remembering Allah only a little. Likewise, they explained His words:

“Then they were succeeded by a generation who neglected prayer and followed their desires,”

as meaning that neglecting prayer consists in delaying it beyond its appointed time and neglecting its due requirements. It is related in a hadith:

“When a servant undertakes prayer with its proper purification, recitation and prostration—or words to that effect—it ascends with a radiance like that of the sun, saying to him: ‘May Allah preserve you as you have preserved me.’ But if he does not complete its purification, recitation and prostration—or words to that effect—it is rolled up like a garment and says to him: ‘May Allah neglect you as you have neglected me.’”

Salmān al-Fārisī said: “Prayer is a measure: whoever gives full measure will receive full measure, while whoever gives short measure—you know what He has said about those who give short measure.”

In Sunan Abī Dāwūd, ‘Ammār reports that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“A servant may finish his prayer with only half of it recorded for him, or a third, or a quarter, or a fifth, or a sixth, or a seventh, or an eighth, or a ninth, or a tenth.”

The scholars have differed, holding two opinions, as to whether someone overwhelmed by distracting thoughts during prayer must repeat it. However, the imams, such as Ahmad and others, hold that he need not repeat it. They cited as evidence what is reported in

---

## Page 7793

the Sahīh, on the authority of Abū Hurayrah, that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When the caller gives the call to prayer, Satan retreats, passing wind so that he cannot hear the call. When the call is finished, he returns. When the call to commence the prayer is given, he retreats; when it is finished, he returns and distracts a person, saying: ‘Remember such-and-such! Remember such-and-such!’—things he had not been thinking about—until he leads the man astray, leaving him unsure how much he has prayed. If any of you experiences this, let him perform two prostrations before giving the closing salutation.”

His statement was general, yet he did not instruct anyone to repeat the prayer.

The second opinion is that he must repeat it. This is the view of a group of scholars, both jurists and Sufis, among the followers of Ahmad and others, such as Abū Abd Allāh ibn Hāmid and others. They base this on his statement quoted earlier: “Only a tenth of it is recorded for him.”

The correct assessment is that he is rewarded only in proportion to his attentiveness. Nevertheless, he is spared the punishment deserved by one who abandons prayer. This is what they mean when they say, “His obligation is discharged by it”: he is not punished for failing to perform the prayer, but his reward is proportionate to his attentiveness. As Ibn Abbās said: “You have of your prayer only what you were conscious of.” This is why the regular Sunnah prayers were prescribed: to make up for deficiencies in the obligatory prayers. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 7794

Surah Tā Hā

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section: “Surah Tā Hā”

Its theme is the easing of hardship associated with the Quran and the scriptures Allah Most High has revealed. It is thus “the surah of His scriptures,” just as Maryam is “the surah of His servants and messengers.” He begins it with His words:

“We have not sent down the Quran to you to cause you distress”

…through His words:

“A revelation from Him who created the earth and the lofty heavens.”

He then recounts the story of Moses: Allah’s calling him, conversing privately with him, and speaking to him. His story is among the most compelling accounts of the messengers, which is why it is repeated in the Quran. He received both divine address and scripture, and was sent to Pharaoh, the defiant, doubting denier of divine lordship and messengership—the most obstinate of disbelievers. Allah recounts the story in full in this surah, up to His words:

“My Lord, increase me in knowledge.”

He then recounts the story of Adam, for his was the first prophethood. The surah mentions Moses and Adam because of the affinity between them, which calls for

---

## Page 7795

mentioning them together, and because of the debate between them. For Moses is Adam’s counterpart in what befell each of them, just as the Messiah is Adam’s counterpart in his creation. He also says:

“If guidance should come to you from Me”

—and so on through the following verses. This resembles the passages elsewhere in the Quran that mention Adam’s prophethood and then that of Moses after him. He commanded the Children of Israel, then commanded His Prophet to observe the prayer enjoined in the Quran, just as He combined the commands to recite and to prostrate in the first surah revealed. He closes this surah by mentioning the Messenger who conveys everything he is commanded to convey, just as He opened it by mentioning the revelation sent down to him.

---

## Page 7796

He also said:

Section: “On the Two Paths of Knowledge and Action”

Allah Most High said to Moses and Aaron:

“Speak to him gently, so that he may take heed or fear Allah.”

And He said in the very same surah:

“Thus We relate to you some accounts of what has gone before, and We have given you a Reminder from Us.”

And so on until His words:

“Thus We have sent it down as an Arabic Quran and have set out warnings in various ways within it, so that they may fear Allah or it may bring them a reminder.”

Thus, regarding each of the two great missions—the mission of Moses and the mission of Muhammad—He stated that its purpose was to bring about either heedfulness or fear. He did not say, “so that he may take heed and fear,” nor did He say, “so that they may fear Me and it may bring them a reminder.” Rather, He made either one of these two the desired outcome. This accords with His words:

“Call to the way of your Lord with wisdom and good exhortation,”

and other similar passages.

---

## Page 7797

Umar ibn al-Khattāb, may Allah be pleased with him, said: “What an excellent servant Suhayb is! Even if he did not fear Allah, he would not disobey Him.” This expresses the full meaning of His words:

“The path of those You have blessed, not of those who have incurred Your anger, nor of those who have gone astray.”

And His words:

“And they exhort one another to truth and exhort one another to patience.”

And His words:

“Possessors of strength and insight.”

And His words:

“They are upon guidance from their Lord, and it is they who are successful.”

And His words:

“Indeed, the criminals are in error and blazing fire.”

And His words:

“Whoever follows My guidance will neither go astray nor suffer misery. But whoever turns away from My remembrance will have a life of hardship, and We will raise him blind on the Day of Resurrection.”

And other similar verses.

The reason is that goodness may come through knowing the truth and following it in both knowledge and action. Soundness of speech and action rests on knowledge and will. Knowledge is the foundation of action, will, love, and other such things, and necessarily entails them unless something intervenes to prevent this.

Thus, knowing the truth leads to following it unless a stronger opposing influence intervenes, such as following one's desires through arrogance and the like. This was the condition of those of whom Allah said:

“I will turn away from My signs those who act arrogantly on earth without right. Even if they see every sign, they will not believe in it. If they see the path of right guidance, they will not take it as their way; but if they see the path of error, they will take it as their way.”

And He said:

“They denied them, though their souls were convinced of them, out of injustice and arrogance.”

And He said:

“It is not you that they deny; rather, it is Allah’s signs that the wrongdoers reject.”

For this reason He said: “O David,

---

## Page 7798

We have made you a successor on earth, so judge between people in truth and do not follow desire, lest it lead you astray from Allah’s path.” And other similar passages. For the original natural disposition with which people are created, when free from corruption, follows and loves the truth whenever it recognizes it.

Truth is of two kinds:

An existing reality, which one must know and report truthfully. The opposites of this are ignorance and falsehood. And a good to be sought—namely, that which benefits a person—which one must desire and act upon. The opposite of this is to desire and follow falsehood. It is well known that Allah has created within souls a love of knowledge rather than ignorance, truthfulness rather than falsehood, and what is beneficial rather than what is harmful. Whenever the opposite arises, it is because of a counteracting influence, such as desire, arrogance, envy, or the like.

Likewise, in a healthy body, Allah has created a love of food and drink suited to it rather than what harms it. If it craves what harms it or dislikes what benefits it, this is due to an illness in the body. Similarly, when the counteracting influences of desire, arrogance, envy, and the like are removed from the soul, the heart loves what benefits it of beneficial knowledge and righteous deeds, just as

---

## Page 7799

the body, once its illness has been dispelled, loves the food and drink that benefit it. Thus, the presence of an impelling factor and the absence of a counteracting factor each brings about the other, and this brings about a person’s well-being. Their opposites produce the opposite result. When knowledge weakens, desire overpowers (1) a person. If both knowledge and desire are present—the impelling and counteracting factors respectively—the stronger prevails.

Since this is so, human well-being lies in faith and righteous deeds. Only two things lead people away from this: first, ignorance, which is the opposite of knowledge, so that they go astray; second, following the desires and appetites within their souls, so that they become wayward and incur wrath. Hence Allah says:

“By the star when it sets!”

“Your companion has neither strayed nor erred.”

And the Prophet said:

“You must follow my Sunnah and the Sunnah of the upright, rightly guided caliphs after me. Hold fast to it and bite onto it with your molar teeth.”

He thus described them as upright, which is the opposite of wayward, and rightly guided, which is the opposite of astray. Through these two qualities, both knowledge and action become sound, and a person becomes knowledgeable and just, rather than ignorant or unjust.

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) The printed edition reads “ghalabahu” (overpowered him). The correction follows Ibn Taymiyyah’s al-Tafsīr al-Kabīr, edited by Dr. Muhammad al-Julaynid, 4:344.

---

## Page 7800

With regard to righteousness, people are of two kinds:

The first is someone who, once he recognizes the truth and it becomes clear to him, follows it and acts upon it. Such a person is invited with wisdom; he takes heed, and the Quran awakens remembrance within him. The second is someone whose desires and resistance to the truth make him need fear to restrain his soul from desire. Such a person is invited with good admonition. This is the second category mentioned in His words:

“Or be moved to fear,”

and in His words:

“So that they may become God-fearing.”

In the surah, in the account of Pharaoh, He says:

“Go to Pharaoh, for he has transgressed. Then say, ‘Would you be willing to purify yourself, and let me guide you to your Lord so that you may fear Him?’”

He thus brings together purification, guidance, and fear, just as He brings together knowledge and fear in His words:

“Only those of His servants who have knowledge truly fear Allah,”

and in His words:

“And in their inscription was guidance and mercy for those who stand in awe of their Lord,”

and in His words:

“If they had done what they were admonished to do, it would have been better for them and would have strengthened them more firmly. Then We would have granted them a great reward from Us and guided them to a straight path.”

This is because, as we have mentioned, both knowledge of the truth—embodied in taking heed and in the remembrance that the Quran brings about—and the fear that prevents one from following desire are causes of a person’s well-being. Each necessarily entails the other when it prevails over

---

## Page 7801

its opposite. Thus, when knowledge and remembrance grow strong, desire is repelled; and when desire is repelled by fear of Allah, the heart gains insight and knowledge. These are the two approaches: knowledge and action. When sound, each necessarily entails whatever it requires of the other. A servant’s well-being lies in attaining what he needs and what is required of him in both. His corruption therefore results from the absence of either: without true knowledge, he is astray and without guidance; without following that knowledge, he is wayward and incurs wrath.

For this reason, He says:

“The path of those You have blessed, not of those who have incurred wrath, nor of those who have gone astray.”

And He says:

“By the star when it descends, your companion has neither strayed nor erred, nor does he speak from desire. It is nothing but revelation revealed.”

Describing the opposite, He says:

“They follow nothing but conjecture and what their souls desire.”

And He says:

“Who is more astray than one who follows his own desire without guidance from Allah?”

And He says:

“Indeed, many lead others astray through their desires, without knowledge.”

And He says:

“Whoever follows My guidance will neither go astray nor suffer.”

Describing the opposite, He says:

“But whoever turns away from My remembrance will have a life of hardship, and We will raise him blind on the Day of Resurrection.”

And He says:

“Those are upon guidance from their Lord, and those are the successful.”

Describing the opposite, He says:

“Indeed, the criminals are in error and blazing fire.”

Ibn ‘Abbās said:

“Allah has guaranteed that whoever reads the Quran and follows what it contains will neither go astray in this world nor suffer in the Hereafter.”

Thus He, glorified be He, pairs guidance with happiness and misguidance with wretchedness,

---

## Page 7802

between the good of this world and the Hereafter and the evil of this world and the Hereafter. He pairs beneficial knowledge with righteous deeds—wholesome knowledge with righteous deeds—just as He pairs their opposites: “misguidance” and “waywardness,” namely, following conjecture and what souls desire. The two members of each pair necessarily accompany one another when sound and free from any counteracting influence. Yet one may fail to accompany the other when a stronger opposing influence intervenes.

Thus, in contexts of censure, prohibition, and seeking refuge, each is censured and prohibited: misguidance and waywardness, ignorance and wrongdoing, misguidance and wrath. For each is something hateful whose absence is sought, particularly since it entails the other.

In contexts of praise, seeking good, and Allah’s favour, however, either one may be sought, or both may be sought; either one may be praised, or both may be praised. Each is good, desirable, and praiseworthy, and each is a means of attaining the other. Complete righteousness, however, requires both together. A person may attain this when he acquires one of them and no opposing influence intervenes.

One who calls people and directs them thereby adopts an approach of kindness and gentleness: he asks for one of the two because it is desirable in itself and is a means of attaining the other. This is gentler than commanding a servant to undertake both together, which might prove burdensome to him.

Commands build up, whereas prohibitions tear down. Commands secure health through taking medicines, whereas prohibitions serve as a protective regimen. Building and recovery proceed little by little, whereas demolition is quicker and protective abstinence is more comprehensive, although in both there may be

---

## Page 7803

a gradual progression as well. How much more so, then, when each of the two is a means and a path to achieving the intended aim, with the other attained along with it.

By His words, glorified be He,

“Perhaps he may take heed or be moved to fear,”

and His words,

“Perhaps they may fear Allah, or it may bring them a reminder,”

He sought to bring about either of these two outcomes through conveying the message. He used the expression “perhaps” to make the matter easier, to show gentleness, and to explain that attaining either one is a path to achieving the intended aim. Thus, both are not sought together at the outset. Hence the saying handed down:

“Part of the reward for a good deed is the good deed that follows it, and part of the punishment for an evil deed is the evil deed that follows it.”

This applies especially to foundational good deeds that entail all the others, such as truthfulness, for it is the foundation of goodness. Thus, in the two Sahīh collections, Ibn Masʿūd reports that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Hold fast to truthfulness, for truthfulness leads to righteousness, and righteousness leads to Paradise. A man continues to speak the truth and strive to be truthful until he is recorded with Allah as one who is ever truthful. Beware of lying, for lying leads to wickedness, and wickedness leads to the Fire. A man continues to lie and deliberately pursue falsehood until he is recorded with Allah as a habitual liar.”

For this reason, He, glorified be He, said:

“Shall I tell you upon whom the devils descend? They descend upon every sinful liar.”

And He said:

“Woe to every sinful liar who hears Allah’s verses being recited to him, then persists arrogantly as though he had not heard them.”

For this reason, it is related that

---

## Page 7804

one of the shaykhs wished to discipline some of his companions who had committed many sins, so he said:

“My son, I enjoin upon you just one quality, so uphold it for my sake. For now, I ask nothing else of you: adhere to truthfulness, and beware of lying.”

He threatened him with severe punishment for lying. When he committed himself to this, truthfulness led him to all other goodness and restrained him from his former ways, for the wicked person knows no bounds in lying.

---

## Page 7805

Shaykh al-Islām Taqī al-Dīn Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—said:

Section

Concerning the words of Allah Most High:

“These two are certainly magicians.”

This passage has puzzled many people. What appears in the Muslims’ copies of the Quran is in hādhāni, with an alif, and this is how the great majority of Quran reciters read it. Most of them read inna, with a doubled n, whereas Ibn Kathīr and Hafs—the latter transmitting from ‘Āsim—read in, without doubling. Ibn Kathīr, however, doubles the n in hādhāni, unlike Hafs.

The difficulty from the standpoint of Arabic grammar concerns the well-known reading: that of Nāfi‘, Ibn ‘Āmir, Hamzah, al-Kisā’ī, and Abū Bakr transmitting from ‘Āsim. This is the reading followed by the majority of reciters, and it is the soundest of the readings in both wording and meaning. This will become clear through a discussion of what has been said about it.

The difficulty arises because a dual noun is inflected with a yā’ in the accusative and genitive cases, and with an alif in the nominative. This is abundantly attested in the language of the Arabs:

---

## Page 7806

in the language of the Quran and elsewhere, with indeclinable nouns, as in His words:

“For his parents (abawayhi), each receives a sixth of what he leaves.”

Then He says:

“But if he has no child and his parents (abawāhu) inherit from him, his mother receives a third.”

He also says:

“And he raised his parents (abawayhi) upon the throne.”

And:

“And wipe your heads and wash your feet up to the ankles (al-ka‘bayni).”

He did not say al-ka‘bāni. He also says:
