“And present to them the example of the people of the town, when the messengers came to it: when We sent two (ithnayni) to them, but they denied them both, so We strengthened them with a third.”

He did not say ithnāni. He also says:

“We said, ‘Load aboard it a pair of every kind, two (zawjayni ithnayni) of each.’”

And He says:

“Eight mates: two sheep (ithnayni) and two goats (ithnayni). Say, ‘Has He forbidden the two males (al-dhakarayni), or the two females (al-unthayayni), or what the wombs of the two females (al-unthayayni) contain?’”

He did not say ithnāni, al-dhakarāni, or al-unthayāni. He also says:

“And of everything We created pairs (zawjayni).”

He did not say zawjāni. And He says:

“But if they are females, more than two (ithnatayni)…”

He did not say ithnatāni. Examples of this are numerous and well known, both in the Quran and elsewhere. The grammarians therefore supposed that indeclinable nouns with context-dependent referents, such as hādhayni (these two) and alladhayni (the two who), followed this same pattern, and that an indeclinable form would take an alif in the nominative case. This is where the difficulty arose.

Abū ‘Amr was an authority on Arabic, so he recited according to the Arabic usage he knew: Inna hādhayni la-sāhirāni (“These two are certainly magicians”). He mentioned that he had predecessors in this reading, and this is what one should presume

---

## Page 7807

of him: that he recites only what he transmits, not merely what he considers correct. It is reported that he said, “I am indeed ashamed before Allah to recite ‘In hādhāni (These two).’” This was because he could see no grammatical justification for it in Arabic. Some considered Abū ‘Amr mistaken in this reading, including al-Zajjāj, who said, “I do not permit Abū ‘Amr’s reading, which differs from the written Quran.”

As for the well-known reading that accords with the Quran’s written form, many grammarians defended it on the grounds that this was the dialect of Banū al-Hārith ibn Ka‘b. Several authorities on Arabic have reported this usage.

Al-Mahdawī said: “Banū al-Hārith ibn Ka‘b say darabtu al-Zaydāni (‘I struck the two Zayds’) and marartu bi-al-Zaydāni (‘I passed by the two Zayds’), just as one says jā’anī al-Zaydāni (‘The two Zayds came to me’).” Al-Mahdawī said that Abū Zayd, al-Akhfash, al-Kisā’ī and al-Farrā’ reported this usage. Abū al-Khattāb reported that it was the dialect of Banū Kinānah, while another reported that it was the dialect of Khath‘am. An example is the poet’s words:

“He bore away from us, between his ears (udhunāhu), a ruinous blow,
which summoned him into the dusty depths.”

Ibn al-Anbārī said: “It is a dialectal usage of Banū al-Hārith ibn Ka‘b and Quraysh.” Al-Zajjāj said that Abū ‘Ubaydah reported from Abū al-Khattāb—one of the foremost authorities in transmitting Arabic usage—that this was the dialect of Kinānah: they retain the same form with the dual alif in the nominative, accusative and genitive cases. They cited the verse:

“He lowered his head as a serpent does; and had the serpent found
an opening for its two fangs (nābāhu), it would have bitten and held fast.”

---

## Page 7808

He also said: “These people say: darabtuhu bayna udhunāhu (‘I struck him between his two ears’).”

I say: Banū al-Hārith ibn Ka‘b are the people of Najrān. There is no doubt that the Quran was not revealed in this dialect. Rather, throughout the Quran, the dual forms of indeclinable nouns take yā in the accusative and genitive, as the examples cited above demonstrate.

It is established in al-Sahīh that ‘Uthmān said: “The Quran was revealed in the dialect of Quraysh.” He told the men of Quraysh who wrote out the Quran manuscript together with Zayd: “If you disagree over anything, write it in the dialect of Quraysh, for the Quran was revealed in their dialect.” They disagreed over only one word (al-tābūt, “the Ark”). They referred the matter to ‘Uthmān, who ordered that it be written in the dialect of Quraysh. Al-Bukhārī related this in his Sahīh.

Anas reported that Hudhayfah ibn al-Yamān came to ‘Uthmān. He had been campaigning with the people of Syria, alongside the people of Iraq, in the conquest of Armenia and Azerbaijan. Their differences in recitation alarmed Hudhayfah, so he said to ‘Uthmān: “Commander of the Faithful, save this community before they differ over the Book as the Jews and Christians did.”

‘Uthmān sent word to Hafsah: “Send us the sheets so that we may transcribe them into copies of the Quran, then return them to you.” Hafsah sent them to ‘Uthmān, who instructed Zayd ibn Thābit, ‘Abd Allāh ibn al-Zubayr, Sa‘īd ibn al-‘Ās and ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn al-Hārith ibn Hishām to transcribe them into copies of the Quran. ‘Uthmān told the three men of Quraysh: “If you and Zayd ibn Thābit disagree over anything in the Quran, write it in the dialect of Quraysh

---

## Page 7809

, for it was revealed in their dialect alone.” They did so, and when they had transcribed the sheets into copies of the Quran, ‘Uthmān returned the sheets to Hafsah. He sent one of the copies they had transcribed to each region and ordered that all other Quranic material, whether on individual sheets or in bound copies, be burned.

The manuscript he obtained from Hafsah was the one in which Abū Bakr and ‘Umar had instructed Zayd ibn Thābit to compile the Quran. The account of this is well known and is recorded in the two Sahīh collections and elsewhere. It was in Zayd’s handwriting, which is why ‘Uthmān instructed that he be among those transcribing copies of the Quran from those sheets. However, he appointed three men from Quraysh to work with him so that it would be written in their dialect. The dialects of Quraysh and the Ansār differed only over the forms (al-tābūh) and (al-tābūt), meaning “the Ark”; they wrote (al-tābūt), in the dialect of Quraysh.

This shows that multiple copies of the Quran were transcribed, as is well known and widely acknowledged. It further demonstrates the error of claiming that certain words are scribal errors, or attributing such a claim to ‘Uthmān. This is impossible for several reasons.

Among them are the multiplicity of copies, the collaboration of a group on each copy, and the subsequent arrival of each copy in a large town containing many Companions and Successors who recited the Quran and checked the text against what they had memorized. When someone transcribes a copy of the Quran and makes an error in part of it, his error is recognized because it conflicts with his memorization of the Quran and with the other copies. Thus, if it were supposed that

---

## Page 7810

a scribe had written a copy of the Quran and everyone else had then copied from it without checking either the original or the copies, an error could have occurred in this way. Here, however, each copy was written by a group and examined by a vast number of people—far more than would be needed to establish mass transmission. Even supposing that the original sheet contained a grammatical error, a group transcribed it who would write only in the dialect of Quraysh. Even where there was no grammatical error, they refused to write it in anything other than the dialect of Quraysh. How, then, could they all agree to write *in hādhāni* (“Indeed, these two”), knowing that this was a grammatical error inadmissible in any of their dialects? Or *al-muqīmīna al-salāta* (“those who establish prayer”), knowing that this was a grammatical error, as some have alleged?

Regarding the words “those who establish prayer,” al-Zajjāj said: “The claim that this is an error is exceedingly far-fetched. Those who compiled the Quran were authorities in the language and exemplars to be followed. How could they leave something for others to correct? Such a thing should not be attributed to them.” Ibn al-Anbārī said: “The report from ‘Uthmān is not authentic because its chain of transmission is disconnected. It is inconceivable that ‘Uthmān would leave something for those after him to correct.”

I say: Further evidence that this is false is that, even supposing this had happened with ‘Uthmān, he would only have seen it in a single copy. As for the possibility that all the copies contained the same error, and that ‘Uthmān saw it in all of them and remained silent, this is impossible, both as a matter of ordinary practice and in light of religious law, on the part of the scribes, ‘Uthmān, and then the Muslims who received the copies and examined their contents, while they had memorized the Quran and knew that it contained a grammatical error

---

## Page 7811

inadmissible in the language, let alone in recitation, yet all of them acquiesced in this wrong without anyone correcting it. Such a claim is known to be false as a matter of ordinary experience. It is also incompatible with the religion of these people—who do not agree upon misguidance, but enjoin every good and forbid every wrong—that they should leave a wrong in Allah’s Book without any of them correcting it, especially when none of them had any interest in doing so. Had ‘Uthmān been told, “Order the scribe to change it,” changing it would have been among the easiest things for him to do.

These and similar considerations establish with certainty that anyone who claims that the written Quran contains a grammatical error or some other mistake is mistaken. Even if such a claim is reported from certain individuals whose statements are not authoritative, they may have erred in what they said. By contrast, those who transmitted, wrote down, and recited the contents of the written Quran could not collectively have erred in doing so. As ‘Uthmān said: “If you disagree over anything, write it in the dialect of Quraysh.” Likewise, ‘Umar told Ibn Mas‘ūd: “Teach people to recite in the dialect of Quraysh, not in the dialect of Hudhayl, for the Quran was not revealed in the dialect of Hudhayl.”

Allah’s words in the Quran support this:

“We never sent a messenger except in the language of his people.”

His people were Quraysh, as Allah said:

“Your people have rejected it, though it is the truth.”

As for Kinānah, they are the neighbours of Quraysh, and the person reporting their usage is reliable. However, a transmitter reports what he has heard. He may have heard this usage with indeclinable demonstrative and relative pronouns and assumed that they used it with other nouns as well. This differs from someone who heard “bayna udhunāhu” (“between its two ears”) and “la-nābāhu” (“for its two fangs”), since these are explicit examples involving nouns other than demonstrative and relative pronouns.

---

## Page 7812

Accordingly, what must be said is this: it has not been established that pronouncing demonstrative and relative pronouns with yā’ when they are dualized belongs to the dialect of Quraysh—or even to that of the other Arabs. Those grammarians who maintained this did so only by analogy, treating the dual forms of demonstrative and relative pronouns like those of other nouns. Otherwise, there is no evidence in the Quran supporting their claim. No other such indeclinable demonstrative or relative pronoun occurs in the Quran in an accusative or genitive position, and the wording here is “hādhāni” (“these two”). This wording is established by mass transmission, both in pronunciation and in writing.

Whoever claims that the scribe made a mistake is himself gravely mistaken, as has been explained at length elsewhere. The written Quran has been handed down through mass transmission, and several copies were written out, all of them with alif. How, then, could a mistake be conceivable here? Moreover, the reciters recited only what they had heard from others. Muslims were reciting Surah Tā Hā during the lifetimes of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and of Abū Bakr, ‘Umar, ‘Uthmān, and ‘Alī. It was among the earliest portions of the Quran to be revealed. Ibn Mas‘ūd said:

“The Children of Israel, The Cave, Mary, Tā Hā, and The Prophets are among the earliest surahs revealed, and among the first I learned.”

Al-Bukhārī narrated this from him.

It is unanimously agreed to be Meccan. Abū al-Faraj and others said: “It is Meccan by unanimous agreement.” Indeed, it was among the earliest revelations. It has also been reported that a written copy was in the possession of ‘Umar’s sister, and that the occasion of ‘Umar’s conversion was his learning that his sister had embraced Islam, while the surah was being recited in her presence.

---

## Page 7813

The Companions must therefore have recited this word. It is impossible that they all recited it with yā’, as Abū ‘Amr did, for had that been the case, no one would have recited it except with yā’, nor would it have been written except with yā’. Thus, we know that they—or most of them—recited it with alif, as the majority of reciters did. The Companions in Mecca, Medina, Syria, Kufa, and Basra recited this surah both during prayer and outside it. The Successors heard it from them, and the generation after the Successors heard it from the Successors. It is therefore impossible that all the Companions recited it with yā’, when the majority of reciters recited it only with alif, having learned their recitation either directly from the Companions or from the Successors, who had learned it from the Companions. This establishes with certainty that the general body of the Companions recited it with alif, just as the majority recited it and as it is written.

Now, we know that the Companions recited only as the Messenger had taught them, in accordance with Arabic usage, specifically the dialect of Quraysh. We therefore know that this eloquent usage, familiar to them in demonstrative and relative pronouns, employs the forms:

“in hādhāni” (“Indeed, these two …”) and “marartu bi-hādhāni” (“I passed by these two”).

They use alif in the nominative, accusative, and genitive alike. Anyone who claims that their dialect uses alif in the nominative must produce evidence for that claim and transmitted examples of their speech, as actually heard from them, in prose and verse. Nothing in the Quran supports his claim; rather, his reliance is on analogy.

We therefore say:

---

## Page 7814

Applying the analogy of other nouns to this form is mistaken, for the distinction between them is established both by reason and by attested usage.

As for transmitted evidence and attested usage, these are as we have explained. As for reasoning and analogy, several accomplished grammarians have recognized the distinction. Ibn al-Anbārī and others report that al-Farrā’ said: “The dual alif in hādhāni (‘these two’) is the alif of hādhā (‘this’). The nūn distinguishes the singular from the dual, just as the nūn in alladhīna (‘those who’) distinguishes the singular from the plural.”

Al-Mahdawī and others also report this from al-Farrā’. Al-Mahdawī’s wording is: “He stated that the alif is not the dual marker; rather, it is the alif of hādhā: ‘I added a nūn to it without changing it, just as I added one to the yā’ of alladhī (‘who’), saying alladhīna in every grammatical case.’” He continued: “Some Kufan grammarians said: ‘The alif here resembles that in yafʿalāni (‘they two do’), so it remains unchanged, just as that alif remains unchanged.’”

He said: “Al-Jurjānī said: ‘Since it is a noun consisting of two letters, one of which is a long-vowel letter, comparable to a short vowel, and since one of the two alifs must be omitted in forming the dual, it would be inappropriate to omit the first, lest the noun be reduced to a single letter. The dual marker was therefore omitted, while the nūn indicated the dual. There was no reason for the nūn to alter the original alif, so it remained unchanged in every grammatical case, just as it does in the singular.’”

Al-Mahdawī said: “Ismāʿīl the judge asked Ibn Kaysān about this question. He replied: ‘Since no case inflection appears in these pronouns, either in the singular or in the plural, the dual follows the same pattern as the singular, since the dual ought not to vary.’ Ismāʿīl said: ‘How excellent your explanation would be, had someone advanced it before you, providing reassuring precedent!’ Ibn Kaysān replied to him: ‘Then let the judge say it

---

## Page 7815

so that it may provide reassuring precedent!’ He smiled.”

I say:

Rather, al-Farrā’ and others had already advanced this explanation. Al-Farrā’ holds the same standing among the Kufan grammarians as Sībawayh does among the Basrans. Ismāʿīl, however, relied on Basran grammar, and al-Mubarrid was especially close to him.

This explanation may be clarified as follows: the singular is dhā (‘this’). Had they treated it like other nouns, they would have formed the dual as dhawāni, not dhāni, just as they said ʿasawāni (‘two staffs’), rajawāni (‘two sides’), and similar forms of triliteral nouns. Hā is a particle used to draw attention. Where they had omitted the final root consonant, they said abawāni (‘two fathers’), so forming the dual restored the original form. In other instances they said … (1) and yadāni (‘two hands’).

As for dhā, they did not say dhawāni. Rather, they proceeded as they did with dhū and dhāt, meaning ‘possessor’: they said huwa dhū ʿilmin (‘he possesses knowledge’) and humā dhawā ʿilmin (‘they both possess knowledge’), just as Allah said:

“Both having spreading branches.”

For the demonstrative, they said dhāni (‘these two’, masculine) and tāni (‘these two’, feminine), just as Allah said:

“So these are two proofs from your Lord.”

Dhā meaning ‘possessor’ is a declinable noun: its inflection changes according to whether it is nominative, accusative, or genitive, yielding dhū, dhā, and dhī. Demonstrative forms, relative pronouns, and personal pronouns, however, are indeclinable;

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank space in the original.

---

## Page 7816

but demonstratives do not distinguish between the nominative, accusative, and genitive in either the singular or the plural, and the same therefore applies to the dual. They said: qāma hādhā (“this one stood”), akramtu hādhā (“I honored this one”), and marartu bi-hādhā (“I passed by this one”). The plural hā’ulā’i (“these”) likewise remains unchanged. The same applies to the dual: hādhāni (“these two”), akramtu hādhāni (“I honored these two”), and marartu bi-hādhāni (“I passed by these two”). The proper grammatical analogy here is therefore to assimilate its dual to its own singular and plural, rather than to the dual of another kind of noun, which is itself governed by its own singular and plural.

With declinable nouns, the dual follows the singular and plural. Thus, one says rajulun (“a man”), rajulāni (“two men”), and rijālun (“men”). The noun is declinable in all three forms: case inflection appears in its dual just as it appears in its singular and plural.

It thus becomes clear that those who claim that Arabic grammar requires inna hādhayni (“indeed, these two”) have no transmitted evidence for this from the known language of the Quran—the language in which it was revealed. Rather, that language requires the dual demonstrative to remain indeclinable, retaining a single form in all three cases, just like singular and plural demonstratives.

On this basis, whether one says that the alif is the singular’s alif with a nūn added, or that it is a dual marker and the original alif has been omitted, or, rather, that this alif combines both functions—this is the substance of Ibn Kaysān’s answer. Al-Farrā’s statement is equivalent in meaning, as is al-Jurjānī’s, and likewise the statement of those who say that its alif resembles the alif in yafʿalāni (“the two of them do”).

---

## Page 7817

It may then be said that the relative pronoun may follow the same pattern, as in His words:

“And the two among you who commit it”

If it is established that, in the dialect of Quraysh, they say ra’aytu alladhayni faʿalā (“I saw the two who did it”) and marartu bi-alladhayni faʿalā (“I passed by the two who did it”)—but if not, it may be said that this pronoun retains the alif in all three cases, because it is indeclinable, and its alif takes the place of the yā’ in alladhīna (“those who”). What al-Farrā’, Ibn Kaysān, and others have stated supports this. Al-Farrā’ compared the demonstrative form to alladhīna, and comparing alladhāni (“the two who”) to it is even more appropriate. Ibn Kaysān explained that a noun of indeterminate reference is indeclinable and does not display case inflection; he therefore treated its dual like its singular and plural. This analysis also applies to the relative pronoun.

This is further supported by the fact that personal pronouns belong to the same category. Nominative and accusative pronouns have both attached and independent forms, whereas genitive pronouns have only attached forms. This is because the genitive is governed only by a preposition or a noun to which it is annexed; it cannot precede its governing word and therefore cannot be detached from it.

The attached singular pronoun is the kāf in akramtuka (“I honored you”) and marartu bika (“I passed by you”). In the plural, one says akramtukum (“I honored you all”) and marartu bikum (“I passed by you all”). In the dual, an alif is added in both the accusative and the genitive: akramtukumā (“I honored you two”) and marartu bikumā (“I passed by you two”). The same applies to the nominative: in the singular and plural, one says faʿalta (“you did”) and faʿaltum (“you all did”), and in the dual, faʿaltumā (“you two did”). The alif alone is added to mark the dual in the nominative, accusative, and genitive, just as it is added to the independent pronouns iyyākumā (“you two”) and antumā (“you two”).

All of this demonstrates that the dual of indeclinable nouns has a single form in all three cases: they made no distinction between its nominative form and its accusative and genitive forms

---

## Page 7818

in the way they do with declinable nouns—and that this uniformity is more marked in the dual than in the singular and plural. For in personal pronouns they distinguish the accusative and genitive pronouns from the nominative pronoun in the singular and dual, yet make no distinction in the dual. With demonstrative and relative forms, they make no distinction between the nominative and the other cases in the singular and plural; this applies all the more to the dual.

All praise belongs to Allah alone. May Allah bestow abundant blessings and peace upon our master Muhammad, his family, and his Companions.

Our shaykh, Shaykh al-Islām Ibn Taymiyyah, discussed this question elsewhere and mentioned the following objection:

Section

An objection may be raised against what we wrote earlier: the form also occurs with yā’ outside the nominative, like other nouns. Allah Most High says:

“Those who disbelieve will say, ‘Our Lord, show us the two among the jinn and humankind who led us astray.’”

He did not say alladhāni adallānā (“the two who led us astray”), as might be expected from what has been said about alladhīna (“those who”): that it retains the yā’ in all three cases. Allah Most High also says in the account of Moses:

“Indeed, I wish to marry you to one of these two daughters of mine.”

He did not say hātāni (“these two”). Hātāni is grammatically dependent on ibnatayya (“my two daughters”). It may be called explanatory apposition, which resembles an adjectival qualification, as in His words:

“And to Thamūd, their brother Sālih.”

However, an adjective is a derived word, or has the sense of a derived word, whereas apposition

---

## Page 7819

can involve other kinds of words, such as proper names and demonstratives. This verse is analogous to His words:

“Indeed, these two are sorcerers.”

As for His words:

“Show us the two who led us astray,”

a distinction may be drawn between the demonstrative and the relative pronoun: the demonstrative consists of two letters, unlike the relative pronoun. Its base is “alladhā,” consisting of several letters. The plural marker is then added, with the dhāl taking an i-vowel and the nūn an a-vowel; or the dual marker, with the dhāl taking an a-vowel and the nūn an i-vowel. As for the alif, I said … (1) in the accusative and genitive; for when a sound noun forms a sound plural, its final consonant takes an i-vowel in the accusative and genitive, and its nūn takes an a-vowel. When it forms a dual, its final consonant takes an a-vowel and its nūn an i-vowel in all three cases.

This shows that alif is the original dual marker. Accordingly, there are two dialectal patterns for its inflection, both found in the Quran: sometimes it follows the form alladhāni, and sometimes alladhayni. However, in His words:

“One of these two daughters of mine,”

the form used is better than “hātāni,” because the two dual forms agree in having yā’. Had hātāni been used, it would have resembled … (2), as though one were to say: “inna ibnatayya hātāni” (“Indeed, my two daughters are these two”). Using yā’ thus marks it as a clarifying dependent—an explanatory apposition completing the meaning of the noun—not a predicate completing the sentence.

As for His words:

“Indeed, these two are sorcerers,”

the noun occurs as an initial subject: the subject of (inna)

> **Footnotes:**
> (1, 2) Blank space in the original.

---

## Page 7820

and its use with alif was more pleasing in expression than saying:

“inna hādhayni la-sāhirāni” (“Indeed, these two are sorcerers”),

because alif is lighter to pronounce than yā’, and because the predicate also contains alif. When both the subject and the predicate have alif, they correspond more closely. This is a sound consideration, and nothing in the Quran is comparable to this in every respect while having yā’. Thus, nothing in sound grammatical analogy contradicts this attested, mass-transmitted reading. There are, however, subtle distinctions between the two. Those who found this problematic did so on grounds of analogy, not attested usage. Once the distinction becomes clear, the weakness of that analogy is apparent.

Someone who regards the use of alif here as established linguistic usage may answer by distinguishing between His words:

“in hādhāni” (“Indeed, these two”),

and His words:

“ihdā ibnatayya hātayni” (“One of these two daughters of mine”).

The latter is a feminine dual, whereas the former is a masculine dual. The masculine singular is dhā (“this”), with alif; thus, an alif was added alongside the dual nūn. The feminine singular, however, is dhī, dhih, or tih (“this”).

In His words:

“One of these two daughters of mine,”

the demonstrative is the dual of tī (“this”), with yā’. Using yā’ in the accusative and genitive therefore makes it more closely resemble the singular. This differs from the masculine dual, whose singular is dhā, with alif: retaining alif is thus more fitting. This is a distinction between the feminine and masculine duals. The distinction between this form and alladhayni (“the two who”) has already been explained. Accordingly, this reading accords with both attested usage and grammatical analogy, and there is no widely known

---

## Page 7821

usage in the language in which the Quran was revealed that contradicts it. And Allah knows best.

His words,

“One of these two daughters of mine,”

are comparable to the Prophet’s saying (may Allah bless him and grant him peace):

“Whoever eats from these two foul-smelling plants must not come near our mosque, for the angels are offended by what offends human beings.”

A similar example involving a relative pronoun is Ibn ‘Abbās’s statement to ‘Umar: “Tell me about the two women concerning whom Allah said: ‘But if you both support one another against him, then Allah is indeed his Protector’”—to the end of the verse.

The end. All praise is due to Allah alone.

---

## Page 7822

Surah al-Anbiyā’ (The Prophets)

He—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section

“Surah al-Anbiyā’” is the surah of the Reminder and the surah of the prophets to whom the Reminder was revealed. Allah begins it with His words:

“No new reminder comes to them from their Lord”—to the end of the verse.

And His words:

“Ask the people of the Reminder if you do not know.”

“We have certainly sent down to you a Book in which you are mentioned.”

“This is the reminder of those with me and the reminder of those before me.”

“And a reminder for the righteous.”

“And this is a blessed Reminder.”

“And We have already written in the Psalms, after the Reminder.”

And His words:

“He said, ‘My Lord, judge in truth.’”

This means—and Allah knows best—“Grant victory to those who uphold the truth,” or “Grant victory to the truth.” It has also been interpreted as: “Judge in truth between us and our people.” The prophets used to say:

“Our Lord, judge between us and our people in truth.”

And He commanded Muhammad to say:

“My Lord, judge in truth.”

Mālik narrated from Zayd ibn Aslam, who said: “Whenever the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was present at a battle, he would say: ‘He said, “My Lord, judge in truth.”’”

---

## Page 7823

Surah al-Hajj (The Pilgrimage)

The Shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—also said:

Section

Surah al-Hajj contains Meccan and Medinan revelation, revealed by night and by day, during travel and residence, in winter and in summer. It encompasses the stages of the journey to Allah, leaving out neither a stage nor an obstacle that prevents one from reaching it.

It mentions the four kinds of hearts: the blind, the diseased, the hardened, and the humble heart that is alive and finds tranquility in Allah. Despite its brevity, it contains teachings on Allah’s oneness, rulings, and exhortations whose significance is clear to anyone who reflects upon them. It also mentions all obligatory and recommended deeds: affirming Allah’s oneness, prayer, zakah, Hajj, and fasting. All of these are encompassed by the words of Allah Most High:

“O you who believe, bow down, prostrate yourselves, worship your Lord, and do good, so that you may succeed.”

His words, “And do good,” include every obligatory and recommended deed. Thus, this verse mentions particular deeds and then gives a comprehensive command. He then says:

“And strive for Allah with the striving due to Him.”

This verse and what follows leave no good unmentioned and no evil unrejected.

---

## Page 7824

Shaykh al-Islām said:

In the midst of the verses concerning the resurrection, Allah says:

“Among people are those who dispute about Allah without knowledge and follow every rebellious devil.”

“It has been decreed concerning him that whoever takes him as an ally”

He follows this with a verse concerning the resurrection, then with His words:

“Among people are those who dispute about Allah without knowledge, guidance, or an illuminating Scripture.”

“Turning away in arrogance to lead others astray from Allah’s path”

And so on, down to His words:

“Among people are those who worship Allah on the edge.”

These passages explain the condition of speculative theologians and devotees: those who dispute without knowledge and those who worship without knowledge—indeed, with doubt. For this surah concerns the religion of Abraham, who argued with knowledge and worshipped Allah with knowledge. Hence it includes mention of Hajj and the six religious communities.

His words about disputing concerning Allah without knowledge condemn everyone who does so without knowledge. They also indicate that disputing with knowledge is permissible, as Abraham did with his people. In the first passage, He condemns disputing without knowledge; in the second, disputing without knowledge, guidance, or an illuminating Scripture.

This—and Allah knows best—is an instance either of following the general with the specific or of ascending from the lower to the higher, to show that the one who argues on the basis of Scripture ranks highest, followed by the one who argues on the basis of guidance. Knowledge is thus an all-encompassing term. Some of it is acquired through inferential reasoning; this is its lowest category and is therefore singled out

---

## Page 7825

under the name “knowledge,” while each of the other categories is given its own specific name. Thus, knowledge is either acquired through inferential reasoning, which is knowledge through reflection; or through guidance by spiritual disclosure, as with hadith scholars, those endowed with keen insight, and other believers—this is “guidance”; or through the Scriptures sent down from Allah, which is the highest category. Thus, the highest is knowledge transmitted from the Scriptures, followed by the spiritual disclosures granted to Allah’s friends, then the inferential reasoning of speculative theologians and other scholars.

---

## Page 7826

And he said concerning the words of Allah Most High:

“And among people is one who worships Allah on the very edge: if good befalls him, he is reassured by it; but if a trial befalls him, he turns back on his face. He loses both this world and the Hereafter. That is the manifest loss.”

“He invokes besides Allah what can neither harm nor benefit him. That is straying far indeed.”

“He invokes one whose harm is nearer than his benefit. What an evil protector and what an evil companion!”

The latter part of this verse has perplexed many people, as a number of commentators have stated, including al-Tha‘labī and al-Baghawī. The wording here is al-Baghawī’s. He said: “This verse is among the difficult passages of the Quran, and it raises several questions. The first: they said that Allah Most High states in the first verse, ‘He invokes besides Allah what cannot harm him,’ meaning that abandoning its worship cannot harm him. His words, ‘one whose harm,’ mean the harm resulting from worshipping it.”

I say: This is one answer.

The author of al-Kashshāf mentioned a different answer. He said: “If you say, ‘Harm and benefit are denied of idols in one verse but affirmed of them in the other, and this is a contradiction,’ I say: Once the meaning is understood, this misconception disappears. Allah exposes the unbeliever’s folly in worshipping an inanimate object that has no power to harm or benefit, while, in his ignorance and misguidance, he believes concerning it

---

## Page 7827

that he seeks intercession through it when he asks it to intercede. Then, on the Day of Resurrection, this unbeliever will cry out and scream when he sees the harm the idols have brought him and his entry into the Fire for worshipping them, with no sign of the intercession he claimed for them:

‘One whose harm is nearer than his benefit—what an evil protector and what an evil companion!’

Or ‘he invokes’ is repeated, as though He said:

‘He invokes besides Allah what can neither harm nor benefit him.’

Then He said: ‘One whose harm,’ through being worshipped, ‘is nearer than his benefit,’ through being an intercessor—‘what an evil protector!’”

I say: He thus attributes its harm to its being worshipped, and mentions the harm the worshipper suffers thereby in the Hereafter.

Al-Suddī made a statement encompassing both answers in his well-known commentary. Regarding ‘what cannot harm him,’ he said: “It cannot harm him if he disobeys it.” Regarding ‘what cannot benefit him,’ he said: “The idol cannot benefit him if he obeys it.” Regarding ‘He invokes one whose harm,’ he said: “Its harm is in the Hereafter, because he worshipped it in this world.”

I say: The answer he has given is sound, but he has not explained why there is no contradiction.

We therefore say: His words, “what can neither harm nor benefit him,” deny that whatever is invoked and worshipped besides Allah has any power to benefit or harm. This encompasses everything other than

---

## Page 7828

Allah—angels, human beings, jinn, stars, and all idols. For nothing besides Allah has the power to harm or benefit either itself or anyone else.

As the Most High says when forbidding the worship of the Messiah:

“Those who say, ‘Allah is the Messiah, son of Mary,’ have certainly disbelieved. The Messiah said, ‘O Children of Israel, worship Allah, my Lord and your Lord. Whoever associates partners with Allah, Allah has forbidden Paradise to him, and his refuge is the Fire. The wrongdoers will have no helpers.’”

“Those who say, ‘Allah is the third of three,’ have certainly disbelieved. There is no god but one God. If they do not desist from what they say, a painful punishment will surely afflict those among them who disbelieve.”

“Will they not repent to Allah and seek His forgiveness? Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

“The Messiah, son of Mary, was no more than a messenger; messengers had passed away before him. His mother was a woman of truth. They both ate food. See how We make the signs clear to them, then see how they are deluded.”

“Say, ‘Do you worship besides Allah what has no power to harm or benefit you? Allah alone is the All-Hearing, All-Knowing.’”

He also said to the Seal of the Messengers:

“Say, ‘I have no power to benefit or harm myself except as Allah wills.’”

And He said:

“Say, ‘I have no power to harm you or guide you aright.’”

And He said in general terms:

“Whatever mercy Allah grants to people, none can withhold; and whatever He withholds, none can release thereafter.”

And He said:

“If Allah touches you with harm, none can remove it but He; and if He intends good for you, none can repel His bounty.”

And He said:

“Say, ‘Have you considered those you invoke besides Allah? If Allah intends harm for me, can they remove His harm? Or if He intends mercy for me, can they withhold His mercy?’ Say, ‘Allah is sufficient for me. In Him let those who trust place their trust.’”

And the man mentioned in Yā Sīn said:

“Why should I not worship the One who created me, and to whom you will be returned?”

---

## Page 7829

“Should I take gods besides Him? If the Most Merciful intends harm for me, their intercession will be of no benefit to me, nor can they rescue me.”

“Indeed, I would then be clearly astray.”

“Indeed, I have believed in your Lord, so listen to me.”

His statement:

“He invokes besides Allah that which neither harms him nor benefits him”

is an unrestricted negation, as in His statement:

“And has no power to harm or benefit them.”

Thus, it cannot harm anyone, whether he worships it or not, nor benefit anyone, whether he worships it or not. The explanation offered by some—“It does not benefit if worshipped, nor harm if not worshipped”—clarifies that there is no reason to place hope in it or fear it. This contrasts with the Lord, who honours His worshippers and has mercy on them, while humiliating and punishing those who do not worship Him.

The precise understanding, however, is that it can neither benefit nor harm in any circumstances. For Allah—glorified be He—encompasses all things in His mercy and bestows blessings upon many of His creatures even though they do not worship Him. The benefit He grants His servants is therefore not confined to His worshippers, although this requires further explanation for which this is not the place. Whatever is besides Him benefits neither those who worship it nor those who do not.

He—glorified be He—is the One who brings harm and benefit: He is able to harm whomever He wills, although the harm He brings upon His worshippers is a mercy for them. As Job said:

“Adversity has touched me, and You are the Most Merciful of the merciful.”

The Most High said:

“If Allah touches you with harm, none can remove it but He.”

He also said to His Messenger Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Say, ‘I have no power to benefit or harm myself except as Allah wills.’”

And the Most High said:

“And those who are patient in poverty and hardship and during battle.”

He—glorified be He—also brings harm upon those who cannot be described as disobedient, such as children, the insane, and animals, because of the wisdom and blessing

---

## Page 7830

and mercy involved, as explained at length elsewhere. The point here is that the negation of harm and benefit from anything besides Him is general. The one negation need not be restricted to those who worship it and the other to those who do not, although this restriction is valid when understood in a sound sense. The explanation that abandoning its worship brings no harm, while the harm resulting from worshipping it is closer than its benefit, rests upon this restriction.

That being so, we say: what is negated is the power of anything besides Him to harm or benefit.

As for His words:

“Its harm is closer than its benefit,”

we say, first: what is negated is their performing the action, in His words:

“That which neither harms him nor benefits him.”

What is affirmed, however, is a noun linked to it in a genitive construction. He did not say, “It harms more than it benefits.” Rather, He said:

“One whose harm is closer than its benefit.”

One thing may be attributed to another through the slightest association. Thus, attributing harm and benefit in this way does not necessarily mean attributing a verbal noun to its agent. Rather, a verbal noun may enter such a construction simply as a noun, like other nouns. It may also be attributed to the entity in which the action occurs, its time, its place, or the cause of its occurrence, even if that cause is not its agent, as in His words:

“Rather, it was the plotting of night and day.”

There is undoubtedly a connection between what is worshipped besides Allah and the harm suffered by its worshippers that warrants such an attribution. It is as though it were said: “One whose evil is closer than its good, and whose loss is closer than its profit.” Reflect on this.

Even if it were regarded as the agent of harm on this basis, because it is a cause of it, not because it is itself the one that

---

## Page 7831

inflicted the harm, this would be like the words of Abraham, Allah’s intimate friend, concerning the idols:

“My Lord, they have led many people astray.”

Leading people astray is thus attributed to them, and this is harmful to those whom they lead astray. Likewise, Allah says:

“They increased them in nothing but ruin.”

This resembles the sayings, “The dirham and the dinar have ruined people,” and “The two red things—gold and silk—have ruined women.” Likewise, when love and infatuation with someone cause harm, people say of that beloved: “He has tormented this man, ruined him, corrupted him, killed him, and made him stumble”—even though the beloved may be entirely unaware of the man’s condition. Similarly, people say of someone who is envied that he torments those who envy him, even though he is unaware of them.

The two Sahīh collections relate from Amr ibn Awf that the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said:

“By Allah, it is not poverty that I fear for you. Rather, I fear that the world will be opened up to you as it was opened up to those before you, and that you will compete for it as they competed for it, and it will destroy you as it destroyed them.”

He thus described the world opened up to them as what destroyed them. This was because of their love of it, their eagerness for it, and their rivalry over it, even though it is an object acted upon and has no choice of its own.

The same applies to whatever is invoked and worshipped besides Allah without having commanded people to worship it—whether because it is inanimate or because it is an obedient servant of Allah, among the angels, prophets, or righteous humans and jinn. Whatever is invoked besides Allah neither benefits nor harms, yet it is the cause of the supplicant’s invoking and worshipping it. It is that person’s worship and invocation of it that has harmed him. Thus, the harm attributed to it is different from the harm denied of it.

---

## Page 7832

Thus, the harm that a worshipper suffers through worshipping it occurs both in this world and in the Hereafter. Although the punishment of the Hereafter is more severe, those who worshipped others besides Allah suffered, because of their association of these objects of worship with Him, punishment from Allah in this world that He made a lesson for those with insight. Allah Most High says:

“That is from the accounts of the towns which We relate to you; some are still standing, while others have been mown down.”

“We did not wrong them, but they wronged themselves. Their gods, whom they invoked besides Allah, availed them nothing when the command of your Lord came, and they increased them in nothing but ruin.”

He thus made clear that those gods did not benefit them; rather, they only made their plight worse. The explanations offered here resemble those given concerning harm. One explanation is that worshipping them only made their plight worse. Another is that, on the Day of Resurrection, those gods will assist against them, making their plight worse. This resembles His words:

“They have taken gods besides Allah to be a source of strength for them.”

“No indeed! They will disavow their worship and turn against them.”

Most commentators have explained tatbīb (ruin) as causing loss, as in His words, Most High:

“May the hands of Abū Lahab be ruined, and ruined is he.”

Others have explained it as destruction and annihilation, while others have said that it means: they only made their plight worse.

His words:

“Their gods, whom they invoked besides Allah, availed them nothing when the command of your Lord came, and they increased them in nothing but ruin”

use the past tense, indicating that this occurred in this world.

It may be asked: “All the evil came from the worshippers themselves, so why is it said, ‘So they added nothing to them’?”

The answer is that they would have been punished for their disbelief in Allah even if they had not worshipped those gods. When, in addition, they worshipped them, they thereby increased in disbelief and punishment. Thus, those gods added only to their loss and suffering; they brought them no gain or good.

---

## Page 7833

Surah al-Mu’minūn (The Believers)

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—said:

Concerning His words, Most High:

“Does he promise you that, when you have died and become dust and bones, that you will be brought forth?”

A long interval separates the particle anna and its subject from its predicate, so He repeats (anna) before the predicate to emphasize it. A parallel occurs in His words, Most High:

“Do they not know that whoever opposes Allah and His Messenger will indeed have the Fire of Hell?”

Because the intervening passage is long, He repeats (anna). This is the explanation given by al-Zajjāj and a group of scholars.

A better explanation is that each of these two sentences is a conditional sentence consisting of two clauses expressing a condition and its consequence. The conditional sentence is emphasized with anna, just as it is emphasized in the poet’s words:

“Indeed, whoever enters the church one day
Will find young wild cattle and gazelles within.”

The clause expressing the consequence is then emphasized with anna, since it conveys the intended point, just as it is emphasized in His words, Most High:

“As for those who hold firmly to the Book and establish prayer—indeed, We do not let the reward of those who set things right go to waste.”

A parallel example of combining emphasis on the overall sentence, composed of the condition and its consequence,

---

## Page 7834

and emphasis on the clause expressing the consequence is His saying, Most High:

“Indeed, whoever fears Allah and remains patient—then surely Allah does not let the reward of those who do good go to waste.”

Here, one cannot say that inna was repeated because of the length of the intervening passage. Another example is His saying, Most High:

“Indeed, whoever comes to his Lord as a criminal—then surely Hell awaits him, where he will neither die nor live.”

Another example is:

“That whoever among you does evil in ignorance, then repents afterwards and makes amends—then surely He is Forgiving, Merciful.”

These are two deliberate emphases conveying two different meanings. Do you not see that emphasizing His words “Forgiving, Merciful” with inna is distinct from using anna to emphasize the statement, “Whoever among you does evil in ignorance, then repents afterwards and makes amends—then surely He is Forgiving, Merciful” towards him? This is perfectly clear, with no obscurity, and occurs frequently in the Quran and in Arabic speech.

As for His saying, Most High:

“Their only words were, ‘Our Lord, forgive us our sins,’”

there is no repetition here whatsoever. “Their words” is the predicate of kāna (“was”), placed before its subject, while “that they said” is construed as a verbal noun and constitutes the subject. Thus, the two expressions are the subject and predicate of kāna. The meaning is: They had nothing to say except, “Our Lord, forgive us our sins.” A parallel is His saying, Most High:

“His people’s only response was to say…”

A response is itself a statement. Likewise, you say: “So-and-so has nothing to say except, ‘There is no power nor strength except through Allah.’” There is therefore no repetition at all.

As for His saying, Most High, “Although they had been, before it was sent down upon them, before it

---

## Page 7835

, in utter despair,” this is among the most difficult examples adduced, and people have found it exceedingly hard to understand. Many scholars of grammar and Quranic commentary have therefore said that it is simply repetition for emphasis. Al-Zamakhsharī said:

“‘Before it’ serves to reinforce the statement, as in His saying, Most High: ‘Thus the outcome for both was that they would be in the Fire, abiding in it forever.’ The purpose of the emphasis here is to indicate that so much time had passed since they last experienced rain that their despair had become entrenched and their despondency prolonged. Their joy at the rain was therefore proportionate to their concern over it.”

Such were his words. They contain two false claims: first, his assertion that this is repetition; and second, his illustrating it with His saying, Most High:

“Thus the outcome for both was that they would be in the Fire, abiding in it forever.”

The first “in” functions as it does when you say, “Zayd is in the house,” meaning that he is present or situated there. The second, however, is governed by “abiding forever,” which conveys a meaning distinct from mere presence. Since the two governing expressions differ, both prepositions are mentioned. Had only one been used, this would have been an instance of omission, with the other indicating what had been omitted. Such a construction is not called repetition. A parallel is your saying, “Zayd is in the house, sleeping in it,” or “residing in it,” and similar expressions consisting of two clauses, each with its own distinct qualification.

As for His saying:

“Before it was sent down upon them, before it,”

this is not repetition. Rather, it contains a subtle meaning. The meaning is: Although they had been, before it was sent down

---

## Page 7836

upon them as rain—before this particular rainfall—in utter despair. There are thus two senses of “before”: one relating to rainfall in general, and another relating to that particular rainfall not having occurred before that time. Before it fell, they therefore experienced two kinds of despair: despair because it was nowhere to be seen, and despair because it was delayed beyond its expected time.

The first “before” is a temporal adverb qualifying their despair, while the second qualifies the rain’s arrival and being sent down. The verse thus contains two adverbials governed by two distinct actions: the sending down of rain and the falling into despair. One adverbial relates to despair, and the other to the rain’s descent.

An illustration of this is what you would say if you were accustomed to receiving a gift from someone, but it was delayed beyond its usual time and he then brought it to you: “I had despaired.”

---

## Page 7837

Surah al-Nūr (The Light)

The godly shaykh and second exemplar of truthfulness; imam of imams and mufti of the Muslim community; ocean of knowledge and full moon among the stars; mainstay of the hadith masters and champion of meanings and words; peerless in his age and unequalled in his time; Shaykh al-Islām and leader of the illustrious imams; the great scholar of his era and interpreter of the Quran; standard-bearer of the ascetics and unrivalled among worshippers; vanquisher of the innovators and last of the scholars qualified for independent legal reasoning; the surging ocean and keen, cutting sword—

Abū al-‘Abbās Taqī al-Dīn Ahmad ibn Shihāb al-Dīn Abū al-Mahāsin ‘Abd al-Halīm ibn Shaykh al-Islām Majd al-Dīn Abū al-Barakāt ‘Abd al-Salām ibn Abū Muhammad ‘Abd Allāh ibn Abū al-Qāsim al-Khidr ibn Muhammad ibn al-Khidr ‘Alī ibn ‘Abd Allāh ibn Taymiyyah al-Harrānī—may Allah sanctify his soul, illuminate his tomb, have mercy upon him, be pleased with him, and grant him contentment—said:

---

## Page 7838

Section: Meanings Derived from Surah al-Nūr

Allah Most High says:

“This is a surah We have sent down and made binding, and in it We have sent down clear verses, so that you may take heed.”

He thus made its provisions binding through clear explanations and the precise delineation of Allah’s limits. Whoever passes beyond what these limits permit into what is forbidden has wronged himself, and whoever approaches what they forbid has transgressed and overstepped the limits.

In this surah, He prescribed the punishment for the two people guilty of unlawful sexual intercourse: one hundred lashes each. He also explained the testimony required to establish unlawful sexual intercourse: four testimonies. Likewise, He prescribed the testimony of spouses engaging in mutual imprecation: each makes four declarations, swearing by Allah.

He forbade overstepping His limits concerning sexual relations, honour and privacy, and concerning obedience to someone vested with authority, whether within his home or within his jurisdiction. No one is to leave or enter without his permission.

Rights are of two kinds: those belonging to Allah, whose limits must not be transgressed; and those belonging to people, over which they have authority, so that no action may be taken without the right-holder’s permission. No one may do anything affecting another person’s rights except with Allah’s permission, even if the right-holder has not given permission. Allah’s permission is the foundation; the right-holder’s permission applies where Allah has authorized it and granted him the right to give it.

For this reason, He included in this surah seeking permission to enter homes and partake of food, and seeking permission in

---

## Page 7839

communal affairs such as prayer, jihad and the like. At its centre He placed the mention of light, which is the source of all goodness and sets everything right. This light arises from obeying Allah’s commands, avoiding His prohibitions, and patiently persisting in doing so, for patience is radiance. Allah grants light to whoever observes His limits out of fear of Him, as He Most High says:

“Fear Allah and believe in His Messenger; He will grant you a double share of His mercy, give you a light by which to walk, and forgive you.”

The opposite of light is darkness. Thus, after mentioning light and the deeds of the believers in this surah, He mentioned the deeds of the disbelievers and the people of innovation and misguidance, saying:

“As for those who disbelieve, their deeds are like a mirage in a desert,”

up to His words:

“Layers of darkness, one above another. If he stretches out his hand, he can hardly see it. Whoever Allah does not grant light has no light.”

Likewise, wrongdoing will be darkness on the Day of Resurrection, and a servant’s wronging himself is a form of wrongdoing. An evil deed brings darkness to the heart, blackness to the face, weakness to the body, diminished provision, and hatred in the hearts of others, as has been reported from Ibn Abbās.

This is further clarified by Allah’s likening the believers’ faith to light and the disbelievers’ deeds to darkness. “Faith” is a comprehensive term for everything Allah loves and is pleased with. And “disbelief”

---

## Page 7840

is a comprehensive term for everything Allah hates and forbids, although a servant does not become a disbeliever when he possesses the foundation of faith along with some branches of disbelief in the form of sins, just as he is not a believer when he possesses the foundation of disbelief along with some branches of faith. Lowering the gaze has a particular connection with light, as we shall explain, if Allah Most High wills.

Abū Hurayrah narrated that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When a servant sins, a black spot is marked on his heart. If he repents, desists and seeks forgiveness, his heart is polished clean. But if he continues sinning, the spot grows until it covers his heart. That is the stain Allah mentioned: ‘No indeed! Rather, their hearts have been stained by what they used to earn.’”

Al-Tirmidhī narrated this and graded it authentic. It is also reported in the Sahīh that he said:

“A veil comes over my heart, and I seek Allah’s forgiveness a hundred times a day.”

This veil is thin, thinner than a cloud. He thus explained that he seeks Allah’s forgiveness in a manner that removes this veil from the heart, so that it does not become a black spot, just as a black spot, when removed, does not become an enveloping stain.

Hudhayfah said:

“Faith first appears in the heart as a white speck. The more a servant’s faith increases, the whiter his heart becomes. If you were to uncover the believer’s heart, you would see it white and radiant. Hypocrisy first appears in it as a black speck. The more a servant’s hypocrisy increases, the blacker his heart becomes. If you were to uncover the hypocrite’s heart, you would find it a murky black.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When light enters the heart, it opens and expands.”

He was asked, “Is there any sign of that, O Messenger of Allah?”

He replied, “Yes

---

## Page 7841

—turning away from the abode of delusion, turning toward the abode of eternity, and preparing for death before it comes.”

In the introduction Imām Ahmad wrote to his book refuting the Jahmiyyah and the heretics, he said:

“Praise be to Allah, who, in every interval without messengers, preserves a remnant of scholars who call the misguided to guidance and patiently endure the harm they suffer from them. Through Allah’s Book they bring the dead to life, and through Allah’s light they give sight to the blind. How many a victim slain by Iblīs have they brought back to life, and how many a lost, wandering, bewildered person have they guided! How good is their effect upon people, and how ugly is people’s treatment of them! They defend Allah’s Book against the distortions of extremists, the false claims of those who promote falsehood, and the interpretations of the ignorant—those who have raised the banners of innovation and given free rein to discord. They differ over the Book, oppose the Book, and agree on departing from the Book. They speak on Allah’s behalf, about Allah, and about Allah’s Book without knowledge. They use ambiguous language and deceive ignorant people through the confusion they create for them. We seek refuge in Allah from the specious arguments of those who lead others astray.”

I say:

In several places in His Book, Allah, glorified is He, has contrasted the people of guidance with those of misguidance, and the obedient with the disobedient, in terms similar to these. For example, He Most High says:

“The blind and the seeing are not alike,”

“nor are darkness and light,”

“nor are shade and scorching heat,”

“nor are the living and the dead alike.”

He also says:

“The two groups are like the blind and deaf, and the seeing and hearing,”

to the end of the verse. And He says concerning the hypocrites:

---

## Page 7842

“Their likeness is that of one who kindled a fire,” and the following verses. He also says:

“Allah is the Protector of those who believe,”

to the end of the verse. And He says:

“A Book which We have revealed to you so that you may bring people out of darkness into light.”

There are many verses on this subject.

This light, which the believer possesses in this world in proportion to the soundness of his deeds and beliefs, will become manifest in the Hereafter, as He Most High says:

“Their light will shine ahead of them and on their right,”

to the end of the verse. Here He mentions light immediately after His command to repent, just as He mentions it in Surah al-Nūr after His command to lower the gaze and His command to repent in His words:

“Turn to Allah in repentance, all of you, O believers, so that you may succeed.”

He mentions this after His commands concerning the rights of family members and spouses and matters relating to women. In Surah al-Hadīd, He says:

“On the Day you see the believing men and women, their light shining ahead of them and on their right,”

and the subsequent verses, up to His words concerning the hypocrites:

“The Fire is your refuge; it is your proper place—an evil destination!”

Thus He, glorified is He, tells us that the hypocrites will lose the light by which the believers had been walking. They will ask to draw some light from them, but will be prevented from doing so by a barrier erected between them and the believers. Likewise, when the hypocrites lost their light in this world, their likeness was that of someone who kindled a fire: when it illuminated everything around him, Allah took away their light and left them in darkness.

As for His words, “The woman and the man who commit unlawful sexual intercourse,” and the rest of the verse, He commands that they both be punished and made to suffer their penalty in the presence of a group of believers. This follows the offender’s own testimony against himself or the testimony of believers against him; for when a sin is committed openly, its punishment is

---

## Page 7843

public, as stated in the report:

“Whoever sins in secret should repent in secret, and whoever sins openly should repent openly.”

Concealing such wrongdoing is not the concealment that Allah Most High loves, as described in the hadith:

“Whoever conceals a Muslim’s faults, Allah will conceal his faults.”

Rather, concealing it amounts to condoning manifest wrongdoing. Another hadith states:

“When a sin is concealed, it harms only the one who commits it; but when it is committed openly and is not denounced, it harms the community at large.”

Thus, when a sin is committed publicly, its punishment is made public, in accordance with whatever measure of justice is possible.

For this reason, speaking of someone who openly practises religious innovations and wickedness does not constitute backbiting, as has been reported from al-Hasan al-Basrī and others. By making such conduct public, he deserves punishment from the Muslims. The least of this is that he be censured for it, so that he may be deterred and people may keep away from him and avoid associating with him. Were he not censured and his wickedness, sin or religious innovation not mentioned, people would be deceived by him. He might even lead some of them to engage in the same conduct, while himself growing bolder, more wicked and more sinful. When his conduct is mentioned, however, he is restrained, and others refrain both from such conduct and from keeping his company or associating with him. Al-Hasan al-Basrī said:

“Are you reluctant to speak of the wicked person? Mention his faults so that people may beware of him.”

This has also been reported as a saying traced back to the Prophet. “Wickedness” is a comprehensive designation applying to anyone who openly commits sin or utters foul speech that reveals to the listener the wickedness of the speaker’s heart. For this reason, a person deserves to be shunned if he openly displays religious innovation, sin, wickedness or shamelessness, or associates with someone of this character, without caring that people censure him; for

---

## Page 7844

shunning him is a form of discretionary punishment. If he commits evil deeds openly, he is shunned openly; if he commits them secretly, he is shunned privately. Such shunning is on account of evil deeds, and forsaking evil deeds means abandoning what Allah has forbidden. As the Most High says:

“And shun defilement.”

The Most High also says:

“And leave them graciously.”

And He says:

“He has already revealed to you in the Book that when you hear Allah’s verses being denied and ridiculed, you must not sit with them until they engage in a different conversation; otherwise, you would be like them.”

It has been reported concerning ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb that when his son ʿAbd al-Rahmān drank wine in Egypt, his brother took him to the governor of Egypt, ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀs, to receive the prescribed flogging. He administered it privately, although floggings were normally carried out publicly. ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb sent word to ʿAmr reproaching him for this. ʿUmar did not recognize that flogging as sufficient: he summoned his son to Medina and administered the prescribed flogging publicly, not considering the obligation discharged by the first punishment. His son lived for some time afterward, then fell ill and died. He did not die from that flogging, nor did ʿUmar flog him after his death, as liars claim.

Regarding the words of the Most High:

“Do not let pity for the two overcome you in applying Allah’s law,”

and the remainder of the verse: the Most High forbids what Satan urges concerning punishments generally and sexual immorality particularly. For this matter revolves around affection, desire, and pity, which Satan makes appealing by inclining people’s hearts sympathetically towards those who commit sexual immorality and fostering compassion for them. Because of this affliction, many people fall into condoning sexual immorality and lacking protective jealousy when

---

## Page 7845

they see someone whom one of their associates desires or with whom he has an improper relationship, or notice his affection, inclination, yearning, and passion—even if he is their own son—and feel pity for him. They imagine this to be compassion towards others, gentleness in dealing with them, and noble character. In reality, it is condoning sexual immorality, degradation, lack of religious commitment, weakness of faith, cooperation in sin and transgression, and failure to forbid one another from indecency and wrongdoing.

Through this, a person becomes involved in facilitating sexual immorality, the gravest form of condoning it. Such was the wicked old woman who joined her people in approving their practice of approaching males sexually and helped them in it. Outwardly, she professed Islam, following the religion of her husband, Lot; inwardly, she was a hypocrite who followed her people’s religion. She did not detest their conduct as Lot did: he denounced it, forbade them to engage in it, and hated it. Likewise, the women in Egypt helped the wife of al-ʿAzīz in her efforts to entice Joseph into committing sexual immorality with her. For this reason, he said:

“My Lord, prison is dearer to me than what they invite me to.”

This was after they had said:

“Indeed, we see her to be clearly misguided.”

There is no doubt that love of sexual immorality is a disease of the heart, for desire produces intoxication. As the Most High said concerning the people of Lot:

“Indeed, they were wandering blindly in their intoxication.”

In the two Sahīh collections, with the wording given here being Muslim’s, Abū Hurayrah reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The two eyes commit adultery, and their adultery is looking.”

And so on to the end of the hadith. Thus, many people seek only some of these

---

## Page 7846

forms mentioned in this hadith: looking, taking pleasure, and conversing. Some go further, to touching and intimate physical contact; others kiss and look. All of this is forbidden. Allah, Mighty and Majestic, has forbidden us to let pity for those who commit adultery prevent us from carrying out the prescribed punishment upon them. How much more, then, should we apply lesser measures, such as shunning, inward discipline, prohibition, rebuke, and the like. Rather, the defiantly sinful should be hated and detested for indulging in the forms of adultery mentioned in the preceding hadith and elsewhere.

For even if someone passionately in love desires only to look at the beloved and enjoy their appearance and conversation, his cure does not lie in giving his soul what it loves and craves in these things. He is ill. If a sick person craves something harmful or recoils from taking unpleasant medicine, and our pity leads us to prevent him from drinking it, we have helped him pursue what harms or destroys him and abandon what benefits him. His illness then worsens until he dies. The same applies to the sinful person who is passionately in love, and others like him: he is ill. Compassion and mercy towards him do not mean allowing him to indulge his forbidden desires. He must not be helped to do so, nor allowed to abandon the acts of obedience that benefit him and remove his illness. The Most High said:

“Indeed, prayer prohibits immorality and wrongdoing.”

That is, prayer contains healing—and something greater still.

Rather, compassion towards him means helping him take the medicine, however unpleasant it may be: prayer, for example, with its words of remembrance and supplications. It also means keeping him from whatever strengthens his disease and worsens his illness, even if he craves it. Let no one imagine that, if he obtains gratification

---

## Page 7847

through something forbidden, his affliction will subside. Rather, this causes him great distress and ultimately increases his affliction and illness. Even if his suffering subsides and his agitation settles immediately after indulging himself, this is followed by a severe, intractable illness from which he cannot escape. What is required, instead, is to avert the greater of two harms by enduring the lesser, before the disease takes firm hold and carries him to ruin and destruction. It is well known that the pain of beneficial treatment is easier and lighter to bear than the pain of a lasting illness.

This makes it clear to you that all punishments prescribed by Islamic law are beneficial remedies through which Allah heals the diseases of the heart. They are an expression of Allah’s mercy and compassion towards His servants, encompassed by His words, Most High:

“And We have not sent you except as a mercy to the worlds.”

Whoever abandons this beneficial mercy out of pity for the sick person has thereby helped bring about his suffering and destruction, even if he intends nothing but good, for in this he is ignorant and foolish. Some ignorant women and men behave this way towards their sick and towards those they raise—their children, young servants, and others. Out of pity, they refrain from disciplining and punishing them for the evil they commit and the good they neglect, thus becoming a cause of their corruption, hostility, and destruction.

Some people are overcome with pity for them because they share the same illness and have themselves experienced the same intensity of desire, coldness of heart, and tolerance of sexual misconduct. They therefore fail to carry out the punishment Allah has commanded. In doing so, such a person is among the most unjust and most tolerant of sexual misconduct towards himself and others like him. He is like a group of sick people for whom a physician has prescribed what will benefit them

---

## Page 7848

but their leader found it bitter, stopped drinking it, and forbade its being given to the others.

Some are overcome with pity because one of the two guilty of unlawful intercourse is dear to them: because they love that person’s appearance and beauty, whether through passionate infatuation or otherwise; because of kinship or affection; because that person has treated them kindly; because of some worldly benefit they hope to obtain from that person; or for some other reason. Or they are moved by the pain of punishment, which softens the heart.

Such a person interprets the following saying to support his position:

“Allah shows mercy only to those of His servants who are merciful.”

The fool also quotes:

“The merciful are shown mercy by the Most Merciful. Show mercy to those on earth, and He who is above the heavens will show you mercy.”

And he cites other such sayings. Yet the matter is not as he claims. Rather, this is putting something where it does not belong. Indeed, the following has been related in a hadith:

“No dayyūth (a man who tolerates sexual immorality in his family) will enter Paradise.”

Whoever does not hate indecent acts, detest them and those who commit them, and become angry upon seeing or hearing of them will not desire punishment for them. Thus, punishment for such acts continues to pain his heart. Allah Most High says:

“Do not let pity for them deter you from enforcing Allah’s law.”

And so the verse continues. Allah’s religion consists in obedience to Him and His Messenger, founded upon love for Him and His Messenger, and upon loving Allah and His Messenger more than anyone or anything else. Allah loves tenderness and mercy, provided they do not undermine His religion.

It is reported in the Sahīh collection that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah shows mercy only to those of His servants who are merciful.”

He also said:

“Allah does not show mercy to those who do not show mercy to people.”

And he said:

---

## Page 7849

“Whoever does not show mercy will not be shown mercy.”

It is also reported in the Sunan collections:

“The merciful are shown mercy by the Most Merciful. Show mercy to those on earth, and He who is above the heavens will show you mercy.”

Such mercy is good and is commanded, either as an obligation or as a recommendation, unlike pity that obstructs the enforcement of Allah’s religion, which is forbidden. Satan wants people to go to excess in all their affairs. If he sees someone inclined towards mercy, he makes mercy seem so appealing that the person no longer hates what Allah hates or feels protective jealousy over what arouses Allah’s protective jealousy. If he sees someone inclined towards severity, he makes severity for reasons other than Allah’s sake seem appealing, until that person abandons the kindness, righteousness, gentleness, maintenance of ties, and mercy commanded by Allah and His Messenger. He then goes too far in severity, exceeding what Allah and His Messenger love in his condemnation, hatred, and punishment.

Such a person abandons the mercy and kindness Allah has commanded, making himself blameworthy and sinful in this respect. He also goes to excess in the severity commanded by Allah and His Messenger, until he oversteps the bounds. This is part of his excess in his affairs. The former is sinful, and the latter is excessive:

“Indeed, He does not love those who commit excess.”

Let both say:

“Our Lord, forgive us our sins and our excesses in our affairs, make our feet firm, and grant us victory over the disbelieving people.”

As for His words, Most High:

“If you believe in Allah and the Last Day,”

the believer in Allah and the Last Day does what Allah and His Messenger love and forbids what Allah and His Messenger hate. Whoever does not believe in Allah and the Last Day follows his own desires. At times, he is overcome by pity

---

## Page 7850

out of personal desire, and at other times severity overcomes him out of personal desire. In both cases, he follows his desires without guidance from Allah:

“And who is more astray than one who follows his own desires without guidance from Allah?”

Unlawful sexual intercourse is a major sin. As for looking and physical intimacy, minor lapses in these matters are forgiven through avoidance of major sins. But if someone persists in looking or physical intimacy, it becomes a major sin. Persistence in such conduct may be more serious than a few acts of grave indecency. Indeed, the harm of continually looking with desire, together with the infatuation, companionship, and physical intimacy connected with it, may be far greater than the harm of unlawful intercourse that is not persisted in.

For this reason, the jurists stipulate that an upright witness must neither commit a major sin nor persist in a minor one. A hadith traced back to the Prophet states:

“No sin is minor when persisted in, and no sin is major when forgiveness is sought.”

Indeed, looking and physical intimacy may lead a man to associate partners with Allah, as He, Most High, says:

“Among people are those who take others besides Allah as equals, loving them as they should love Allah.”

Thus, infatuation with physical beauty arises only from weakness in one’s love of Allah and weakness of faith. Allah, Most High, mentions it in the Quran only in connection with the wife of al-‘Azīz, who associated partners with Allah, and the people of Lot, who likewise associated partners with Him. The utterly infatuated lover becomes a slave to his beloved, submitting to that person, his heart held captive.

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, brought together mention of the prescribed punishments—if someone’s intercession prevents one of Allah’s prescribed punishments, he has opposed Allah—in the report narrated by Abū Dāwūd from Ibn ‘Umar, who said: The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever’s intercession prevents a prescribed punishment

---

## Page 7851

ordained by Allah has opposed Allah in His command. Whoever knowingly argues in support of falsehood remains under Allah’s displeasure until he desists. Whoever attributes to a Muslim something that is not true of him will be confined in Radghat al-Khabāl until he extricates himself from what he said.”

Thus, anyone who intercedes to prevent the prescribed punishments from being carried out opposes Allah in His command, for Allah has commanded punishment for transgressing His limits. A believer must therefore not let compassion for those who practise religious innovation, wickedness and sin, or for oppressors, overcome him.

All of this is summed up in Allah’s description of the believers:

“Humble towards the believers, firm towards the disbelievers.”

And His saying:

“Firm against the disbelievers, merciful among themselves.”

All these major sins are branches of disbelief. A Muslim does not become a disbeliever merely by committing a major sin, but he forfeits the description of having the required degree of faith. Thus, in the authentic hadith collections, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“A person who commits unlawful sexual intercourse is not a believer while committing it.”

And so the hadith continues. Their deficiency in faith warrants withholding compassion and mercy from them, and that branch of disbelief makes them deserving of severity proportionate to it. There is no contradiction in one and the same person being shown mercy and loved in one respect, yet subjected to punishment and hated in another, or being rewarded in one respect and punished in another.

The position of Ahl al-Sunnah wa-l-Jamāʿah is that both can coexist in the same person, contrary to what the Khawārij and others like them among the Muʿtazilah claim. According to them, anyone among those who face the qiblah who deserves punishment will never leave the Fire. They thus held that such people who worship Allah alone must remain there forever.
