“When you see them, their bodies impress you, and when they speak, you listen to what they say. They are like propped-up logs. They think every cry is directed against them. They are the enemy, so beware of them. May Allah destroy them!”

If, then, Allah has said what He has said about these hypocrites, whose bodies impress the beholder because of their splendour, fine appearance, and outward adornment—even though they are not people one looks at with desire—what, then, of those whom one looks at with desire?

---

## Page 7974

This is because a person may be looked at on account of his faith and piety; here, what matters is his heart and deeds, not his appearance. He may also be looked at because his form points to the One who fashioned him, and this is good. Or he may be looked at out of admiration for his physical beauty, just as one looks at horses and other animals, or at trees, rivers, and flowers. If this, too, involves admiration for worldly life, status, and wealth, it is blameworthy, in accordance with His words:

“Do not gaze longingly at the splendour of worldly life We have granted some groups of them to enjoy, so that We may test them thereby.”

If, however, it does not diminish one’s religious commitment and merely affords the soul some rest—as when looking at flowers—then it belongs to those idle pursuits that do not help one uphold the truth.

Whenever any of these kinds of looking is accompanied by desire, it is undoubtedly forbidden, whether the desire is for pleasure through looking or for intercourse. There is a difference between what a person feels when looking at trees and flowers and what he feels when looking at women and beardless youths. This distinction accounts for the difference in the religious ruling. Looking at beardless youths thus falls into three categories:

“The first” is looking accompanied by desire. This is forbidden by consensus.

---

## Page 7975

And “the second” is looking that is known with certainty to involve no desire, such as a scrupulously pious man looking at his handsome son, his beautiful daughter, or his beautiful mother. Such looking is not accompanied by desire unless the man is among the most depraved of people. Whenever desire accompanies it, it becomes forbidden.

The same applies to someone whose heart is not inclined towards beardless youths, as was the case with the Companions and with peoples unfamiliar with this indecency. In this respect, such a person makes no distinction between looking at his own son, his neighbour’s son, or an unrelated boy. No desire enters his heart, for he has not become accustomed to such things, and his heart was free of them to begin with.

In the time of the Companions, slave women used to walk along the streets with their heads uncovered and serve men, while people’s hearts remained sound. But if a man wished to let beautiful Turkish slave women walk among people in places and times such as these, as those slave women once did, this would be a source of corruption.

The same applies to handsome beardless youths. They should not go out into places and alleyways where there is reason to fear that they will become a source of temptation, except as needed. A handsome beardless youth must therefore not be allowed to display his beauty, sit in the bathhouse among unrelated men, dance among men, or engage in similar things that tempt people. The same applies to looking at him.

The scholars’ disagreement concerns only “the third category” of looking: looking at him without desire, but with fear that it may be aroused. On this there are two views within

---

## Page 7976

Ahmad’s school. The sounder of the two—which is also reported as the explicit ruling of al-Shāfi‘ī and others—is that it is impermissible. The “second” view is that it is permissible, because the presumption is that desire will not be aroused, and mere doubt does not make it forbidden, though it may be disliked.

The first view is the stronger one. Likewise, the stronger view in the schools of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad is that looking without need at the face of a woman outside the prohibited degrees of marriage is impermissible, even when desire is absent, because there is a fear that it may be aroused. For this reason, being alone with such a woman is forbidden, since it is a likely occasion for temptation. The governing principle is that anything conducive to temptation is impermissible: avenues to corruption must be blocked unless there is an overriding benefit. Thus, looking that may lead to temptation is forbidden unless there is an overriding need, as when a prospective husband, a physician, or someone in a similar position looks at a woman. Looking is permissible when needed, provided there is no desire. Looking without need at someone who may be a source of temptation, however, is impermissible.

If someone repeatedly looks at a beardless youth or the like, prolongs his gaze, and says, “I am not looking out of desire,” his claim is not to be believed. If he has no reason that makes looking necessary, then he looks only for the pleasure this brings to his heart.

An accidental glance, however, is pardoned if he turns his gaze away, as established in the authentic hadith collections:

“Jarīr said: I asked the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, about an accidental glance. He said, ‘Avert your gaze.’”

It is also related in the Sunan: “He said to ‘Alī, may Allah be pleased with him:

---

## Page 7977

‘O ‘Alī, do not follow one glance with another. The first is yours, but the second is not.’” A hadith in the Musnad and elsewhere states:

“The gaze is a poisoned arrow from the arrows of Satan.”

It also contains:

“Whoever looks at a woman’s beauty, then lowers his gaze from her, Allah will instill in his heart a sweetness of worship that he will experience until the Day of Resurrection.”

Or words to that effect.

Thus it is said that lowering one’s gaze from those whom it is forbidden to look at—such as a woman or a handsome beardless youth—brings three benefits of immense value.

The first is the sweetness and delight of faith, which are sweeter and more pleasing than what one has given up for Allah. Whoever gives something up for Allah, Allah replaces it with something better. The soul loves to gaze at such beauty, especially the souls of those devoted to self-discipline and purity. A tenderness remains within them that draws them to beautiful forms, until such beauty seizes one of them and overpowers him as a beast of prey would.

For this reason, one of the Successors said: “I fear more for a young man who has repented when a handsome youth sits beside him than when a beast of prey sits beside him.”

Another said: “Beware of gazing at the sons of kings, for the temptation they pose is like that of maidens.”

The leading scholars and religious authorities—including the imams of guidance and the shaykhs of the spiritual path—have continually urged people to avoid keeping company with youths. Indeed, it is related that Fath al-Mawsilī said: “I kept company with thirty of

---

## Page 7978

the abdāl, each of whom advised me, upon parting, to avoid keeping company with youths.” Another said:

“No servant falls from Allah’s favor without Allah afflicting him with the company of these foul creatures.”

Gazing then gives rise to love. It becomes attachment, because the heart attaches itself to the beloved; then yearning, because the heart pours itself toward the beloved; then consuming passion, because it clings to the heart like a creditor who will not leave his debtor. Then comes passionate love, until it becomes enslavement. Someone enslaved by love is reduced to servitude, and Taym Allah means “servant of Allah.” Thus the heart becomes a slave to someone unfit even to be a brother or a servant.

This afflicts only those who turn away from sincere devotion to Allah and harbor some form of associating partners with Him. As for those sincerely devoted to Him, Allah Most High says concerning Joseph, peace be upon him:

“Thus it was, so that We might turn evil and indecency away from him. Indeed, he was one of Our chosen servants.”

The wife of al-ʿAzīz was a polytheist, and despite being married, she fell into the evil she committed. Joseph, peace be upon him, however, was protected by Allah through his sincere devotion to Him, despite being unmarried, despite her attempts to seduce him and her enlisting the women’s help against him, and despite her punishing him with imprisonment for remaining chaste. This fulfilled the words:

“I will surely mislead them all, except Your chosen servants among them.”

Allah Most High says:

“Indeed, you have no authority over My servants, except those who follow you among the wayward.”

Waywardness means following one’s desires, and this matter is among the greatest avenues of following desire. As for those who advocate passionate love for beautiful forms—whether among the philosophizers, such as Ibn Sīnā and his like, or among the Persians, as is related

---

## Page 7979

concerning some of them among the ignorant Sufis—such people are misguided. Besides sharing the Jews’ waywardness and the Christians’ misguidance, they have exceeded both communities in this regard. Even if such love is thought to benefit the lover by refining his soul and improving his character, or the beloved through attending to his interests, teaching him, cultivating his character, and so forth, its harm far outweighs its benefit. How can its benefit compare with its sin?

This is no different from saying that fornication benefits both parties through the pleasure and enjoyment the man obtains and the payment and other benefits the woman receives, or saying that drinking wine has physical and psychological benefits. Allah Most High says concerning wine and gambling:

“Say: ‘In them is great sin and benefits for people, but their sin is greater than their benefit.’”

This was before the prohibition, to say nothing of what He said when prohibiting them and afterward. Worshipful devotion to these forms is a kind of indecency; its inward aspect belongs to hidden indecency and is a hidden sin. Allah Most High says:

“Abandon both outward and inward sin.”

And He Most High says:

“Say: ‘My Lord has only forbidden indecencies, whether outward or inward.’”

And He Most High says:

“When they commit an indecency, they say: ‘We found our forefathers doing it, and Allah has commanded us to do it.’ Say: ‘Allah does not command indecency. Do you say about Allah what you do not know?’”

There is no disagreement among the imams of the religion that this is not recommended, just as it is not obligatory. Whoever regards it as praiseworthy and commends it has departed from the consensus of Muslims, Jews, and Christians—indeed, from the position held by sensible people of every nation. He is

---

## Page 7980

one of those who follow their desires without guidance from Allah.

“And who is more astray than one who follows his desires without guidance from Allah? Indeed, Allah does not guide the wrongdoing people.”

And He Most High says:

“But as for one who feared standing before his Lord and restrained himself from desire, Paradise will indeed be his refuge.”

And He Most High says:

“Do not follow desire, lest it lead you astray from the way of Allah. Indeed, those who stray from the way of Allah will have a severe punishment for having forgotten the Day of Reckoning.”

As for someone who gazes at beardless youths, imagining that he is looking at manifestations of divine beauty, and makes this his path to Allah—as some groups who claim spiritual knowledge do—his assertion constitutes greater disbelief than the assertion of idol-worshippers or the disbelief of the people of Lot.

Such people are among the worst apostate heretics, who must be put to death according to the consensus of every religious community. For the idol-worshippers said:

“We worship them only so that they may bring us nearer to Allah.”

These people, however, maintain that Allah—glorified is He—is present in the idols themselves and dwells within them. By His appearance and self-disclosure in created things, they do not mean that these are proofs of Him and signs pointing to Him. Rather, they mean that He—glorified is He—has appeared and disclosed Himself within them. They liken this to the presence of water in a tuft of wool, butter in milk, oil in olives, oil in sesame seeds, and similar things, implying that His very Essence dwells within His creatures or is united with them.

Thus, they say of all created things what the Christians say of Christ alone. They then regard beardless youths as manifestations of beauty, endorsing this most grievous form of associating partners with Allah as a means of deeming indecencies lawful—indeed, of deeming everything forbidden lawful—as someone said to the foremost of

---

## Page 7981

their shaykhs, al-Tilimsānī:

“If your assertion that existence is one is true, what difference is there between my mother, my sister, and my daughter, such that this is lawful and that is forbidden?”

He replied:

“They are all the same to us. But these veiled people said, ‘It is forbidden,’ so we said, ‘It is forbidden for you.’”

Among these adherents of divine indwelling and union are those who restrict indwelling and union to particular individuals: certain prophets, such as Christ; certain Companions, as the extremists claim concerning ʿAlī; certain shaykhs, as the followers of al-Hallāj and others do; certain kings; or particular forms, such as those of beardless youths.

One of them says:

“I am merely looking at the attributes of my Creator and beholding them in this form.”

The disbelief in this statement is too obvious to escape anyone who believes in Allah and His Messenger. Were someone to say such words about a noble prophet, he would be a disbeliever. What, then, if he says them about a beardless boy? May Allah disgrace a group whose object of worship is of the same kind as those they penetrate!

He Most High has said:

“Nor would he command you to take the angels and prophets as lords. Would he command you to disbelieve after you had become Muslims?”

If those who take the angels and prophets as lords are disbelievers despite acknowledging that they were created by Allah, then what of those who take certain created things as lords while claiming that Allah is within them or united with them, so that His existence is theirs, and making other such assertions?

---

## Page 7982

As for the second benefit of lowering the gaze, it is illumination of the heart and discernment. Allah Most High said of the people of Lot:

“By your life, they are wandering blindly in their intoxication.”

Attachment to physical forms corrupts the intellect, blinds inner perception, and leaves the heart intoxicated—indeed, maddened. As it has been said:

Two intoxications: passion and wine—
When will one beset by both regain his senses?

It has also been said:

They said, “You have gone mad for your beloved.” I replied,
“Passion is worse than the madness of the mad.
Its captive never regains his senses,
While the madman’s fits come only now and then.”

Allah, glorified is He, mentioned the Verse of Light immediately after the verses concerning lowering the gaze, saying:

“Allah is the Light of the heavens and the earth.”

Shujāʿ ibn Shāh al-Kirmānī’s discernment never failed him. He used to say: “Whoever cultivates his outward self by following the Sunnah and his inward self through constant

---

## Page 7983

awareness of Allah’s watchfulness, lowers his gaze from what is forbidden, and restrains himself from desires”—he also mentioned a sixth quality, which I think was eating lawful food—“will never err in his discernment.” Allah Most High rewards His servant’s deeds with a recompense of the same kind: He releases the light of his inner vision and opens for him the door to knowledge, understanding, unveilings, and other things perceived through the heart’s insight.

The Third Benefit

Strength, steadfastness, and courage of heart.

Allah grants him the power of insight alongside the power of proof. As a report states:

“Satan fears even the shadow of one who resists his own desires.”

Thus, those who follow their desires suffer the humiliation, weakness, and abasement that Allah has decreed for those who disobey Him. For Allah has granted honour to those who obey Him and humiliation to those who disobey Him. Allah Most High says:

“They say, ‘If we return to Medina, the more honourable will surely expel the more abased from it.’ Yet honour belongs to Allah, His Messenger, and the believers.”

He also says:

“Do not lose heart or grieve, for you will have the upper hand, if you are believers.”

Hence the saying of the spiritual masters: “People seek honour at the gates of kings, but they find it only in obedience to Allah.”

Al-Hasan al-Basrī used to say: “Though their horses carry them at a smooth, swift pace, and their docile mules clatter beneath them, the humiliation of disobedience hangs upon their necks. Allah insists on humiliating those who disobey Him.”

Whoever obeys Allah has shown loyalty to Him insofar as he has obeyed Him. Whoever disobeys Him has a share in the conduct of those who oppose Him through their sins. In the supplication of qunūt, it is said:

“Indeed, none whom You take as an ally is humiliated, and none whom You oppose is honoured.”

---

## Page 7984

Moreover, the Sufis renowned among the Muslim community—those held in enduring good repute—did not approve of such things; rather, they forbade them. Their statements condemning keeping company with youths, refuting the advocates of divine indwelling, and explaining the Creator’s distinctness are too extensive to mention here.

Only those who imitated them approved of such things: people who were disobedient, wicked, or unbelieving. They made a show of claiming to be allies of Allah and to have attained true faith and spiritual knowledge, while being among the worst enemies of Allah and the worst practitioners of hypocrisy and falsehood.

Allah Most High brings together the good of this world and the Hereafter for His allies who are mindful of Him, while making His enemies’ bargain a losing one. And Allah, glorified is He, knows best.

---

## Page 7985

Surah al-Furqān (The Criterion)

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—said:

Section

The three gravest major sins are disbelief, then unlawful killing, then unlawful sexual intercourse, as Allah has ordered them in His words:

“Those who do not invoke another god besides Allah, nor kill a soul whose killing Allah has forbidden except with just cause, nor commit unlawful sexual intercourse.”

In the two Sahīh collections, it is related that 'Abd Allah ibn Mas'ūd said:

“I asked, ‘O Messenger of Allah, which sin is greatest?’

He replied, ‘To set up a rival to Allah when He created you.’

I asked, ‘Then which?’

He replied, ‘Then to kill your child for fear that he will share your food.’

I asked, ‘Then which?’

He replied, ‘To commit adultery with your neighbor’s wife.’”

There is a rational basis for this ordering: human beings have three faculties—the faculty of reason, the faculty of anger, and the faculty of desire. The highest is the rational faculty, which distinguishes humans from all other animals and which they share with the angels. As Abū Bakr 'Abd al-'Azīz, a scholar of our school, and others have said:

“Angels were created with intellect but without desire,

---

## Page 7986

and beasts were created with desire but without intellect, while human beings were created with both intellect and desire. Whoever’s intellect prevails over his desire is better than the angels, but whoever’s desire prevails over his intellect is worse than the beasts.”

Next comes the faculty of anger, which wards off harm, followed by the faculty of desire, which secures benefit.

Some natural philosophers say: “The faculty of anger is the animal faculty, because animals possess it whereas plants do not. The faculty of desire is the vegetative faculty, because animals and plants share it, and plants possess it whereas inanimate things do not.”

But the following objection may be raised: if he means that desire itself is shared by plants and animals, that is not so. Plants have neither longing nor voluntary movement, neither desire nor anger. If, however, he means growth and nourishment themselves, these are attendant upon desire and are its effects. There is a parallel to this in anger: the effect of anger, and what follows from it, is repulsion and resistance. This property exists in all solid, strong bodies. Thus, desire and anger themselves are confined to living creatures. Their effects, however—encroachment and repulsion—are shared with strong plants. The capacity to repel and resist exists in hard, strong plants, but not in soft, tender ones. Hence, among plants, the capacity to repel is confined to certain kinds; yet it exists in all solid bodies. Thus, desire and anger stand in a relation of generality and specificity.

---

## Page 7987

The reason for this is that the soul’s faculties of action involve either attraction or repulsion. The attracting faculty, which draws in what is agreeable, is desire and related qualities, such as love, will, and the like. The repelling faculty, which wards off what is incompatible, is anger and related qualities, such as hatred and aversion. Insofar as this power is shared by human beings and beasts, it consists simply of desire and anger; insofar as it is distinctive to human beings, it consists of intellect, faith, and the intervening spiritual faculties.

Disbelief concerns the intellectual faculty of reason and faith; hence, those who lack discernment are not described as disbelievers. Murder arises from the faculty of anger and from transgression in its exercise, while unlawful sexual intercourse arises from the faculty of desire. Thus, disbelief constitutes transgression and corruption in the human intellectual faculty; murder constitutes transgression and corruption in the faculty of anger; and unlawful sexual intercourse constitutes transgression and corruption in the faculty of desire.

Another clear perspective is that Allah created His creatures to worship Him. The individual’s continued existence depends on the body, while the species’ continued existence depends on marriage and procreation. Disbelief therefore corrupts the purpose for which they were created; murder destroys lives already in existence; and unlawful sexual intercourse corrupts the future generations of the species. The former destroys what exists, while the latter corrupts what does not yet exist. This is comparable to someone destroying existing wealth or preventing something already taking shape from coming into existence. Destroying what already exists is the greater corruption; hence this ordering. And from

---

## Page 7988

a third perspective, disbelief corrupts the heart and spirit, which rules the body; murder destroys the body that bears it, putting an end to an existing life. Unlawful sexual intercourse, however, corrupts the manner of existence rather than existence itself. Yet this applies specifically to unlawful sexual intercourse; from this it becomes clear that sodomy involves greater corruption than unlawful sexual intercourse.

Section

The nations that are the finest of humankind—the Arabs, Romans, and Persians—are divided according to these three faculties. These are the nations among whom human virtues have become manifest, and they inhabit the central regions of the earth in both longitude and latitude. Other peoples, such as the black peoples, the Turks, and others like them, follow in their wake.

Among the Arabs, the intellectual faculty of reason and speech predominated, and their name was derived from this characteristic. They were called Arabs, from iʿrāb, meaning clarification and clear expression, which is the distinctive characteristic of the faculty of reason and speech.

---

## Page 7989

And among the Romans, the faculty of appetite predominated, manifesting itself in food, sexual relations, and the like. Their name was derived from this: they were called Rūm, for one says rumtu hādhā, arūmuhu when seeking and desiring something. Among the Persians, the faculty of anger predominated, manifesting itself in repelling, resisting, asserting superiority, and exercising leadership. Their name was derived from this: they were called Furs, just as one says farasahu, yafrisuhu when subduing and overcoming someone. Thus these three qualities predominate among these three nations, among both their settled and nomadic populations. For this reason, the Arabs were the finest of nations, followed by the Persians—since the faculty of resistance ranks higher—and then the Romans.

Section

In accordance with these faculties, there are three virtues:

The virtue of reason, knowledge, and faith, which perfects the faculty of reason and speech; and the virtue of courage, which perfects the faculty of anger. The perfection of courage is forbearance, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The strong man is not the one who overpowers others in wrestling; rather, the strong man is the one who controls himself when angry.”

Forbearance and noble generosity are bound together, just as temperance is the perfection of the faculty of appetite. When a noble person is temperate and a generous person is forbearing, balance is achieved.

And the virtue of generosity and liberality, which perfects the faculty of desire and love. Generosity arises from gentleness, ease, and suppleness of character, just as courage arises from

---

## Page 7990

strength, toughness, and rigidity of character. Thus, the faculty of anger is the faculty that secures victory, while the faculty of appetite secures provision. Both are mentioned in His saying:

“Who has fed them against hunger and made them safe from fear.”

Provision and victory are frequently linked in the Quran, the Sunnah, and ordinary speech. As for the fourth virtue, known as justice, it is a quality encompassing all three: maintaining balance among them. These latter three constitute the practical moral virtues, as illustrated in the report concerning Saʿd, when al-ʿAbsī said of him:

“He does not distribute equally, judge fairly, or go out on military expeditions.”

Section

The three communities—Muslims, Jews, and Christians—also correspond to these three faculties. Muslims possess reason, knowledge, and moderation in their affairs, for their Prophet’s miracle is Allah’s knowledge and His speech; they are the community of the middle way.

As for the Jews, the faculty of appetite was weakened among them, to the point that foods and clothing were forbidden to them that were not forbidden to others. They were commanded to be stern and forceful in the ways prescribed for them. Their sins mostly arise from harshness and severity, rather than appetite.

---

## Page 7991

And among Christians, the faculty of anger was weakened, so they were forbidden to take revenge or defend themselves. Their faculty of appetite, however, was not weakened, so the foods forbidden to their predecessors were not forbidden to them; rather, some things previously forbidden were made lawful for them. Indulgence in food, drink, and desires appeared among them to a degree not found among the Jews. They also possess tenderness, compassion, and mercy not found among the Jews. Most of their sins therefore arise from desires rather than anger, while most of their acts of obedience concern securing victory rather than provision.

Since traits associated with Jesus existed among Sufis and jurists—whether in legitimate or distorted forms—they displayed desires and inclinations toward women, boys, and enchanting voices for which they deserved blame. Likewise, since traits associated with Moses existed among jurists—whether in legitimate or distorted forms—they displayed anger, harshness, arrogance, and similar qualities for which they deserved blame.

Section

The faculty of appetite falls under the general category of love, and the faculty of anger under that of hatred. Anger (ghadab) and hatred (bughd) are related through “greater derivation” (al-ishtiqāq al-akbar). Hence the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The firmest bonds of faith are love for Allah’s sake and hatred for Allah’s sake.”

For these two faculties are the foundation. He also said:

“Whoever loves for Allah’s sake and hates for Allah’s sake

---

## Page 7992

and gives for Allah’s sake and withholds for Allah’s sake has perfected his faith.”

Love and hatred are thus the foundation. Giving springs from love and constitutes generosity, while withholding springs from hatred and constitutes stinginess.

As for anger, it may be said to be a particular form of hatred: the intensity that arises within the soul, accompanied by the boiling of the heart’s blood in pursuit of vengeance. This is anger in its specific sense. For this reason, some speculative theologians contrast appetite with aversion rather than with anger. Whoever contrasts appetite with anger must not mean anger in this specific sense, for its relation to aversion is like that of covetousness to appetite. Anger in its general sense, however, is the faculty that repels through hatred, as opposed to the faculty that attracts through love.

Section

Performing what is commanded springs from the volitional faculty of love and appetite, while refraining from what is forbidden springs from the faculty of dislike, hatred, anger, and aversion. Enjoining what is right springs from love and will, while forbidding what is wrong springs from hatred and dislike. The same applies to encouraging what is right and warning against what is wrong, urging the former and restraining from the latter. Hence, souls are restrained from wrongdoing only through the repelling faculty of anger. Through this, justice and equity are established in judgment and distribution

---

## Page 7993

and other matters, just as benevolence is established through the attractive faculty of appetite. For warding off what is disliked without attaining what is loved amounts to absence: neither what is loved nor what is disliked is present. Attaining both what is loved and what is disliked, however, constitutes a flawed existence, since both are present together. These two alternatives compete for preference: some souls may choose one, while others choose the other. This applies when they are evenly balanced. When a little of what is disliked accompanies much of what is loved, existence is preferable; conversely, when much of what is disliked accompanies little of what is loved, absence is preferable.

Yet because the causes of both what is loved and what is disliked—that is, good and evil—are present, and, assuming both exist, aid is obtained just as provision is obtained amid fear, warding off what is disliked has come to hold great importance both in the revealed law and in human nature. In the revealed law, this finds expression in piety: it is accorded great importance in the Book, the Sunnah, and scholarly consensus. The ultimate good outcome belongs to those who possess it, and theirs is the reward.

In human nature, this is evident in the esteem people have for those who aid them by warding off harm from an enemy or any other source. Those who provide sustenance hold those who provide aid in greater esteem than the latter hold the former. This—and Allah knows best—is because aid without sustenance is beneficial, since the causes that bring sustenance are present and continue to operate. Sustenance without aid, however, is of no benefit, for the causes that bring aid are dependent.

This, however, is open to question. It may be said that the two stand in a reciprocal relationship: those who provide aid love those who provide sustenance more than those who provide sustenance love those who provide aid. For sustenance is loved, whereas aid is held in esteem.

---

## Page 7994

It may also be said:

Rather, aid is more important, as explained above. For warding off what is disliked is itself loved, and this can only be achieved through the repelling faculty, which is stronger than the attracting faculty. Those who provide aid are therefore accorded particular esteem because they repel opposition. Those who provide sustenance, by contrast, face no opposition; rather, they have support. Thus, those who provide aid are both loved and esteemed.

This may be countered by saying:

Losing what is loved is also disliked, and what is loved can only be attained through the attracting faculty. We do not concede that the repelling faculty is stronger. Indeed, attraction may be stronger; in fact, attraction is fundamentally stronger, for it is what is sought in the first instance, while repulsion serves it and is subordinate to it. Just as the repelling faculty wards off the opposing factor, the attracting faculty supplies the positive cause. Giving the impediment precedence over the positive cause is unjustified. Rather, the positive cause is stronger in absolute terms, for it is indispensable for something to exist. The impediment, however, is needed only when an opposing factor is present, and there may be no opposing factor. Thus, the positive cause and love are the foundation and mainstay of what truly exists and what is rightly sought, whereas the impediment and hatred are secondary and subordinate.

For this reason, Allah wrote in the Book kept with Him above the Throne:

“My mercy prevails over My wrath.”

For this reason, good is found in Allah’s names and attributes, whereas evil pertains to actions, as in His words:

“Tell My servants that I am indeed the All-Forgiving, the Most Merciful, and that My punishment is indeed the painful punishment.”

And His words:

“Know that Allah is severe in punishment, and that Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

---

## Page 7995

It remains to ask: Why, then, is piety accorded such importance?

The answer is that it preserves the innate nature and prevents its corruption. The servant needs to safeguard this nature because innate love requires no stimulus. Hence, the foremost concern of the messengers’ call was sincere devotion to Allah alone and the prohibition of associating partners with Him. Innate acknowledgment is already present because its underlying cause exists; what is needed is to purify it and ward off the association of partners with Allah.

This is also why people need governance that prevents them from wronging one another and enables them to benefit one another, just as Allah has enjoined beneficial zakat and forbidden harmful usury. The foundation of religion is the worship of Allah, which rests upon love, turning to Him, and turning away from all besides Him. This is the innate nature upon which He created humankind.

Concerning this love, which is the foundation of religion, a group among the deviants of the Mosaic tradition—jurists and speculative theologians—went astray, to the point of denying it. They claimed that love for Allah means nothing more than the will to worship Him. Moreover, many of them neglect to act upon what they have been commanded to do: they enjoin righteousness upon others and forget themselves. This is widespread among them: the absence of both love and action.

A group among the deviants of the tradition of Jesus—Sufis and devout worshippers—mingled this love with love for what Allah hates. They rejected hatred and aversion, so they neither condemned anything nor instilled aversion to it, or fell short in aversion and condemnation. They included forms, sounds, and love for rivals to Allah within this love. Thus, the wayward among the former were subject to wrath and a curse arising from

---

## Page 7996

hatred, for they harbor hatred without love. The misguided among the latter, meanwhile, were characterized by misguidance and excess, because they direct love toward what is not rightly worshipped. They have longing, will, and love, but these are not directed toward a proper object of pursuit, will, or love. Rather, they have mingled things together, gone to excess, and associated partners with Allah. Thus, they love both truth and falsehood, allowing both what is loved and what is hated to be present, just as the others hate both truth and falsehood, rejecting both what is loved and what is hated. May Allah, glorified be He, guide us along His straight path.

What is praiseworthy in the latter is their love and acknowledgment of truth; what is praiseworthy in the former is their hatred and rejection of falsehood.

---

## Page 7997

Surah al-Naml (The Ants)

Shaykh al-Islām said:

This is an explanation of verses that have proved difficult to understand, so much so that some works of Quranic commentary contain nothing but mistaken interpretations of them. Among these is the saying of the Most High:

“Whoever comes with a good deed will have something better in return”—to the end of the verse.

The well-known interpretation transmitted from the early generations is that the good deed is the declaration, “There is no deity worthy of worship except Allah,” and that the evil deed is associating partners with Allah. Al-Suddī is reported to have said: “This is at the Reckoning: for every good deed, ten evil deeds are cancelled. If a single evil deed remains, his recompense is the Fire, unless Allah forgives him.”

I say: It is established in the authentic hadith collections that a good deed is multiplied tenfold and up to seven hundredfold, that an evil deed is counted as one, that intending a good deed counts as a good deed, and that intending an evil deed is not recorded.

Those who held the first view did so because righteous deeds are encompassed by tawhīd (monotheism). For Allah is worshipped by doing what He has commanded, as He says:

“Yes indeed, whoever submits himself to Allah while doing good”—to the end of the verse.

And the Most High says:

“Have you not seen how Allah sets forth the parable of a good word”—to the end of the verse.

---

## Page 7998

Thus, the good word is tawhīd. It is like a tree, with deeds as its fruits at all times. Likewise, the evil deed is acting for other than Allah, and this is shirk (associating partners with Allah). For a human being is ever acting and intending: he must act, and he must have a purpose for which he acts. If he acts for Allah and for another, that is shirk. Sins are a form of shirk, for they constitute obedience to Satan.

He says:

“I disavow your former association of me with Allah”—to the end of the verse.

And He says:

“Did I not command you, O children of Adam, not to worship Satan”—to the end of the verse.

And in the hadith:

“and from Satan’s evil and his shirk.”

Yet if a person is a monotheist, committing certain sins diminishes his tawhīd, as the Prophet said: “The fornicator does not fornicate”—and so on. Whoever is not a believer is not sincere in his devotion. And in the hadith: “May the slave of the dinar perish”—and so on. And in the hadith of Abū Bakr: “Say: ‘O Allah, I seek refuge in You from knowingly associating anything with You’”—and so on.

But if he does not set up another as Allah’s equal, loving that other as he loves Allah—rather, Allah is more beloved to him, more feared by him, and the object of greater hope than any created being—then he has freed himself of major shirk.

---

## Page 7999

Surah al-Ahzāb

Shaykh al-Islam—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Allah Most High says:

“The Prophet has a greater claim over the believers than they have over themselves, and his wives are their mothers. Blood relatives have a greater claim upon one another in Allah’s Book than do other believers and emigrants, though you may still show kindness to your allies. That is inscribed in the Book.”

This conveys a meaning like that of the authentic hadith:

“I have a greater claim over every believer than he has over himself. Whoever leaves wealth, it belongs to his heirs; whoever leaves a burden or dependants, their care falls to me.”

Allah gave him a greater claim over them than they have over themselves, then gave relatives a greater claim upon one another. For his having a greater claim over them than they have over themselves entails his having a greater claim over them than their blood relatives have. Yet this does not entail his owning their wealth while they are alive, nor, likewise, after their death. Rather, it entails his shouldering their burdens and providing for their dependants from his own funds: the fifth, a fifth of that fifth, or all the fay’ revenues, according to the well-known difference of opinion.

This also shows that the priority entitling someone to inherit, mentioned in the Prophet’s words—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—

“to the nearest male relative,”

is conditional upon faith.

---

## Page 8000

This qualified verse governs the unqualified verse in Surah al-Anfāl for three reasons.

First: This verse, in Surah al-Ahzāb, came after the Battle of the Trench, whereas the verse in Surah al-Anfāl came immediately after Badr.

Second: These are unqualified and qualified formulations concerning the same ruling and the same cause. The ruling here entails permissibility, entitlement, prohibition for others, and an obligation to hand over what is due.

Third: The verse in Surah al-Anfāl mentions this greater claim after also severing ties of allegiance between believers and disbelievers; it therefore provides a second proof. These two verses explain the unqualified wording in the verse on inheritance. This is thus an instance of explaining the Quran by the Quran, although the Prophet’s statement,

“A disbeliever does not inherit from a Muslim,”

also accords with it. As for a Muslim inheriting from a disbeliever, there is an anomalous dissenting opinion on this matter. Here too, we draw evidence from the two verses together with the hadith. The two verses also encompass all other forms of legal authority concerning marriage, property, blood-money obligations, and matters relating to death. His words,

“though you may still show kindness to your allies,”

provide evidence for bequests, as do the verses in Surah al-Nisā’.

The verse in which He says,

“So when Zayd had ended his marriage to her, We gave her to you in marriage, so that there would be no restriction on the believers concerning the wives of their adopted sons,”

demonstrates that what was made lawful for him was lawful for his community. For He states that this marriage was intended to spare the community any restriction concerning such marriages. If his performing an act made lawful for him did not imply its permissibility for his community, this explanation would not be sound. This is evident.

---

## Page 8001

Moreover, if this holds for his marriage to the wife of an adopted son—a marriage previously believed to be forbidden—it applies all the more where no such doubt exists. Furthermore, this holds in marriage, a sphere in which he was granted permissions not shared by his community, such as marrying without a numerical limit and marrying a woman who offered herself to him without a dowry. Since He has explained that permitting him to contract this marriage establishes its permissibility for his community, this applies all the more to matters in which no special privilege comparable to those in marriage is evident. This indicates that everything else made lawful for him, including transactions, foods, clothing, and the like, is lawful for his community, except where evidence establishes an exception specific to him.

This principle is also supported, in two respects, by His words in the passage concerning what He made lawful for him:

“and a believing woman who offers herself to the Prophet, if the Prophet wishes to marry her—a privilege for you alone, not for the other believers. We know what We have prescribed for them regarding their wives and those whom their right hands possess, so that there may be no hardship for you.”

First: When He permitted him to marry a woman who offered herself to him, He said:

“a privilege for you alone, not for the other believers,”

to clarify that this was exclusive to him. It follows that wherever He makes no mention of exclusivity, the permission extends to his community. Otherwise, there would be no point in singling out this instance for an explicit statement of exclusivity.

Second: As for the wives, female slaves, and female relatives He made lawful for him,

---

## Page 8002

He left the permission unrestricted, whereas, in the case of the woman who offered herself to him, He qualified it as exclusive to him. Thus, His omission of this qualification in the other cases indicates that the permission is shared.

If it is said:

“Silence indicates neither exclusivity nor a shared ruling, whereas the qualification of exclusivity rules out a shared ruling. Its purpose, then, is to prevent anyone from supposing, on the basis of separate evidence, that the ruling is shared. Making something lawful for him certainly does not indicate that it is exclusive to him. But does it indicate that the permission is shared, or does it indicate neither? This is the point at issue. Once it is qualified as exclusive to him, it indicates exclusivity.”

The reply is:

If it did not indicate that the permission is shared, the ruling would not be established for the community, since there would be no evidence for it—just as no ruling of prohibition is established concerning matters on which He remained silent, because there is no evidence for it.

At this point, one might argue that they regarded it as lawful on the basis of the default rule. But that is not so, because sexual relations are forbidden unless made lawful by the revealed law. It would therefore have been forbidden to them, and there would have been no need to make it exclusive to him, had the unqualified address not entailed a shared and general ruling, belonging to the category of expressions that are particular in wording but general in their ruling.

The underlying principle is that an expression’s linguistic scope may become narrower or broader through established usage, whether in the revealed law or elsewhere. Thus, although the address to him is linguistically particular in its wording, it is general by established usage. It is among those expressions whose scope has shifted, through the established usage of the revealed law, from particular to general. Similar shifts occur in addresses to kings and the like, and such instances are numerous, just as

---

## Page 8003

a general expression may become particular through established usage. Moreover, this rests on the principle of contrary implication: singling something out for mention alongside a general expression that entails general applicability indicates that the ruling is particular to it. Thus, when He singled out the passage concerning the woman who offered herself by expressly mentioning exclusivity, this indicated that the remaining cases were not exclusive to him. Their lack of exclusivity is indicated precisely by the absence of any mention of exclusivity while permission is affirmed for the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. It follows that affirming permission for him without restricting it to him entails general applicability.

Accordingly, an address whose linguistic form is particular falls into three categories.

First, it may indicate general applicability, as with expressions that are general by established usage, such as an address to the Messenger or to an individual member of the community. The same applies to the implicit indication conveyed by the wording, as in the saying, “I will not drink your water, even when thirsty,” and the expressions “a grain’s weight,” “a large sum,” and “a dinar.”

Second, it may indicate that the ruling applies exclusively to what is mentioned and not to anything else. This is the case with contrary implication when grounds for general applicability are present, yet one category is singled out for mention.

Third, its wording may indicate neither of these, but general applicability may then be established through its underlying meaning, whether by analogy with a case having an even stronger claim to the ruling or by other forms of analogy,

---

## Page 8004

and a distinction must be drawn between the implicit indication of the wording and analogy with a case having an even stronger claim to the ruling. In the former, the ruling is derived from wording that encompasses both cases in customary speech; in the latter, it is derived from the ruling itself. Thus, even if the ruling were established by an action, tacit approval, or an address whose speaker unquestionably intended only the particular case mentioned, establishing that ruling for one category would entail establishing it for whatever has a stronger claim to it. Here, general applicability rests entirely on meaning, whereas in the former case it rests on both wording and meaning. Reflect on this, for it resolves the dispute among our fellow scholars and others over whether implicit indication is derived from wording or constitutes clear analogy, and shows you that it is of two kinds. The difference is that, when general applicability is derived from the wording, the speaker intends it and uses a single instance to illustrate it, as when a grammarian says, “Zayd struck Amr.” This is unlike what is derived from meaning.

The verse mentioned earlier, namely His words:

“We gave her to you in marriage so that there would be no…”

indicates that his actions, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, entail permissibility for his community. Yet an action in itself unquestionably has no general scope through wording or linguistic convention. Its general applicability rests instead on the established principle that the community shares his rulings and follows his example.

This is also indicated by His words in the same surah:

“Indeed, in the Messenger of Allah you have an excellent example.”

The verse calls for following his example in what befell him. Once it is established that he is to be followed with respect to the rulings governing him in his afflictions, the same applies to his actions, since an afflicted person has duties to fulfil and prohibitions to observe. Thus, this indicates

---

## Page 8005

that, as a rule, his community shares his rulings concerning obligations and prohibitions, just as the other verse indicates that they share his rulings concerning permissibility.

His words:

“Tell your wives, your daughters, and the women of the believers to draw their outer garments around themselves,”

are evidence that the command to observe hijab was directed only to free women, not slave women. For He specifically mentioned his wives and daughters, without saying, “those whom your right hand possesses, your slave women, and the slave women of your wives and daughters.” He then said:

“and the women of the believers.”

Slave women are not included among “the women of the believers,” just as those whom their right hands possess were not included in His words “their women,” but were mentioned separately alongside them in the verses of Sūrat al-Nūr and Sūrat al-Ahzāb. It may, however, be said that this argument rests only on the view that “those whom the right hand possesses” refers exclusively to females. On the view that it refers to both sexes, or to males, the argument requires further consideration.

Moreover, His words:

“For those who swear to abstain from their wives,”

and His words:

“Those among you who declare their wives unlawful to them as their mothers,”

refer only to women married with a dower, not to women owned as slaves. The same applies here. Thus, the verse concerning outer garments relates to cloaks worn when going outside the home, whereas the verse concerning the screen relates to conversation within the home. Taken together with the report in the Sahīh—

“When he chose Safiyyah bint Huyayy, they said, ‘If he screens her, she is one of the Mothers of the Believers; otherwise, she is among those whom his right hand possesses’”—

this indicates that hijab was specific to free women. The hadith also provides evidence that the status of Mothers of the Believers belongs to his wives, not his concubines.

---

## Page 8006

and the Quran indicates nothing beyond that, for He says:

“His wives are their mothers.”

And He says:

“Nor may you ever marry his wives after him.”

This also provides a third proof from the verse: the pronoun in His words, “When you ask them,” refers to his wives; slave women are not mentioned in this address. Whether his concubines would be lawful to others after his death, however, requires further consideration.

Section

Anyone who holds that “release” and “separation” are explicit terms of divorce because the Quran uses them in that sense, and who defines explicit terms as those the Quran has used for divorce—as do al-Shāfiʿī, al-Qādī, and others among the jurists of our school—holds a weak position for two reasons.

The first is that there is no evidence for this principle; indeed, it is unsound. In reality, people speak their own languages, which may agree with or differ from the language of the Arabs. These include other varieties of Arabic, whether established or altered in wording or meaning; later-developed Arabic; Arabized speech learned from non-Arabs; and non-Arabic languages. Divorce and similar acts take effect in all these kinds of language, since what matters is the meaning. Such usage may not have been forbidden to them; or it may have been forbidden, but they did not observe the prohibition. That does not, however, give effect to an act they have not performed.

Moreover, the Quran’s use of a word in a particular sense

---

## Page 8007

does not imply that the word cannot bear any other meaning.

The second reason—and the decisive one—is that these expressions occur in the Quran mostly in senses other than divorce. For example, Allah says:

“When you marry believing women and then divorce them before you have touched them, you have no waiting period to count for them. So provide for them and release them.”

Here, after an irrevocable divorce involving no waiting period, there is a command to release them and provide for them. This does not mean pronouncing a second divorce, for, by agreement, such a divorce neither takes effect nor is commanded. Rather, it means actually letting the woman go: ceasing to keep her confined. Marriage entails union through marital right and legal status, as well as physical, practical union through keeping the wife with the husband. Both are consequences of marriage and necessarily accompany one another. When the marital right ceases, the husband is commanded to relinquish his control. This is comparable to the distinction between ownership and possession in matters of property: in both cases, possession follows from the contract. Thus, if the contract is terminated, either by relinquishing control, which constitutes possession.

His words:

“Then come: I will provide for you and release you”

cannot be used as evidence that “release” means divorce. He may mean actually letting them go, since He couples this with providing for them. Actually letting them go, however, necessarily entails divorce. Alternatively, He may mean both together. He does not mean divorce alone, for that would not benefit them but would instead harm them. The same applies to His words:

“When they approach the end of their waiting period, either retain them honourably or part with them honourably.”

The same applies to His words:

“Or part with them honourably.”

For when a woman whose divorce is revocable approaches the end of her waiting period, the husband is not commanded to pronounce a second divorce if he does not take her back. Rather, he is instructed

---

## Page 8008

to let her go—that is, to release her and physically part from her, neither confining her nor exercising control over her, just as one relinquishes possession of property.

His words:

“Call them by their fathers’ names; that is more just in Allah’s sight. If you do not know their fathers, then they are your brothers in faith and your associates. There is no blame upon you for your unintentional mistakes, but only for what your hearts deliberately intend”

explicitly establish that there is no blame for mistakenly attributing a man to someone other than his father or his patron.

This may also be cited as evidence that a person incurs no blame for any mistaken word or deed. This can be established either through the generality of the wording—on the grounds that a general expression is not restricted to the particular circumstance mentioned alongside it—or through the general applicability of its meaning, based on the common factor that an unintentional error has no effect upon the heart. It is thus an action of a bodily member without any deliberate intention of the heart. The heart is the foundation, as the Prophet said:

“When it is sound, the rest of the body is sound; and when it is corrupt, the rest of the body is corrupt.”

If the foundation has done nothing, the actions of its branches without its participation cause no harm, because it remains sound and free of corruption. The whole body is therefore sound, not corrupt. Consequently, there is no sin in this, since sin can arise only from corruption within the body. This verse thus reinforces His words:

“Our Lord, do not hold us accountable if we forget or make a mistake.”

He said: “I have done so.”

This is further supported by His words concerning oaths:

“Allah does not hold you accountable for your unintentional oaths, but He holds you accountable for what your hearts have earned.”

“But He holds you accountable for the oaths you deliberately bind yourselves by.”

For

---

## Page 8009

if, even in an oath by Allah—with all the gravity it carries—one is held accountable only for what the heart has earned, then the same applies, with even greater reason, to other utterances. If someone swears to something he believes to be true, but it turns out otherwise, this is an error that falls under inadvertence, because his heart has not earned any transgression. Similarly, had he stated this without an oath, he would not have incurred the sin of lying, just as someone who mistakenly attributes a man to someone other than his father or patron incurs no sin. If he does not incur the sin of lying without an oath, then with an oath he is not subject to the ruling applicable to someone who violates his oath.

For when an oath concerning the past is reinforced by swearing, the same principle applies to what one swears concerning the future. If someone performs the act covered by his oath while forgetting his oath, or mistakenly, unaware that this is the act covered by it, his heart has earned neither a violation nor a breach of oath. Likewise, had he made a promise concerning this without an oath, he would not have broken his promise; and had he been commanded to do it but omitted it under those same circumstances, he would not have been disobedient.

This evidence encompasses divorce and other matters, either through the general applicability of its meaning or through the generality of both its meaning and its wording. What difference is there between inadvertence accompanying the making of an oath and its accompanying the breach of that oath? His words:

“But He holds you accountable for the oaths you deliberately bind yourselves by”

mean that this is a cause of accountability, not that it necessarily entails accountability, as is unanimously agreed. Error may therefore occur in both its cause and its condition.

Whoever says, “Inadvertence is not excused in divorce,” has no evidence in his favour; rather, the evidence is against him. If the words of divorce slipped from his tongue without any deliberate intention in his heart, divorce would not take effect, by agreement. But if he deliberately utters the words in jest, then his heart has deliberately intended to utter them, just as if he deliberately uttered an oath using them.
